--- Page 1 ---
<meta>This is the title page of Robert Fludd’s monumental work on the relationship between the universe and humanity. It establishes the structural division of the entire project.</meta>

# Of Both Worlds
## Namely, the METAPHYSICAL, PHYSICAL, AND TECHNICAL HISTORY of the GREATER as well as the LESSER WORLD

->Divided into two Volumes according to the difference of the WORLD.<-

->BY THE AUTHOR ROBERT FLUDD, also known as de Fluctibus, Esquire and Doctor of Medicine of Oxford.<-

### Volume One
->Concerning the History of the Macrocosm <note>The "Macrocosm" refers to the great world or the entire universe.</note>, divided into two treatises.<-

Of which

*The first concerns*
> The Metaphysical origin of the Macrocosm and its Creatures.
> The Physical progress of the Macrocosm in its generation and corruption.

*The second concerns the Art that Mimics Nature <note>original: "Arte Naturæ Simia" — literally "the ape of nature," a common Renaissance concept where human art and technology imitate the natural creative processes of God.</note>, produced within the Macrocosm and therein nourished and multiplied; whose principal daughters we have here reviewed by a living anatomy, namely:*
> Arithmetic.
> Music.
> Geometry.
> Perspective.
> The Art of Painting.
> Military Art.
> The Science of Motion.
> The Science of Time.
> Cosmography.
> Astrology.
> Geomancy <note>A method of divination that interprets markings on the ground or the patterns formed by tossed handfuls of soil, rocks, or sand.</note>.

->AT OPPENHEIM.<-
->At the expense of JOHANN THEODOR DE BRY.<-
->From the press of HIERONYMUS GALLER.<-
->IN THE YEAR 1617<-

<summary>This is the title page for Robert Fludd's encyclopedic work exploring the "Macrocosm" (the universe) and the "Microcosm" (humanity), outlining the metaphysical, physical, and mathematical sciences. It serves as a manifesto for the interconnectedness of divine creation and human ingenuity.</summary>
<keywords>Robert Fludd, Macrocosm, Microcosm, Metaphysics, Liberal Arts, Johann Theodor de Bry, Alchemy, Astrology, 17th-century science</keywords>

# TO GOD
## THE BEST AND GREATEST,
### MY INCOMPREHENSIBLE CREATOR,

->BE GLORY, PRAISE, HONOR, BLESSING<-
->AND TRIUMPHANT VICTORY,<-
->FOREVER AND EVER,<-
->AMEN.<-

---

<image-desc>Large ornamental drop cap letter 'T' set within a square frame featuring a landscape with trees and a figure.</image-desc>

It is yours, O <term>Creative Nature</term> <note>original: "Natura naturans," a philosophical term referring to God as the active, creative force behind the universe rather than the created world itself</note>, infinite and glorious, to regenerate my soul, which is wasting away in ignorance, by the power of your splendor. It is yours, by the admirable clarity of the Holy Spirit of discipline, to awaken, bring to life, and elevate to the true peak or summit of knowledge those sparks of reason that survived after the <term>Fall of Adam</term> <note>original: "Adami lapsum," the biblical loss of perfection in Eden</note> and have been most miserably submerged for a long time in the abyss of bodily darkness. May your will be done in the celestial and immortal part of me, a man, just as it is in the earthly and mortal part, so that whatever is produced by and in them may all, through due consideration, be a sharer in your goodness. Thus shall this heartfelt offering, consecration, and, as it were, sacrifice of mine be pleasing to your most holy divine power; thus the fruit of your goodness in me—receiving the rays of its own growth flowed in from your lucid fountain of all and only goodness—shall, with your divine Spirit leading the way, fly upward in a true resurrection with its whole transfigured self, and shall rejoice to visit its super-celestial fatherland, in which you, Highest Prince and greatest Emperor, live and reign perpetually. Convert the hearts of malevolent readers into better ones, I beseech you; let them distinguish with open eyes your true light from the darkness of the Devil. Be, O good God, favorable and merciful to me, you who live forever.

Your Creature,
->*the most unworthy of all.*<-

->**I, A MAN.**<-

->\* \*<-
->\*<-

<summary>This preface serves as a spiritual dedication, where the author petitions God as the active force of nature to enlighten his mind and sanctify his work. It establishes a humble, mystical tone, framing the subsequent text as a "heartfelt offering" intended to lead the soul back to its heavenly origin.</summary>
<keywords>theology, Natura naturans, Fall of Adam, enlightenment, divine grace, resurrection, spiritual journey, humility</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the formal dedication of Robert Fludd's work to King James I of England. It follows the title page and establishes the philosophical and spiritual tone of the volume.</meta>

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut headpiece featuring intricate leafy scrollwork, a central decorative shield or urn, and symmetrical floral patterns.</image-desc>

# TO THE MOST SERENE AND MIGHTY PRINCE
# JAMES,
## THE IMMEDIATE MINISTER AND GOVERNOR IN THE KINGDOMS OF GREAT BRITAIN, FRANCE, AND IRELAND, UNDER THE THRICE-GREATEST EMPEROR OF THE HEAVENS AND THE EARTH AND HIS OWN INCOMPREHENSIBLE CREATOR; AND THE DEFENDER OF THE FAITH:

->Health of soul and body, joy in JESUS CHRIST, the abolition of darkness, and the enjoyment of super-celestial light through the illumination of the Holy SPIRIT.<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'C' featuring a floral and leafy background within a square frame.</image-desc>

TO WHOMSOEVER it is denied to understand the secrets and sacred mysteries of *Nature, stripped bare and uncovered, as offered to us by God, the best and greatest; O most mighty King, fortified by knowledge, for such a man it will be impossible to know himself and to aspire to the true enjoyment of the Holy Spirit of understanding. Therefore, let the storehouse of nature be diligently uncovered, with contemplation leading the way, so that we may finally arrive at the sanctuary of GOD and His wisdom. I speak now of great things—let it be said with Your Majesty’s kind permission—things which cannot be acquired or obtained by the royal power of the whole world, nor by violence, majesty, or any human art, but are drawn only from the spring of the good and sincere will of* GOD, *poured down from the holy heavens, and granted to the worthy according to the divine pleasure. May you be, O* MOST SERENE KING, *illuminated by a ray of divine light, purified from human mists and filth, adorned in mind with heavenly gifts, a despiser of earthly things, a contemplator of heavenly and divine things, free from Devilish arrogance, endowed with Christian humility, and full of Holy justice. May you possess, O* MOST MERCIFUL KING JAMES, *the true knowledge of Nature, the only ladder of the Patriarch Jacob <note>A reference to the biblical Jacob's Ladder (Genesis 28), which Fludd uses as a metaphor for the chain of being connecting Earth to Heaven.</note> directing the journey to heaven: for without this medium, there is no passage from earth to heaven. Blessed be* GOD, *and may He bless you and your Empire, so that we, His creatures and your subjects under Him, may live happily with justice—not feigned, but sincere.*

->To Your Majesty<-
->most devoted and most humble subject<-

R. FLUDD. <note>Robert Fludd (1574–1637), a prominent English Paracelsian physician and mystical philosopher.</note>

---

### T H E  M I R R O R
# O B J E C T S  O F  T H I S
# M A C R O C O S M.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut header featuring a symmetrical arrangement of floral scrolls and foliage centering on a classical urn.</image-desc>

In this Emblematic mirror, therefore, it is demonstrated how:

* **GOD**
    * In the beginning created all things metaphysically <note>original: "metaphysicè"; referring here to the spiritual or supernatural realm beyond physical matter</note>.
    * From the beginning, ordained those things which He willed.
* **SPIRITS <note>original: "DÆMONES"; in this context, the author likely refers to 'daemons' in the classical sense—intermediary spirits or angels—rather than purely evil beings</note> metaphysically**
    * Carry out the divine ordinances.
    * Announce the commands of God.
* **NATURE Physically**
    * Fulfills the divine will.
    * Obeys the ordinance of the creator by generating physical things and, in turn, dissolving <note>original: "corrumpēdo"; the natural cycle of growth and decay</note> them.
* **ART <note>referring to "Ars" or human skill, including alchemy and technology</note> by divine permission, the physical operations**
    * Imitates.
    * Assists.
    * Perfects.

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page begins the primary philosophical explanation of the work, establishing the cosmological framework of the "Macrocosm" (the great world/universe) which will be mirrored by the "Microcosm" (the little world/humanity) in later sections.</meta>

## A MOST LUCID EXPLANATION
## OF THE EMBLEM AT THE BEGINNING
## OF THIS VOLUME.

BEFORE the creation of things, the incomprehensible and almighty Creator of the Macrocosm <note>The "Great World" or the entire universe.</note> divided the universal mass into three parts differing in purity by His immense power. From the purest part, He ordained that the Cherubim, Seraphim, Archangels, and all other orders of Angels be created; from the less pure, the heavens and the ethereal regions; and from the most impure, the elements and all sublunary <note>Things below the moon; the earthly realm subject to change and decay.</note> things, by saying: "Let them be made," and they were made.

*The first division of matter, however, He disposed beyond the comprehension of natural things because of the simple purity of its substance; for it is metaphysical and supernatural, which modern writers of Philosophy confirm is circumscribed by the Empyrean Heaven—that is, the burning and fiery realm. But the remaining parts of the less pure mass, namely the ethereal and the elementary, are rightly called "natural" because they are subject to Nature (the governess of the Microcosm under GOD). Furthermore, two efficient causes of these parts contained within the fabric of the Macrocosm are enumerated: namely, Nature, and her Ape, which we call Art. The Father and Lord of Nature is GOD, the Best and Greatest; she is closest to Him, and from Him we recognize that all her virtues and effects are derived. She is, as it were, the knot and bond of the elements, and possesses the power of rightly tempering the mixture of elements in their due proportions, mixing them aptly in every compound, and impressing upon each species its fitting form. Nature is also a certain infinite force procreating like from like, since she is the fertile mother of all qualities and things, which she increases and nourishes. And to describe her in a single word according to the opinions of Zoroaster and Heraclitus, she is an invisible fire, which they call the Soul of the World <note>original: "anima mundi"</note>. We, however, have depicted her visibly in almost the same manner as some of the more recent Philosophers; we have done this with the intention that her power might be better understood by the reader's mind. Thus, we have fashioned a naked virgin of tender and blooming age, with star-bearing and beautifully gilded hair, praiseworthy for the clearest keenness of her eyes, and lovely for the blushing color of her cheeks; and the virgin was so remarkable in the whiteness of her whole body, and so sweetly adorned with all the gifts of the greatest Creator, that the Poets could prefer neither Pallas, nor Venus, nor Juno, nor any other goddess, however charming, to her beauty. By her singular prudence, the first*

<meta>The catchword "mobile" indicates the next page likely discusses the "Primum Mobile" or the first moved sphere of the heavens.</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the divine creation of the universe, dividing it into the supernatural Empyrean, the celestial ether, and the earthly elements. It personifies Nature as a beautiful virgin and the "Soul of the World," acting as the intermediary between God and the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>Macrocosm, Creation, Nature, Art, Soul of the World, Empyrean, Elements, Alchemy, Cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the relationship between Nature and Art, likely following a visual diagram of the 'Macrocosm' or Great World. The catchword 'TRA-' from the previous page leads into 'tractatur' (handled).</meta>

The mobile <note>referring to the *Primum Mobile* or First Mover, the outermost sphere of the heavens</note> is governed by her, and the eighth sphere, adorned with stars, is handled and turned by her hands. By the influence of that same sphere, the generations of elementary things are daily prepared and arranged by her fingers. The bodies of the Planets also serve as instruments for the works of Nature; they are, as it were, little hammers by which metals are produced in the terrestrial mines. The region of the heart and breast of this virgin <note>Nature is personified here as a celestial virgin</note> is the true seat and matrix of the heavenly Sun, and her womb is filled with the lunar body. From her breasts, she perpetually instills and suckles all the creatures of the elements with innate heat and radical moisture, from which their life and vegetation arise. 

By a certain golden splendor of her heart, the stars—both fixed and wandering <note>planets</note>—are illuminated, and they borrow, as it were, their souls, lives, and beauties from it. Their imperceptible influences are infused into her matrix by the Mercurial Spirit (which the Philosophers call the Spirit of the Moon) and are carried downward to the earth, penetrating even to its center; by their impressions, diverse generations, differing in both place and species, are continually made. By the actions of this Nature, the Planets come together, and by this joining, they produce various things differing in species in the animal, vegetable, and mineral kingdoms. 

Starting from the lower parts, beginning at the loins, the four Elements are encompassed; for the right foot occupies the earth, while the left occupies the water, signifying the conjunction of her sulfurous and mercurial nature. By these, it is argued that nothing can be generated or grow without the union of both. This Nature, I say—not a GODDESS, but the closest minister of GOD—has beneath her a handmaid or follower who, by imitating her Mistress and impressing the likenesses of things produced by her into herself through consensus, follows and imitates her footsteps and outlines in a wonderful manner. 

And hence it is that we have depicted her in the form and habit of an Ape under the feet of Nature; which, by a more worthy name, we call **Art**. This beast (I mean Art), born from human ingenuity, learns many secret, beautiful, and notable things from the diligent observation of her Mistress, with which she is accustomed to enrich men in every kind of learning; so also, conversely, she brings no small utility and profit to that same teacher and Mistress. For unless Art, produced by human ingenuity, sometimes gives aid in certain matters, Nature herself would soon fail in the perfection of her operation. 

For Art sometimes corrects Nature, supplies what is missing, and indeed, in the discourse of mineral things, seems to surpass her—if only we give some faith to those Chemical Philosophers <note>Alchemists</note>. This is especially true since it is certain and proven by experience that in the animal circle, eggs hatch more quickly, silkworms are multiplied and transformed by their growth into diverse forms in the manner of Proteus, and bees are procreated from the head of an ox; in the vegetable circle, crops grow and multiply by the artifice of the plow (by which fields are tilled and harrowed), trees by grafting, and herbs and other vegetables by planting; and in the mineral circle, metallic bodies and lesser minerals are prepared for medicine as well as for other uses, cleansed of their dross, and the **Physic Stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note>, whose praises the Philosophers have preached so much, is forged. It is therefore our purpose in this volume to discuss these two effective causes of the Macrocosm both clearly and copiously.

<summary>This text describes Nature as the divine intermediary governing the cosmos and Art (represented as an 'Ape of Nature') as the human capacity to observe, imitate, and perfect natural processes through alchemy and agriculture.</summary>
<keywords>Nature, Art, Macrocosm, Alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, Mercurial Spirit, Elements, Robert Fludd, Astrology, Generation</keywords>

# TREATISE
### On the structure of the Macrocosm <note>The 'Great World' or the universe as a whole</note>, and its history in seven books

<image-desc>A large, complex circular engraving depicting the structure of the macrocosm (the universe). The right side of the image is concealed by a blank vertical paper flap. The visible left portion shows a series of concentric circles:
1. At the center is a small circular scene of the Earthly landscape.
2. Surrounding the center are several elemental spheres (likely water and air).
3. Seven planetary spheres follow, marked with their astrological symbols: Moon (☽), Mercury (☿), Venus (♀), Sun (☉), Mars (♂), Jupiter (♃), and Saturn (♄).
4. Beyond the planets is a sphere for the fixed stars (the Firmament).
5. A broad ring follows, filled with swirling flames and numerous winged angelic heads (representing the celestial or seraphic realm).
6. The outermost boundary consists of heavy, dark, billowing clouds.
At the top of the diagram, rays of divine light emanate from an unseen source above the clouds, piercing down through the various spheres toward the center.</image-desc>

The First Mover <note>original: *Primum Mobile*; the outermost sphere of the heavens that sets all others in motion</note> is governed by her, and the eighth sphere, adorned with stars, is handled and turned by her hands. By the influence of that same sphere, the generations of elementary things <note>physical matter made of earth, water, air, and fire</note> are daily prepared and arranged by her fingers. The bodies of the Planets also serve as instruments for the works of Nature; they are, as it were, little hammers by which metals are produced in the terrestrial mines <note>Fludd is referencing the alchemical belief that each planet "grows" a specific metal in the earth, such as the Sun growing gold and the Moon growing silver</note>. The region of the heart and breast of this virgin <note>Nature is personified here as a celestial virgin or the *Anima Mundi* (Soul of the World)</note> is the true seat and matrix of the heavenly Sun, and her womb is filled with the lunar body. From her breasts, she perpetually instills and suckles all the creatures of the elements with innate heat and radical moisture <note>the essential biological fluids believed to sustain life in early medicine</note>, from which their life and vegetation arise. 

By a certain golden splendor of her heart, the stars—both fixed and wandering <note>planets</note>—are illuminated, and they borrow, as it were, their souls, lives, and beauties from it. Their imperceptible influences are infused into her matrix <note>womb</note> by the Mercurial Spirit (which the Philosophers call the Spirit of the Moon) and are carried downward to the earth, penetrating even to its center; by their impressions, diverse generations, differing in both place and species, are continually made. By the actions of this Nature, the Planets come together, and by this joining, they produce various things differing in species in the animal, vegetable, and mineral kingdoms. 

Starting from the lower parts, beginning at the loins, the four Elements are encompassed; for the right foot occupies the earth, while the left occupies the water, signifying the conjunction of her sulfurous...

<summary>This page describes the personification of Nature as a divine woman whose body encompasses the entire universe, explaining how she uses the planets as tools to create minerals and sustain life. It details the mystical mechanics of how celestial influence flows from the "Sun" of her heart down to the Earthly elements.</summary>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface or "To the Reader" section, introducing the spiritual and philosophical justifications for the work that follows. It establishes continuity by framing the text as a guide for a dedicated seeker of wisdom.</meta>

<image-desc>A wide decorative woodcut headpiece featuring symmetrical acanthus leaf scrolls, floral patterns, and a central stylized human face or mask.</image-desc>

# TO THE BENEVOLENT READER
## AND THE SINCERE SON OF PHILOSOPHY,
### FROM THE HOLY SPIRIT OF UNDERSTANDING,

->illumination.<-

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial letter 'T' decorated with dense foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>

To you, my friend, I most gladly offer these writings of mine to be read; certainly not to <term>Momus<note>In Greek mythology, Momus was the personification of mockery and harsh criticism. In Renaissance texts, he represents the "unfair critic."</note></term> or any other person overwhelmed by the darkness of ignorance or envy. Indeed, it is the habit of such people to defile with an impure mouth everything adorned in the garments of truth, and thus most impiously profane even the sacred rites of the uncreated Creator. Furthermore, I do not believe this subject of ours should be submitted to the judgment of those who claim it is too deep for human grasp or purely divine. For that reason, they decree that these sacred secrets are in no way to be sought by us, constantly repeating that old <term>axiom<note>An established rule or principle.</note></term>: *What is above us, is nothing to us* <note>original: "Quæ supra nos, nihil ad nos"</note>. Truly, by attempting to withdraw us entirely from the contemplation of divine matters in this way, they seem to suggest clearly enough that humans—made in the image of the immense Creator himself—differ little or not at all from brute beasts devoid of all reason.

But is it not found in the Holy Scriptures that the Wise Man shall rule the stars, and that the very offspring of the uncreated Creator promised us that the way would be opened to those who knock? This is to say nothing of that immortal spirit breathed into man at the first creation by the divine mind, which, through this breath of life, made him most near to that same mind. It allowed him to look more deeply into the works of the mind, and to contemplate and search them out with his soul. This is surely what <term>Trismegistus<note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary figure associated with the synthesis of the Greek god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth, credited with writing the foundational texts of alchemy and Hermeticism.</note></term>—the most divine of all philosophers and closest to Moses—confirms in these words in his first discourse of <term>Pimander<note>Also known as the *Poimandres*, the first book of the *Corpus Hermeticum*, which describes the creation of the world and the soul's ascent.</note></term>: *When man,* he says, *held power over all things in himself, he took notice of the works of the seven governors; these governors, rejoicing in the meditation of the human mind, each made man a sharer of their own proper order.*

<sig>B 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: -ed (continuing the word "shared")</meta>

<summary>This preface addresses the "Son of Philosophy," defending the pursuit of hidden or "sacred" knowledge against those who believe it is forbidden or beyond human capacity. The author argues that humans are divinely intended to contemplate the works of the Creator, citing both the Bible and the Hermetic tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Hermeticism, Hermes Trismegistus, Poimandres, divine contemplation, alchemy, sacred secrets, human reason, creation, Momus</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the preface "To the Reader" from the previous page. The text continues Fludd's argument that humans are divinely empowered to understand the cosmos, transitioning from the study of the stars to the contemplation of the Divine itself.</meta>

# EPISTLE TO THE READER.

...[they] who, after he learned their essence and beheld their proper nature, already desired to penetrate and break through the circuit of the spheres, and to comprehend the power of the governor presiding over the fire. By these things, indeed, he clearly teaches that it has been granted by God to men—on account of the nobility of their form—not only to know the natures, movements, and actions of the stars upon these lower realms, but even to proceed further to the fiery and <term>Empyrean<note>In ancient and medieval cosmology, the Empyrean was the highest heaven, believed to be the dwelling place of God and composed of pure light or fire.</note></term> heaven, and there to contemplate and admire the power of the presiding governor, the first of all things. 

From this, he also called God the *mind of human reason*, whom he also termed *life* and *light* in that place, from which he willed man himself to be born and fashioned. It is impossible, therefore, to know yourself unless you also know God himself; for "he who knows himself, passes over," by the testimony of the same <term>Trismegistus<note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary figure to whom the Hermetic corpus—a series of philosophical and spiritual texts—is attributed. Fludd relies heavily on these texts to bridge Christian theology and natural philosophy.</note></term>, "to God." 

It is enough for us, therefore, that we cannot know ourselves unless—with every effort of our intellect, all the strength of our soul, and constant prayers for divine grace and light—we approach daily by degrees to rightly contemplate such things. In this way, we may perceive the admirable idea of the Creator through <term>a posteriori demonstration<note>An argument that works backward from effects to causes. Here, Fludd means observing the physical world (the effect) to understand God (the cause).</note></term>. May the Almighty mercifully grant this to you, to me, and to all, through the merits of His only-begotten Son, Amen.

->ROBERT FLUDD<-

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut tailpiece featuring a central stylized face (resembling a satyr or a foliate head) surrounded by symmetrical scrolls, acanthus leaves, and floral decorative motifs.</image-desc>

<summary>In this concluding section of his preface, Robert Fludd argues that self-knowledge is impossible without the knowledge of God, citing Hermes Trismegistus to support the idea that humans are meant to contemplate the highest heavens. He frames the study of nature as a gradual, prayerful ascent toward understanding the Creator through His works.</summary>

<keywords>Robert Fludd, Hermes Trismegistus, Empyrean, self-knowledge, a posteriori, cosmology, Hermeticism, divine light</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal Preface (Proem) of the work. It transitions from the general address to the reader—which concluded by stating that man must approach God through the study of his creations—into a philosophical explanation of the "a posteriori" method (reasoning from effects back to their causes).</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut header featuring elaborate acanthus leaf scrolls, flowers, and vines.</image-desc>

### P R E F A C E,
# IN WHICH IT IS DEMONSTRATED,
## HOW CAUSES ARE INVESTIGATED THROUGH THEIR EFFECTS,
### THAT IS, BY DEMONSTRATION
->from what follows.<-
<note>original: "à posteriori". In logic, this refers to reasoning that works backward from observed effects to discover their underlying causes.</note>

<image-desc>Large ornamental drop cap 'Q' decorated with floral motifs.</image-desc>

SINCE it is impossible for even the wisest or most ingenious person to demonstrate or know GOD himself—the author of the fabric of the <term>Macrocosm<note>From the Greek 'makros kosmos,' meaning 'the great world.' This refers to the entire universe, which Fludd viewed as a giant living organism that mirrors the 'Microcosm' or 'little world' of the human being.</note></term>—through <term>prior principles<note>original: "à priori". This refers to reasoning from causes to effects, or knowing something by its very essence rather than its outward appearance.</note></term>, seeing as defining Him in His essence was never granted to anyone (not even to the divine leader Moses), nor will it ever be granted in any age—so omnipotent and incomprehensible is He in His very being!—it is therefore necessary that He be investigated through what follows, so that our knowledge may approach Him through His more excellent effects. The Psalmist <note>Referring to Psalm 19:4, "Their voice goes out into all the earth."</note> teaches us to observe His more noble effects in the heavens, and His glory in the firmament, as if in a most polished mirror, with universal eyes. To gain knowledge of these things, since they are placed above us, it will be necessary to ascend through the levels of the elements, as if moving from more complex things toward simpler ones through <term>analysis<note>original: "resolutionem". In 17th-century science, this was the process of 'resolving' or breaking down a compound substance into its primary, simple components.</note></term>. Nor should any rest be allowed in this ascent until those first principles are reached, which, because of their extreme simplicity, cannot undergo further analysis. This journey is therefore immense, very thorny, and dangerous; and truly, it is not easy to find the ladders by which we might reach the peak of this invisible height without the help of divine light and the illumination of the Spirit. 

Therefore, I humbly pray and implore Your Majesty, most merciful GOD, Creator of all things and my helper—let it not displease You that I, an unworthy sinner, pray for Your mercy: deign to illuminate me in the knowledge of created things, so that, since You are unknown by <term>first causes<note>original: "per prius".</note></term>, You may at least be known from <term>what follows<note>original: "ex posteriori".</note></term>, namely through Your glorious effects. Indeed, You promised to open the door to those who knock diligently, and You have forbidden no one from eagerly searching the fountain of goodness; especially since that Spirit of life, which You breathed into our father Adam, warns and urges us every moment to strive toward the clear fountain of blessedness and the ultimate goal of divine knowledge. This inner drive is no less constant than the way the heat of the Sun (Your most noble creature) is perceived to move an unripe fruit toward its mature perfection. Indeed, by this very breath of Yours, adorned with a divine mind, daily the [minds] of men...

<vocab>Macrocosmus (the universe), a priori (from cause to effect), a posteriori (from effect to cause), Deus (God), Moyses (Moses), Psalmista (the Psalmist/David), cognitio (knowledge), Spiritus (Spirit), creatura (creature/created thing)</vocab>

<summary>Fludd argues that because God's essence is unknowable to humans directly, we must study the physical universe (the Macrocosm) to understand the Creator through His "effects." He describes this intellectual journey as an ascent from the complex physical elements up to the simplest divine principles.</summary>

contemplation is swept up to that third heaven, the dwelling of the blessed, and is made happy by beholding your inexplicable glory, and grows rich with spiritual wealth: Illuminate, I say, O good GOD, me your servant though unworthy, with the brilliance of your light, so that without the interruption of darkness or the Devil I may rightly and effectively know you and your creatures, for the love and merits of our Lord JESUS CHRIST your son, who lives and reigns with you forever and ever.

*Since, therefore, we must arrive at the knowledge of causes through effects, our investigation must begin from those things more known to us, which are of two kinds: namely, **matter** and **form**. Moses and Saint Peter <note>Fludd refers to Genesis 1:2 ("the Spirit of God moved over the face of the waters") and 2 Peter 3:5, which describes the earth being formed out of water.</note> have left to us the teaching that the **matter** of all things is water; which we perceive by our senses to be more subtle the higher it ascends, and thicker the more it sinks toward the center. For the earth, which is the dregs and the thicker and more shapeless part of the dark waters, has visible, sensible, and dense water falling upon its surface. Similarly, that thick and visible water has a thinner and more invisible water floating above it; whose even more subtle part, overflowing higher, is established as the <term>sphere of fire<note>In the traditional Aristotelian/Ptolemaic cosmology, the "sphere of fire" was the highest region of the elemental world, located just below the moon.</note></term> because of its heat and subtlety, which is the portion of the waters of the lower region more subtle than all the rest. And so, without doubt, the mass of water ascends in the same proportion up to the summit of the higher heaven in the form of a pyramid, always sharper and thinner at the top: From this it is certain that the lowest water is of the same substance as the higher water, which is purely Spirit, but differs from it only in form, which we will demonstrate below to be the light created on the first day: as it is by the more copious presence of this light that the waters are made thinner; but by a smaller amount they are rendered more thick and dense. Moreover, the portion of light contained in the lowest heaven is of the same nature and essence as that of the higher heavens, but differs only because of its composition; since the density of matter hinders the active disposition of the lower light, and constrains its rays in such a way that it renders them destructive and burning, as is seen in <term>artificial fire<note>Here meaning man-made fire, like a hearth fire or a candle, as opposed to celestial light.</note></term>. Once these things are known, will it be impossible—through experiments and conclusions made in water, both visible and invisible, and in the fiery light of the lower region of the world—to investigate also the natures of those subtle waters and the thinnest light of the higher heavens, since there is no difference between them except in the proportion of their corporeality? For we see that a <term>smaller machine<note>A reference to the concept of the Microcosm (man or small models) mirroring the Macrocosm (the universe).</note></term> performs the same work as a large one, though not in the same proportion: For our thick fire, by its own power, makes images painted on paper move; and in the same way the luminous nature of the sun moves its light and ethereal body from the west to the east: Similarly, by the power of artificial fire, [a thing] is driven high upwards and tossed here and there; so that fire sent from heaven into animate bodies drives them here and there and makes them move: Finally, we perceive the sun itself, by the effect of its light, refining denser things; surely the power of elementary and artificial fire will also perform this same thing? Through experiments of this kind, therefore, and from the action of the heat of the lower, thick light in the same region’s*

## CHAPTER I.
->*Concerning the infinite nature and the maker of all things.*<-

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial letter 'N' decorated with floral patterns and a small figure.</image-desc>

INFINITE NATURE, which is the immense Spirit, ineffable, beyond understanding, outside of all imagination, above all essence, and unnamable—to be proclaimed by the heart alone—the most wise, most merciful, FATHER, WORD, and HOLY SPIRIT; the highest and only good, an incomprehensible depth, the unity of all creatures, which is more powerful than all power, greater than all excellence, worthier than all praise; an indivisible TRINITY, a most splendid and unutterable light; finally, the divine mind, free and separated from all mortal thickening <note>original: "concretione mortali," referring to the density or "clotted" nature of physical matter</note>, the glory of all things, divine nature, necessity, end, and renewal. This, I say, <margin>Hermes, in the Sacred Sermon <note>original: "Hermes fac. serm." likely referring to the "Hieros Logos" or Sacred Sermon attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note></margin> the highest and greatest GOD of all, whose name be blessed forever, most skillfully built the wondrous machine of the whole Macrocosm <term>Macrocosm</term> <note>The "great world" or the universe as a whole.</note> and most beautifully decorated its structure. Indeed, nearly all philosophers, both ancient and modern—except for the Peripatetics <term>Peripatetics</term> <note>Followers of Aristotle, who believed the universe had no beginning and would have no end.</note>, who claim the world has existed from eternity—assert this with a unanimous consensus, calling this most divine founder of the universe by many names appropriate to every property of his action. Among these, Hermes calls him *eternal*; Thales calls him *the most ancient*; Plato calls him *the unbegotten principle of the world*; others call him *the infinite cause, existing as if outside all things, yet within all things and everywhere*; others call him *the Being of beings* <note>original: "Ens entium"</note>, *the first cause*—insofar as other causes and effects depend upon him—*the maker of all things, the author*, and according to Plato, *the founding intellect*. 

Wherefore, several others have called him the Prince, governor, and arranger of worldly things, the first motor and the first mover, and the immovable principle; for all things are moved by him while he remains immovable and subject to no passion <note>In this context, "passion" means being acted upon or changed by external forces.</note>, since he is always stable, fixed, and permanent. *Trismegistus* calls him the *principle of the universe*, by which he seems to understand the sole maker of the workmanship; finally, *Plato and Mercury Trismegistus* call him *Father*, insofar as he is the author of all fertility and the parent of all things. Lastly, the greater chorus of philosophers (among whom I name *Orpheus and Democritus*) concludes that GOD *has every name, since all things are in him and he is in all things*; not otherwise than how all straight lines drawn from a center to the circumference are said to be in the center, or just as a number is said to be in unity <note>The number one.</note>, which is the common measure, source, and origin of all numbers, and contains every number joined uniquely within itself. For this reason, the Pythagoreans and those most skilled in the science of numbers applied the *monad or unity* <term>monad</term> <note>From the Greek "monas," meaning "unit" or "one." In Pythagorean philosophy, it represents the first cause or God.</note> to God the Maker; both because he was alone and unique before all things, and also because he stood as the first actor and sole mover in perfecting the vast structure of the workmanship; his...

Finally, to the spiritual power, or the fiery and lucid form, through whose presence the said matter or subject was brought from potentiality into act, and all parts and regions of the machine, together with their creatures, were most excellently distinguished and arranged, they assigned the *Triad* <note>original: "Triadem," the "Three," representing the divine spark or form that organizes matter.</note>, because this very essence claimed for itself the place of the Third Being in the constitution of the universal workmanship. Among them, I can never sufficiently admire or praise the sacred revelation of *Hermes Trismegistus* <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure linked to the god Hermes and the Egyptian god Thoth, regarded as the author of the Hermetic Corpus.</note> and his depth in divine mysteries; for in his *Poimandres* <note>original: "Pimandro," the first book of the Corpus Hermeticum, which deals with creation.</note> he revealed to us, divinely and beyond human understanding, the hidden secrets of GOD and his universal structure. After him, we consider *Plato* to be fuller of divine knowledge; since, against the assertions of the Peripatetics <term>Peripatetics</term> <note>Followers of Aristotle, who generally taught that the universe was eternal rather than created from a beginning.</note>, he constantly asserted that the world and those things visible and invisible within the world were made by the first unbegotten principle. Although it is impossible to describe this three-fold Artificer essentially, nevertheless, according to the contemplation of some Doctors <note>Refers to learned scholars or "Doctors of the Church."</note>, he is depicted to the eyes in this way: namely, either in *human form*.

### ON THE PRINCIPLES OF THE MACROCOSM.
C 2

Or in the figure of the Sun; for under this form the Pagans <term>Ethnici</term> <note>original: "Ethnici." This refers to the non-Christian "pagan" philosophers of antiquity, particularly those of the East.</note> and especially the Orientals adore **GOD**.

<image-desc>A large, detailed woodcut or engraving of the Sun. In the center is a circular human face with calm, symmetrical features. <margin>
The Sages indeed compared the Deity to the Sun, because
</margin>
From **GOD** all spiritual and invisible splendor is derived, and all things are inwardly illuminated by him, no differently than how all visible and external clarity and brightness proceed from the Sun itself; for just as the Sun is in material things, so in spiritual things **GOD** is the Emperor holding a fiery scepter. 

According to the wise *Sidrac* <note>Referring to the *Book of Sidrac*, a popular medieval encyclopedia of knowledge written as a dialogue between King Boctus and the philosopher Sidrac.</note>, three things are perceived in the essence of the Sun: namely, the Sun itself, its light or brightness, and its life-giving heat. They relate the Sun to **GOD** the Father, its brightness to the Son, and its heat to the Holy Spirit; the Splendor <note>original: "Splendor," referring to the divine light.</note> arises and is derived immediately from the single Solar body, while the effect of the heat and its benevolent virtue descend from both.

Or in the form of a *Triangle*, because, just as in a triangular body three distinct angles are found in a single subject, so in the one Deity <note>original: "Deitate."</note> there exist three distinct persons by no means divided from unity. And, as it seems to us, that incomprehensible and infinite extension of the divine Triangle <term>Trigonus</term> <note>original: "Trigonus," a term for a triangle, often used in mathematical and mystical contexts to signify the Trinity.</note> can be described, not ineptly, according to the limits of human understanding by the following demonstration.

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.

<image-desc>A theological and cosmological diagram centered around an equilateral triangle representing the Holy Trinity. Surrounding the inner contents and within the triangle are the words "Heaven" (original: "Cælum"), "of the Trin-" (original: "trini="), and "-ity" (original: ". tatis"), forming the phrase "Heaven of the Trinity." Between the triangle and the central circles is a zig-zag or wavy pattern representing divine radiance. In the center is a series of concentric circles representing the structure of the universe. Calum"), "E Ethereal Heaven" (original: "E Æthereum. Calum"), and "D Empyrean Heaven" (original: "D Empyreum. Calum").</image-desc>

God can be compared to this incomprehensible Triangle, because, encompassing all things within Himself and extending Himself everywhere by His virtue, He brings all things into action <note>original: "actuat." This suggests God is the "active principle" that makes everything function.</note>, and outside of Him there is nothing. Furthermore, each corner-point <term>conus</term> <note>original: "conus," literally "cone," referring here to the vertex or point of the triangle.</note> of the angle corresponds to a third part of the world, and yet all joined together in the unity of nature, they constitute a single regular, equal, and most perfect Triangle. This is done so that it may be declared through this demonstration that, just as all the angles of the aforementioned Triangle, projecting from the spherical surface of the world, are found to be similar and equal in every dimension, shape, and capacity, so also are the three persons in the unity of the divine essence <term>essentia</term> <note>original: "essentia," the fundamental nature or "being" of God as understood in scholastic theology.</note> co-equal in every measure of perfection. Similarly, it is to be gathered from this same demonstration that, however much the for-

### ON THE PRINCIPLES OF THE MACROCOSM.
<sig>C 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: CA-</meta>

form, although a triangular figure is, among all others in the nature of things, the least capacious <term>capax</term> <note>original: "capax," referring to geometric capacity. In classical geometry, a triangle encloses the smallest area relative to its perimeter, whereas a circle encloses the most.</note>. Nevertheless, because of the trinity of persons and their equality in the godhead, this triple essence—when compared to an equilateral Triangle—is extended beyond all nature. It is so spacious in its own nature that, by its infinity, it even encompasses that figure which is the most capacious in the entire universe of things: namely, the circular figure.

We have therefore described GOD in this place insofar as He is said to be within all things—that is, insofar as He is said to be present everywhere. But indeed, insofar as *Trismegistus* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary sage of the Hermetic tradition.</note> called the world the *image of* GOD *himself*, to that extent we say that the image and likeness <note>original: "simulachrum"</note> of God himself is seen in the Spirit of the world, just as a human likeness is perceived in a mirror. For nothing could be seen in the worldly mirror <term>speculum mundanum</term> before GOD shone upon it; no more than a man’s likeness can be perceived in a mirror without the light of fire or the Sun. 

Now, GOD before the creation of the world shone only upon himself, and reflected his own radiance <note>original: "ardorem," meaning heat or burning light.</note> back into himself, as *Trismegistus* testifies. But when He willed to create the world, He changed his form, and all things were suddenly revealed and all things converted into light, as is recorded in the first book of the *Pimander* <note>The *Poimandres*, a foundational text of the Corpus Hermeticum describing the creation of the cosmos.</note>. Therefore, the hidden GOD has now manifested his splendid face in the worldly mirror, his light having been brought into action; He continually communicates his idea to us and his creatures through that most holy gift, because the Sages <note>The ancient philosophers and mystics.</note> said that this visible and external world was formed according to the example of its maker. 

Just as, therefore, a man’s face is seen in a mirror through reflection, so the triple distinction of the one essence is reflected everywhere into the universal spirit of the world. The demonstration of this matter is clear to the eye in the following example.

Æthe:), and the center contains a dark orb representing the material world. Shaded rays emanate outward from the diagonal edges of the diamond, representing divine light.</image-desc>

TREATISE I. BOOK I.

## CHAPTER II.

->*In how many ways is GOD said to be in a place, how is He present everywhere and fills all things? And concerning His principal seat in this world?*<-

Before we discuss the seat or proper dwelling of <term>Nature-naturing</term> <note>original: "Naturæ naturantis." A philosophical term describing God as the active, creative force behind the world, rather than the physical world itself.</note>, we must ask whether He can be in any place at all, since He is infinite in His essence? To this doubt, the entire School of Theologians responds by making a distinction: He is in a place *not definitively*, like intelligences <note>i.e., angels or spiritual beings.</note> or spiritual creatures, nor *circumscriptively* <note>meaning, He is not "contained" within borders or edges like a physical object.</note>, as physical things are, *but only by His presence*; although according to Gregory <note>Likely St. Gregory the Great, a 6th-century Pope and theologian.</note>, GOD is said to be in all things in three ways: namely, either *presentially*, as He makes good manifest to them; or *potentially*, namely through His influence; or *essentially*, that is, through the intimacy of His essence. Moreover, GOD is said to be everywhere insofar as His divine power permeates all beings, through which He establishes the relationship of the potentiality of the whole universe to Himself. Thus He is also said to fill all things, not so that He is contained by them, but rather so that they are contained by Him. 

For, says Ambrose <note>St. Ambrose, a 4th-century Bishop and influential Church Father.</note>, it is the property of the Deity to be everywhere and in all things. It is clear, therefore, that GOD is not in all things in a way where any object can contain Him according to its own small measure—as the greater contains the less—but He is said to fill all things so that there is no place where He is not. We understand, then, that GOD is within all things but not enclosed, because He is infinite and immeasurable; and outside all things but not excluded, since He encompasses all created things by His uncircumscribed greatness. And from this, it follows that all things created by GOD are called "good" by Him through participation <note>This refers to the Platonic idea that physical things are not "goodness" itself, but "participate" in it by reflecting God's nature.</note>—that is, through His presence, or according to the degrees of essence by which angels are placed above souls, and souls above animals. And yet, insofar as any creature has obtained more or less dignity according to the beauty of its form from the first nature or proceeding Being, to that extent (as Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who influenced Renaissance thought.</note> testifies) it will possess a higher or lower seat in the goodness acquired through participation. 

But indeed, as "good" is understood absolutely, simply, and—to use the words of Iamblichus—"above essence," to that extent it is taken for the cause of good, that is, for the principle of essential good. *Hermes Trismegistus* <note>The legendary sage of the Hermetic tradition.</note> discusses this in the place where he warns us to beware of calling anything "good" except GOD alone. Therefore, all the works of the Creator are found to be participants in goodness—not the "above essence" goodness which belongs to GOD alone—but according to the essence they receive, insofar as they take their essence, form, and substantial light from the fiery benevolence of the Creator. By these gifts, they are illuminated, given form, and brought from potentiality into actual existence, and consequently they are called "good" by participation. 

And indeed, the world and the world's creatures received this essence-making goodness from the Creator Himself, not because He was forced by necessity (as some philosophical traditions suggest), but from His free and most abundant will, wishing to perfect all His creatures, according to the mind of the Royal Psalmist: "Whatever the Lord willed, He did in heaven and on earth" <note>original: "Quæcunque voluit Dominus, fecit in cælo & in terra." (Psalm 135:6)</note>. Finally, the ancient philosophers placed the habitation of this Nature-naturing in heaven, because of the beauty and clarity of that place. Hence Plato asserted that GOD *dwells in a fiery substance*, that is, in the ether. With this, the more recent philosophers (among whom I name Isidore, Bede, and Basil <note>St. Isidore of Seville, the Venerable Bede, and St. Basil the Great—medieval and early Christian scholars.</note>, true worshippers of this Nature) seem to agree, calling the Empyrean Heaven the dwelling place of GOD. For although GOD is said to be everywhere (as stated above), yet properly and principally, that is—

It draws on Scholastic, Platonic, and Hermetic authorities to argue that all creatures are "good" only through their participation in God's essential light.</summary>

...then He is said to be present in this heaven in a greater way, where the works of His power shine more brightly, as if in a more worthy place and position in which the divine power operates more fittingly, as Damascenus <note>John of Damascus, an 8th-century monk and theologian.</note> testifies. For GOD is not said to be above all things and outside all things by a physical distance, but by the excellence of His nature, as is held in the *Theological Resolutions, treatise 1, part 1, question 4.* And this is why this heaven is called *intellectual*, because GOD, whose seat it is, is called an Intellectual Spirit. Some others have attributed this super-celestial region to angels and blessed souls, and therefore established another dwelling-place beyond this one, which they called the *heaven of the Trinity*, belonging solely to the Trinity—namely, the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit. They say that this is GOD Himself in reality, but differs in our way of reasoning. For according to *Albert* <note>Albert the Great (Albertus Magnus), a 13th-century philosopher and teacher of Thomas Aquinas.</note>, this heaven is the excellence of divine power, which contains and encompasses all created things. Hence, to be in this heaven is to be in the equality of the power of GOD, who creates, contains, and saves all things. Through the vision of *Ezekiel*, all these things are clearly explained; though these two regions—namely, of the Intelligences <note>A term for angels or spiritual beings who "govern" the spheres.</note> and of the Trinity—seem to differ only in the purity and exaltation of their essence, the Prophet indicates they are contained in one Empyrean heaven <note>From the Greek 'empyros,' meaning fiery; the highest part of the universe in ancient and medieval cosmology.</note> (so called from its perfect illumination), not unlike how air and fire are contained in the ethereal heaven. For the single heaven surrounding the curve of the firmament reflected the likeness of a man, whose upper part from the loins upward shone with a fire the color of amber <note>original: "carabeæ." Referring to the 'chashmal' or 'electrum' described in Ezekiel 1:27.</note>—that is, the thinnest and most spiritual fire; while the lower part from the loins downward glowed with a fire somewhat denser and thicker. Through this partitioning of the place, he seems to indicate two regions differing in dignity: namely, the pre-celestial heaven proper to spiritual creatures, and that super-celestial heaven.

# CHAPTER III.

## *Concerning the origin of prime matter: is it created or uncreated?*

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'I' with floral scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>

A massive disagreement has arisen among recent Philosophers concerning the origin of the first matter of creation. The larger choir of them says assertively that it was created; among whom *Artephius* <note>A medieval author to whom many alchemical texts were attributed.</note> uses nearly these words: *The Creator of all in the beginning, without the utterance of a word, said: "Let such a creature be," and afterward that nature or prime matter was created by GOD.* But the other, smaller choir—in which *Paracelsus* <note>The famous 16th-century Swiss physician and alchemist who revolutionized medicine and chemistry.</note> participates with the rest of his school—calls this matter the *Great Mystery* <note>original: "mysterium magnum."</note>, which bore no likeness to anything created, nor was it even created, but was only prepared by GOD, the highest craftsman. Both of these schools attempt to prove their reasons from the Holy Scripture delivered through Moses, interpreting the nature and sense of the word *Beginning* <note>original: "Principi." Referring to the first word of Genesis.</note> in various ways. For *Artephius*, with the others of his opinion, explains the start of the narrative of Genesis handed down to us by Moses—namely, *In the beginning GOD created the heaven and the earth*, etc.—in this way: *In the first place, or first of all, GOD created the heaven and the earth.* Attempting to refute this exposition of the word *Beginning*, the followers of *Paracelsus* propose three doubts to us: namely, whether the interpreter intended to refer the name of the *Beginning* to some *time*, or to *order* and *time* together, or whether he intended to designate a *first place* by it. To these questions, they respond by denying them absolutely and confidently. *First*, they argue that time did not yet exist, since it only had its origin from the "evening and the morning"; hence they in no way admit that interpretation of *beginning*. *Second*, they argue that it is absurd to place the world before its own mat-

It also introduces a debate between traditionalists like Artephius and followers of Paracelsus over whether "prime matter" was created out of nothing or existed as a "Great Mystery" before formal creation.</summary>

...[prime] matter; because clearly potential things are not placed after actual things, nor the principle after that which is principled; they therefore entirely reject this interpretation. Finally, they contend that the name of *Principle* cannot be adapted to *Place* by the reasoning taken from these words of the *first chapter of the Gospel of St. John: The Word was in the beginning;* <note>original Latin: "Verbum erat in principio."</note> for to wish to include the Word of GOD in a place is believed to be a crime by all pious people, since it is incomprehensible; and furthermore, even before the creation of the world, there was no place, because there was nothing placed. With these, I say, and similar arguments, *Paracelsus* with some of his disciples strives to refute the aforementioned opinion of the contrary sect; he understands the primordial words of Moses in this sense: *In the great uncreated mystery, GOD created heaven and earth,* <note>original Latin: "In mysterio magno increato creavit DEUS caelum & terram."</note> that is, in the formless, dark, and potential prime matter, GOD created heaven and earth. And this Paracelsian opinion does not seem to differ from that of the *Peripatetics* <note>Followers of Aristotle's school of philosophy.</note>, who, thinking the world to be eternal, *decreed that prime matter was also co-eternal with the World itself.* Some *Philosophers* also rise up against the creation of *Hyle* <note>original Greek: "ὕλη" (hyle), a term for the raw, underlying substance of the universe.</note> with an argument of this sort: *That which is formless is not created: But hyle is formless: Therefore hyle is not created.* The major premise is clearly demonstrated by the fact *that creation is an inactuation* <note>The process of bringing something from a state of "potential" to "actual" form.</note> *and making something out of nothing: But hyle in its nature is nothing in actuality, wherefore it is certain that it lacks all form.* But referring all these ambiguous and controversial disputes of those Philosophers to my superiors in Philosophy, and to those far more learned than I in these matters, I shall at least descend to the description of that matter.

## CHAPTER IV.
### Description of Prime Matter or the subject of the universal craftsmanship.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'M' featuring scrolling floral motifs and a small figure within the ornamentation.</image-desc>
MATTER or the subject, in whose womb that greatest architect of our Macrocosm <note>The "Great World" or the universe at large.</note> arranged his structure, is that Philosophical *Hyle*; <note>original Greek: "ὕλη".</note> which the Physicists have called absolutely first matter: and indeed, as nothing is more common, so also nothing is found more unknown than it. Regarding its investigation, therefore, two among the other ancient Philosophers especially devoted their diligent labor: the first of whom, namely *Thales* (with whom *Heraclitus and Hesiod* agreed), after long study and much midnight oil, constantly affirmed that *water is the first matter of things*, and confirms the reason for his opinion *because, he says, the seed and nourishment of all things is moisture.* Both *Pagan* and *sacred* writers also seem to approve of this opinion. For the former consecrated *Neptune, Oceanus, and Thetis* <note>Mythological figures associated with water and the sea.</note> for this reason; the latter—among whom I name that divine High Priest of Philosophy, *Moses*—called that matter *the abyss and water*; and *St. Peter* in *2 Peter 3* indicated to us that *the heaven and the earth were from water and through water.* Another diligent searcher of this matter was *Anaxagoras*, who after many labors endured in this study, was of the opinion *that the first matter of things was a rude and indigested mass, called Chaos*, with whom the *Poet in the Metamorphoses* <note>Refers to the Roman poet Ovid, who describes the universe beginning as "Chaos."</note> seems to agree. Other Philosophers wrote otherwise about this matter: For *Anaximander* established infinite principles as the material origin, *Anaximenes* infinite air, *Diogenes* air possessed of divine reason, *Plato* an invisible and formless species, *Zeno* the Stoic the substance of fire turned through air into water, the *Epicureans* atoms, *Empedocles* the four elements; almost all of whom, on account of the occult and hidden disposition of this mystery—which without doubt is truly and essentially known only to GOD alone—wrote with uncertainty and indeterminately, contradicting one another, and...

### ON THE PRINCIPLES OF THE MACROCOSM. ###
<note>The "Macrocosm" is a term used by early scientists and philosophers to refer to the "great world" or the universe as a whole.</note>

<warning>Large brown stain on the upper right side of the page, though text remains legible.</warning>

Whatever they vigorously and plausibly conceived in their minds, they immediately set forth in their books. This is what *Plato* also seems to prove sufficiently in his *Timaeus* when he describes this matter as **a thing perceptible without the senses, and scarcely believable by illegitimate reasoning**; <note>original Latin: "esse rem sine sensu perceptibilem, & ratiocinatione spuria vix credibilem"</note> for from these words it is clear that his knowledge in this matter is like a dream and mere imagination. *Augustine* also seems to agree with him in this respect, as is evident from the following discourse: **When**, he says, **I conceive of something formless, I understand nothing before I understood it; just as by seeing nothing, darkness is seen; by hearing nothing, silence is heard.** The difficulty of knowing this thing is admitted by *Aristotle* himself, who asserts that this matter cannot be known or described by itself, except by analogy—that is, through examples taken from qualities that are foreign to its actual nature. Hence he, along with other Greek philosophers, likened it to <term>hyle</term> <note>original Greek: ὕλη (hyle), literally meaning "wood" or "timber."</note>, which is to say, to a certain immense forest whose wood is fit for constructing any house, machine, or other thing. For this reason, the divine *Moses* also compared it sometimes to an empty and void earth, and sometimes to waters and the abyss; *Hermes Trismegistus*, in his *Pymander*, compared it to a certain horrendous shadow migrating into a humid nature; *Plato* to a mother, a nurse, and the seat of generated things, because it contains and fosters all things; *Augustine* to darkness and silence, as has been said; *Pythagoras* to duality; *Some* to a mirror in which the soul of the world is perceived; *others* to wax and clay, which are fit for receiving any impression; *There are also those* who wish to compare it to an ointment, which is ready to receive and retain any scent. And it is from this that *Plato* called it infinite; namely, because of its inclination toward infinite forms: although this matter could also be called infinite as far as we are concerned, for **God** alone knows how far this potential extends beyond the convexity of the world. And *Aristotle* seems to understand this same thing in his *book on the heavens and the world*; where he says, **that outside the heavens there is neither place, nor body, nor dimension, nor fullness, nor time, nor number, nor measure of movement**, and consequently nothing exists outside the heavens except this formless matter, to which the aforementioned properties alone are proper. Hence it is also that *Hermes Trismegistus* in his sacred discourse speaks in such words: **The shadow**, he says, **is infinite in the abyss, etc.**

We conclude, therefore, from all the writings of both ancient and more recent philosophers, that this "prime matter" is the primordial Being—infinite, formless, in potentiality for something as much as for nothing; of no quantity or dimension, since it can be called neither small nor large; of no quality, since it is neither subtle nor thick nor perceptible; of no property or inclination; neither moving nor resting; without any color or elementary quality. It is, however, the first passive subject of all actions and capable of receiving all things: hence it is called the Mother of the World, in whose womb the ethereal spheres, decorated with ruddy fires, and the four elements suspended below near the center, are contained as if in a maternal uterus. Moreover, we have depicted in this place an imaginary image of this formless matter—imitating the description of *Hermes Trismegistus* and the truth-telling *Moses*—under the form of a very black smoke, or vapor, or a horrendous shadow, or a dark abyss, or finally a certain rude, undigested, and imperceptible mass.

### CHAPTER V.
->*On darkness and privation.*<-

*Augustine*, in his work *Against the Manicheans*, <note>The Manicheans were a religious group who believed in a dualism between light (good) and darkness (evil). Augustine wrote extensively to refute their ideas.</note> asserts that <term>privation</term> <note>In philosophy, "privation" is not just the absence of something, but the absence of a quality that should naturally be present (like blindness is the privation of sight).</note> is nothing other than darkness, which is defined as the absence of light. But if the meaning of darkness is rightly considered, we will perceive that it extends further than the term "privation." For, by the testimony of *Moses*, darkness was upon the face of the abyss before light or form was created; whereas "privation" cannot be named except in respect to some "position"—that is, where there is an absence of some preceding form. Therefore, to agree with *Augustine*, every privation is darkness—that is, of a luminous form 
<meta>catchword: luminous</meta>

<summary>This page distinguishes between "darkness" as the primordial state before creation and "privation" as the absence of a specific form, using the Book of Genesis and St. Augustine to define the nature of the void.</summary>
<keywords>Augustine, Moses, darkness, privation, abyss, prime matter, Manicheans, infinity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of darkness and privation, specifically how the removal of light relates to the corruption of material things in the lower heavens. It transitions from a philosophical definition of darkness to the theological and alchemical interpretation of the first day of creation.</meta>

# ON THE PRINCIPLES OF THE MACROCOSM.

the removal of light; but not conversely: From which it is manifest that darkness or privation happened only to things subject to corruption <note>In this context, "corruption" refers to the natural process of breaking down or decaying, which allows for new life to begin.</note> from the creation of the world, such as are the elements and elemental things of the lower heaven: for since the form of the elements is not sufficient to satisfy the appetite of matter (hence their volatility and imperfection), it is necessary that their same matter always hunger for a new form. Indeed, since it cannot satisfy this hunger without the absence of the preceding form, it is necessary that corruption or <term>putrefaction</term> <note>Alchemists viewed putrefaction as a necessary stage of death and decay that precedes rebirth and purification.</note> precede that new form, the old form having first been extinguished and cast off. The signs of this are darkness, which are nothing other than blackness and gloom, signifying the absence of light and form, from which the beauty and essence of the putrefied thing were previously derived and known. This *blackness* the Chemical Philosophers <note>The "Chemical Philosophers" refers to alchemists who sought to understand the spiritual and physical laws of nature through laboratory work.</note> have called by many names: among which they most frequently called this sign of putrefaction the *raven’s head* <note>original Latin: "caput corvi"</note>, by which they understood a purely accidental principle, and a medium joining matter and form, and coupling the bridegroom with the bride; which dark color indeed perishes immediately after generation. 

Here, therefore, it must be considered that the matter of a thing, while it is submerged in darkness, is held in a deprived state of reform, just like the first matter of the <term>macrocosm</term> <note>The "Macrocosm" refers to the universe or the "Great World," as opposed to the "Microcosm" or the "Little World" of the human being.</note> before the production of light. For it has the potential for the presence and beauty of a certain light, without which it can never be led into actual existence. 

It must be understood, truly, that *the word "darkness" has a two-fold meaning among Philosophers; for sometimes it is taken for some dark substance, namely, as for dark and black air, or for the shadow of some opaque body; sometimes it is also taken for a mere accident, namely for the color of blackness: In the first meaning, it is handed down by Moses as being over the face of the abyss; in the second, it is held to be the privation of light;* however it is taken, nevertheless, darkness is always entirely repugnant to light. For darkness makes a thing hidden and concealed; it brings on fear and sorrow, removes shame, brings about sleep, and is most friendly to rest; hence it is the companion of matter. Light, however, renders a thing known and manifest, removes fear, brings confidence, courage, and joy, makes one modest, drives away numbness and sluggishness, nor does it allow an animal to rest, since it excites life and motion in its blood: for it is, as was said above, the origin of forms and actions.

### CHAPTER VI.

->*On the universal essence by which the artisan informed the matter of the universal work.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' containing floral motifs and stylized foliage within a square frame.</image-desc>

The most wise maker of the world, who said, *I am the light of the world, the true fire, and the father of lights*, decreed to make this formless matter—this foundation of nature, or subject of the universal machine, and habitation of forms—and, to speak with *Plato*, to make it their nurse; so that by their presence, the mass of the entire abyss, overwhelmed in darkness, might be rendered visible and perceptible and led into actual existence. To accomplish this with ease, He first communicated the clarity of His fire to the <term>Empyrean heaven</term> <note>The Empyrean was considered the highest, most spiritual part of the heavens, composed of pure light or fire.</note> (for *the spirit of his mouth*, whom *Hermes Trismegistus* <note>original Latin: "Mercurius Trismegistus". Hermes Trismegistus was a legendary figure to whom many ancient philosophical and alchemical texts were attributed.</note> calls the *God* of fire and the divinity of the *spirit*, *was carried over the waters*); then and secondarily, He most freely communicated it to the ethereal heaven and the sun and His other spherical creatures, so that through their power, as if through instruments, the ethereal heaven might be adorned, and form and life might be bestowed upon the lower creatures. 

Now, this fiery creature, created on the first day in the likeness of the divine idea, and given as the first and most excellent gift from God for the perfection of the rest of His structure, *Moses* called light, *Artephius* <note>An influential medieval alchemist.</note> called clarity, *Plato* called the idea, and *Aristotle* the divine and best principle. For Aristotle himself, in the *Book on the Five Substances* <note>original Latin: "lib. de 5. substant."</note>, acknowledges that light gives form, beauty, and being to all things, since without it no matter can be manifested or known, but remains hidden and as if only in potentiality. This same light *Hermes Trismegistus*...

<meta>This page continues the exploration of light as the primary creative force, transitioning from the discussion of darkness on the previous page to the metaphysical and physical properties of the first-created light.</meta>

### TREATISE I. BOOK I.

[Trisme]gistus <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the origins of alchemy and Hermetic philosophy.</note> in his sacred sermon called it "holy splendor," which he asserts flourished in the beginning and led the elements forth from under the sandy and humid nature; by which holy splendor *Marsilio Ficino, in his commentary on that sermon*, maintained that the unformed bosom of matter was meant to be illustrated with forms. Finally, almost all agree by common consent to call it the first act, the form, the appearance, and the essence. Therefore, this light is a unique substance, and indeed a corporeal one, existing simply in itself, the simplest of all things, the worthiest and most noble; indeed, it is of such great nobility, according to *Augustine in his commentary on Genesis*, that even corporeal things are judged more perfect and noble the more they participate in light; hence he says emphatically that light holds the first place among physical bodies. And this *substance* of light the same *Augustine and Dionysius* define (on account of its sweet heat) *as a pure fire, incombustible, immeasurable, and indivisible, increasing and multiplying itself into infinity, extending itself everywhere, and present to all things in a single moment, grasping all things yet not to be grasped itself, always shining in itself, but invisible and unknown to others through itself:* for it never illuminates visibly unless some body intervenes whose matter is fit to be illuminated. And it is from this that the substance of the middle heaven, although it is very bright in itself and holds the first place among composite bodies due to the abundance of its light, nevertheless does not illuminate during the nighttime. From the source of this super-celestial light is undoubtedly derived that invisible fire of *Zoroaster and Heraclitus*, from which they teach all things were fathered; whose power, though invisible, all animals and all other sublunary creatures—indeed, even the sea itself in its ebb and flow—seem to recognize and feel deeply by day and night. Finally, the Philosophers call this fire a "simple act" because of its liveliness; than which nothing is more mobile, or swifter and more powerful in its motion, nothing more subtle, or more penetrating because of its subtlety, nothing more useful or fuller of power, nothing more beautiful, or more uniform in its essence, nothing more capable of perfecting and exalting beings according to their degree, and nothing more quickly dissolving them or more easily removing their bonds, as *Chalcidius* testifies *in his commentary on the Timaeus.* From these points it is manifest—contrary to *Damascene and Aristotle* (who in this somewhat contradicts himself)—that light is not an "accident" <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a property that does not exist by itself but only within a substance (like the color of an apple). The author argues here that light is a fundamental substance, not just a property.</note>, which is nothing in itself; hence it does not seem likely that it would make the substance in which it inheres more noble, since that is the duty of an essential form, from which both the name of any body and its very being are derived. We conclude, therefore, that light is *either uncreated*, namely GOD "naturing" <note>original: "naturantem," referring to Natura Naturans, or Nature acting as a creative, divine force.</note> all things; (for in GOD the Father is the true light, then in his Son the illustrating and overflowing splendor, and in the Holy Spirit the burning radiance surpassing all understanding) *or created from that uncreated light*, which is like the simplest soul and true essential form of any of the three heavens, informing the clearest spirit as its tether and vehicle. Hence *Iamblichus*, on account of the brightness of the ether, thought that it was nothing other than light itself, even in any of their composite creatures: For in the angels it is a certain infused, shining intelligence, poured over all limits of reason yet received in different degrees according to the nature of the recipient; it descends next to the celestial bodies, where it produces in them a life-giving power, from which life and effective propagation with a life-giving splendor are bestowed upon the lower things: In humans, it is the bright discourse of reason; in other animals, it is a hidden fire governing the actions of life and sense clearly; in vegetables, it is a certain luminous soul, hiding around their centers, causing growth and multiplication into infinity; in minerals, it is also a spark of splendor driving them toward the goal of perfection. Therefore, from the supernatural light created on the first day, the differences of the macrocosm’s heavens and the bodies existing in them arise (for they are distinguished by purity, simplicity, and dignity): For according to the degrees of excess or lack of this essence, the highest heaven differs from the lowest, and the lowest from the middle,
