->## ON THE PRINCIPLES OF THE MACROCOSM.<-

...and every element of each heaven is found to be lowered or elevated by the presence or absence of this light: for the further matter <note>original: *materia*</note> is removed from the nobility of form, the coarser, more impure, darker, and more unworthy it is. From this also comes the diversity of created things and substances; from this their perfection and imperfection, rawness and maturity, volatility and fixation, thickness and subtlety, darkness and splendor, heaviness and lightness, and every proportion of things distinguishing one from another. This is according to *Hermes Trismegistus, who asserts in his sacred sermon that distinct things are balanced by the fiery spirit that carries them:* for prime matter <note>original: *materia prima*—the formless "first stuff" of the universe</note> before the production of this light was not capable of the aforementioned qualities, because, being equally dark and empty, it always retained the same state of imperfection, as if entirely destitute of any action by which it might be moved from one state to another.

From these points, it is manifest that if clarity and light were withdrawn from the world, it must be believed that the matter of the world would return to its first state and disposition, and no part of it would be more excellent than the others in dignity, location, or any other quality or quantity. We shall explain that all these things are possible through the following demonstration *a posteriori* <note>A logical term meaning "from the effect to the cause"—explaining how we can observe these truths in the physical world</note> to the senses, since the fiery nature is derived from light, and the airy body draws its primary origin from water. # TREATISE I. BOOK I.

Here, the bright form, beginning to appear and shine in a circular fashion within the womb of the abyss of *hyle* <note>original: "hylæ," referring to primordial matter or the "first stuff" of creation</note> or darkness, drives the particular darkness and the thicker part of the matter contained within the circle of light toward the center by its own power. The watery spirits <note>referring to the subtle, vaporous substances that emerge during the separation of the elements</note> now begin to be revealed; they are thinner the closer they are to the seat of light, and thicker the further they are removed from the seat of light.

->*Demonstrations by which it becomes clear that if the action of the bright form ceases, and in its total absence, the world and its matter must necessarily return to the first abyss and dark primordial matter, according to the reasons we have expressed above.*<-

Since the presence of solar light is the most closely related and similar thing to the "bright creature" <note>the primordial light</note> created on the first day—inasmuch as, according to the Fathers of Theology, the solar body was made on the fourth day as a receptacle for all those same rays—we shall therefore begin this demonstration of ours with its power and effect. By this, we will prove that the presence of light itself caused this formal alteration in the matter, and that its absence would cause that same matter, which was once given form, to return to its original—that is, its formless—state.

Let there be made, therefore, a sphere <term>pila: a ball or globe</term> of lead of sufficiently large capacity, so that the thickness of its material is not too thin (lest it break quickly) nor too porous (lest air penetrate through it). In the top of this, let a hole be made into which a certain curved tube, descending almost to its bottom and made of the same material, is fixed in such a way that no air can enter or exit by any other path except through the hollow of that channel. The other end of the same channel or curved tube should extend almost to the bottom of another vessel filled with water. Let the sphere be A, the channel or tube B, and the water-filled vessel C.

We conclude, therefore, that the sun's rays, heating sphere A, will expel the thick and smoky air contained in sphere A through channel B into vessel C, to the bottom of the water contained in that vessel C. This thick air, expelled by the power of the heat, will mix with the water of vessel C and produce many bubbles, one after another. But as soon as the sun withdraws, or some other object is placed between the sun and the sphere, or the sphere itself is placed in shadow, the prior state will return to the hollow of the sphere due to the absence of the sun and the cooling of the sphere—namely, that same portion of thick and dark air that was in there before.

The proof is as follows: since the sphere cannot find air of the same nature in the water-filled vessel, it draws to itself the water itself (which is even thicker) in place of the air. Necessity forces a proportion of water to descend into the sphere equal to the amount of air that the solar rays first expelled from it through the thinning and purification of the air. By this experiment, it is shown (as if pointed out with a finger) that every purification in this world and every inequality or diversity in thickness or thinness comes from the act of the essence of light itself, and that by its presence, this difference is maintained in such a state until the end of the world. That all things would return to their original state if light were lacking is infallibly proven by this very experiment. The truth of this is declared even to common eyes by the two experiments written below, which derive their effects from elementary fire. Moreover, we have depicted the demonstration of this first experiment thus: <note>The text likely refers to an accompanying diagram not present in this OCR.</note>

Descending now even further, so that we may draw forth another demonstration from the efficacy of our fire—which in the beginning received its power from the sun, and consequently and secondarily from that same luminous source—we also bring forward this testimony. By this, we prove that in the absence of the fiery nature, all things return to their former state; and the experiment is of the following kind:

If a form of a certain small altar <term>arula: a small altar or brazier used to hold burning coals</term> be constructed, in the middle of whose base one end of a straight tube enters, while the other end enters into a globe placed beneath the altar (made of lead, glass, or some other material), so that no air can escape from the "belly" of the altar or the concavity of the globe except through this tube; and if then one end of a certain bent siphon <note>original: "siphonis," referring to the curved tubing</note> enters the globe or sphere, penetrating to its bottom, with its other end similarly extended into a jar located next to the globe, reaching almost to its bottom (as will be demonstrated below), then if fire is lit upon the altar, we say that the air contained in its concavity, now rarefied <note>thinned out or expanded</note> by the heat of the fire, seeks a larger place. Wherefore, through the tube **E.F.**, the rarefied air in the altar **A.** descends into the sphere **C.** and forces out the moisture contained in the sphere through the siphon **G.H.** into the jar or flask **I.K.L.M.** But when the fire is extinguished, the thinned air recedes and ascends back into its original place, by which...

Similarly, the preceding operation of nature is confirmed by the following pneumatic <term>spirituali: literally "spiritual," but in this 17th-century context, it refers to "pneumatics" or the study of air and gases as "spirits"</term> experiment taken from the conclusions of *Heron*: where the dense air enclosed in the upper region of the small altar is thinned <note>original: "subtiliatur" — made subtle or less dense</note> by the presence of the fire lit upon it. In this thinning, its more "sooty" <note>meaning the denser, unrefined part of the air</note> part is expelled through opening **A** into the lowest region, as into a part more remote from the agent <note>the fire acting upon the air</note>, so that the air of the upper region is illuminated and activated. When the fire is extinguished, however, that sooty and thick portion of air, which had exited from the upper region, returns to its own prior seat and joins with the subtle air, so that the aerial spirit then returns to its former state of bulkiness, in which it was before the approach of the fire.

From these things it is therefore clear that all things given form by the essential light put on their formlessness again after the benevolent gaze of light and its formative presence <term>formalis habitus: the "formal habit" or the way light gives a specific "form" to matter</term> have left and deserted them; and consequently, when light ceases, all things will relapse into the former nature and disposition of dark *hyle* <term>hyle: a Greek term for "prime matter," the raw, chaotic substance of the universe before it is organized by light</term>.

# CHAPTER VII.

->*Concerning the two simple and primary qualities derived from the two first substances; likewise concerning the two qualities arising from those simple ones.*<-

From each of the two aforementioned parts of the world's constitution arises a certain simple nature, which bears the character of its own source. For when the fiery spirit was carried over the waters (which we called the source of light and origin of forms above), it produced a certain essential quality accompanying its actions. Because of this, the Philosophers called it an "agent" <note>the active force</note>, as if it were the effect of form or of the most lively light; they also called this quality "heat" <note>original: "caliditatem"</note>. 

The nature of heat is to penetrate even to the very center of thickness, and by penetrating to open up dense and solid things. By expanding them, it resolves <note>meaning to break down into constituent parts or melt</note> them and makes them loose. After this resolution, it dissipates and separates the thick from the subtle, and cleanses them by rarefaction <note>thinning out</note> or attenuation, making them volatile and light; thus, they are elevated by nature and carried upward. 

Finally, heat gathers, digests, matures, fixes, and perfects those things that are <term>homogeneous: things of the same kind or nature</term>; but it cuts, scatters, expels, and drives back those things that are <term>heterogeneous: things of a different or conflicting nature</term>. It accomplishes all of this insofar as it is immediately derived from the most noble and subtle body of all, and accompanies that body's thinnest and most noble substance. 

The origin of this active nature having thus been declared, it remains for the quality or simple nature of the other constitutive part—namely the passive one—to now be revealed, since no action can occur without a corresponding <term>passion: the state of being acted upon or receptivity</term>. Hence it is necessary,

TREATISE I. BOOK I.

<meta>ghosting/bleed-through from reverse page</meta>

...it is that such a disposition is derived from *hyle* <note>original: "hyle" — a Greek term used by philosophers to describe "prime matter" or the raw, unformed substance of the universe</note> or the primary matter of the macrocosm, buried in darkness before the rise of heat. For at the first appearance of light, the shadows began to move, and just as heat was introduced by the motion of light, so also by the motion of the substance of the abyss, another quality was produced—one that tolerates the actions of heat and is therefore called "passive" (insofar as it suffers more than it acts). This quality accompanies the thicker substance of matter and is intensified or diminished according to the density of that matter, just as heat accompanies the essence of light and is perceived as more intense or relaxed according to the abundance or scarcity of that light.

Therefore, the nature of cold, manifested by the rise of heat, is entirely opposed and hostile to heat. It resists heat as it pursues it with its subtle penetration; by resisting, it gathers, restricts, and condenses the more impure, thicker, and darker parts of the substance of the abyss, contracting them toward the center of the cosmos. Cold naturally flees there, as if to a place most remote from the world’s periphery, in which a greater and purer essence of the luminous Spirit exists, included in it from the beginning. And from this comes the thickness of the elements of the lower heaven; from this comes the density, opacity, and darkness of the earth. From this it also follows that no heavy object could pass beyond the point of the center, even if a hole were bored directly through the entire mass of the earth; namely, because the central point of the earth is the part furthest from the world's circumference, and consequently, the seat in which cold naturally desires to rest, as if free and immune from the enmity and contrary action of heat.

We conclude, therefore, that heat more readily embraces forms, performs the functions of life for every single thing, and becomes the companion of its soul; cold, however, possesses material bodies, loves them, and naturally inheres in them. From the mutual action and passion of these, certain parts of the macrocosm are thinned <note>original: "subtiliantur" — rendered subtle or less dense</note> more or less according to the retreat of cold and the approach of heat—namely the upper regions—and certain parts are thickened according to the absence of heat and the presence of cold—namely the lower regions. Thus the world and its distinct regions were made; if these natures should fail, it is necessary that all things return to their original state and be reduced from something "in act" to something "in potency" <note>from actual existence back to a state of mere potentiality</note>.

From these natures or simple qualities arise two other dispositions, which Artesius <note>Likely referring to a medieval or Renaissance authority on natural philosophy</note> called "simples derived from simples"—namely, humidity and dryness. The first of these is made from the mixing and tempering of the two simples: for if heat is joined with an equal part of cold, the nature of humidity is created. But we call that quality "dryness" when joined with heat or cold where humidity is entirely absent. It is clear, therefore, that dryness is nothing other than the absence of humidity, and thus these two qualities are said to be mutually contrary; they are, however, passive with respect to the first 

->simples, as we shall declare in its proper place<-
->below.<-

# CHAPTER VIII.

## *That two things concur for the existence of prime matter: under what form prime matter appears when reduced into act? and what is thick and subtle water.*

---

<image-desc>An ornate square woodcut initial letter 'A' containing decorative floral patterns and a small landscape scene featuring distant hills and a structure.</image-desc>

At the existence of <term>prime matter<note>The fundamental, unformed substance from which all physical things are created.</note></term> two things are required. The first of these is a certain "Being in potentiality," which is the underlying subject of that hidden matter; the other is a kind of dark veil which, as long as it is present, prevents any impression of a specific thing from being formed upon it. This veil is called "Darkness" by <term>Moses<note>Referring to the author of the Book of Genesis.</note></term>. By the presence of these shadows, beings are said to exist "in potentiality" (as possibilities), but by their absence, they are said to be "reduced from potentiality into act" (brought into physical reality). Therefore, Moses called this matter—while it was still unformed—the <term>abyss<note>original: "abyssus"</note></term>, recording that darkness was upon the face of the abyss. But as soon as the Spirit of the Lord was said to move over the waters, providing them with a fiery vigor, the darkness gradually departed. That which was previously called the abyss because of its gloom, now beginning to take form, claimed for itself the name of "waters." Indeed, it appeared to represent the likeness of water before it was refined into spirit through the process of <term>subtiliation<note>The process of making a substance more refined, thin, or spiritual.</note></term>. Even the divine <term>Trismegistus<note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the Hermetic writings are attributed.</note></term> seems to assert this in his *Pymander*, saying: *a horrific shadow, gliding around in a slanting revolution, migrated into a moist nature*, which is to say, it was converted into water. In his sacred discourse, he says that *through divine power, an infinite shadow, water, and a thin intellectual spirit existed within chaos.* Therefore, those shadows are dispelled by the presence of a specific light; this matter is purified from its dark gloom by the clarity of that same light. It is then called "thin spirit," and from a thing once utterly hidden and covered by the sooty face of dire gloom, it is now rendered visible, pleasant, very clear, and indeed most limpid. However, it is clearer in one part than another, depending on its position—whether it is farther from or closer to the source of light. For its lower part is turbulent, becoming gross and thick because it is compacted and defiled by moist vapors and earthy exhalations, which the dark abyss vomited and breathed out from its own depths. From the violent agitation of this matter, the winds are stirred up, as are the clouds, thunder, hail, and snow. For this reason, this part of the waters is called "corporeal" and "corpulent." The middle part, however, is not entirely physical, nor plainly spiritual; rather, according to the Philosophers, it is "as if it were not a body, but already a soul," or "as if it were not a soul, but already a body." That is to say, it participates in both extremes. Because the luminous soul, which possesses the upper part and renders it entirely formal, is distinct from its opposite (yet corporeal creatures cannot live without it), the Creator ordained this "middle spirit" for a purpose beyond the mere separation of the waters from the waters. It serves as a receptacle and a vehicle for the soul, through which the soul may move toward lower things, so that it may be further introduced into bodies. Thus, by means of this vehicle, the soul may manifest its powers to the members of those bodies. And this is why the Philosophers call it the "Spirit of the World," and <term>Albertus<note>Likely Albertus Magnus, the 13th-century philosopher and alchemist.</note></term> calls it the <term>Mercurial Spirit<note>original: "spiritum Mercurialem" — in alchemy, Mercury represents the principle of mediation and fluidity.</note></term>, because it has the power of penetrating and altering bodies. Likewise, the Greeks name it "air" and "ether," as if it were a "burning spirit," as if they were saying:

<sig>E 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: spirit-</meta>

<summary>This chapter explains the transition of "prime matter" from a dark, potential state (the Abyss) into a visible, formed state (the Waters) through the action of divine light. It describes a hierarchy of substance, from thick corporeal matter to a "middle spirit" that acts as a bridge between the soul and the physical body.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how prime matter transitions from a state of potentiality (darkness) into a state of actual existence (light). It focuses on the hierarchy of the cosmos—from the highest spiritual realms to the lower physical elements—and how the "fiery soul" descends through these layers.</meta>

<header>TREATISE I. BOOK I.</header>

...a spiritual receptacle and container of the fiery, bright, and clear soul descending from the <term>Empyrean heaven<note>The highest part of heaven in ancient cosmology, believed to be filled with pure light and fire.</note></term>: wherefore in this spirit or <term>quintessence<note>original: "quinta essentia"; the 'fifth essence' or purest element that permeates the universe.</note></term> dwells every generative and seminal power. Finally, the highest part of this spiritual matter has been so purified and made spiritual by the newly created light and its inexplicable splendor—and as it were so intoxicated by its bright and illuminated nectar—that almost its entire substance is nothing other than pure essence-making form (especially regarding its relation to lower things), by which the other dark parts of <term>hyle<note>A Greek term used by philosophers to describe primordial, unshaped matter.</note></term> are brought from potentiality into act. 

If any spark of this formal mass should descend into its middle region, it is immediately received by the middle spirit (namely, the <term>ethereal spirit<note>A subtle, celestial substance thought to fill the space between the heavens and the earth.</note></term>), and wrapped in its slender little body; and then both together—that is, the vehicle and that which is carried—descending downward, are enclosed within the body of the lower region, just as the soul itself descended in the middle spirit, as will be more fully explained at the end of the second book of this treatise. 

From these things, therefore, it is clear that darkness relates to matter in potentiality just as light relates to matter in act, and that matter not yet brought into act is like a certain substance overshadowed and hidden by the gloom of darkness: that is, it sits between "something" and "nothing," a being barely imaginable because it is unformed and unknown from the start. However, by the presence of light, it is clarified, informed, and made manifest; and it is known to exist afterwards in the form of "thin waters," according to <term>Thales<note>An early Greek philosopher who argued that water was the first principle of all things.</note></term>, <term>Hesiod<note>An early Greek poet who described the origin of the gods and the world.</note></term>, **Moses**, and the **Apostle Peter**. The lower region of these waters is called air, containing water which is called "thick air" and the spheres of the upper sublunary elements; the middle region is called ether and the middle spirit; and the highest 

->is called the dwelling place of light and fiery love, as we shall<-
->demonstrate more extensively in its proper place below.<-

<summary>This page describes the cosmology of light and matter, explaining how the 'fiery soul' descends from the highest heavens through the middle ether to the lower physical world. It aligns classical philosophy (Thales, Hesiod) with biblical tradition (Moses, Peter) to define the structure of the universe.</summary>

<keywords>prime matter, quintessence, hyle, Empyrean, ether, Thales, Hesiod, Moses, potentiality vs act, fiery soul</keywords>

## CHAPTER IX.

->*That the universal substance of the heavens, both spiritual and corporeal, is either an element or composed of elements.*<-

Therefore, following the completion of this <term>divine workmanship<note>original: "divini opificii"; referring to the act of creation by God as the Great Architect or Artisan.</note></term>, every portion of substance within any of its regions is found to be either an element or an <term>elementated thing<note>original: "elementatum"; a technical term in alchemy and natural philosophy for a complex body composed of simpler elements.</note></term>. Of these, that which is called an "element" is the first, smallest, and simplest part, from which the elementated substance of any heaven is immediately formed. Because the substance of any given heaven is considered thicker, less pure, and more imperfect depending on the greater or lesser presence of light, the "simplicity" of an element is understood either in an absolute sense—pertaining only to the elements of the spiritual heaven—or it is considered relatively. For if the components of the lower heaven are compared to those [of the spiritual heaven]...

<sig>E 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: simple</meta>

<summary>This page defines the basic structural components of the universe, distinguishing between "elements" as the simplest units and "elementated" things as complex bodies. It explains that the purity and simplicity of these substances are determined by their proximity to divine light.</summary>

<keywords>Macrocosm, divine workmanship, element, elementated substance, spiritual heaven, simplicity of matter, light and darkness</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of elements from Chapter IX, specifically how the "simplicity" and "purity" of an element are relative terms depending on which "heaven" or level of reality they occupy.</meta>

...are called simple and pure; but in respect to the higher realm, these elements are judged to be dense and material, and consequently less pure. For the sacred writers confess that the elements of the spiritual heaven—owing to their inexplicable simplicity, spiritual purity, and ineffable virtues—exist not only in the blessed intelligences and angels who frequent that mansion, but even in God himself, the ordainer and patron of the entire structure. 

In the angels, there is a stability of essence and an "earthly" force, which are the firm seats of God; there is also in the angels clemency and piety, which is a "watery" and cleansing virtue; furthermore, there is in them a subtle and "airy" spirit, which is why in Holy Scripture they are called the "feathers" or "wings of the winds"; finally, there is in them love, which is a most splendid "fire." And it is from this that the Royal Psalmist speaks: 

->*“Who makes your angels spirits, and your ministers a burning fire.”* <note>original: "Qui facis angelos tuos Spiritus, & ministros tuos ignem ardentem." A reference to Psalm 104:4.</note><-

Furthermore, the Seraphim, Virtues, and Powers are fiery; the Cherubim are earthly; the Thrones and Archangels are aquatic; while the Dominations and Principalities are airy. Finally, of the <term>Archetype<note>The "Archetype" refers to God as the original model or pattern upon which the entire universe is built.</note></term> itself, it is said: 

->*“Let the earth be opened and bring forth the Savior.”* <note>original: "Aperiatur terra & germinet Salvatorem." A reference to Isaiah 45:8.</note><-

Similarly, He is called the "fount of *living* water," which purifies and regenerates. He is also called the "Spirit of life," the breathing breath; and finally, according to Moses and Saint Paul, He is a "consuming fire." 

In the same way, the sphere of the Trinity is undoubtedly the brightest and most subtle part of that spiritual heaven, since it is the mansion of the Trinity, to which the Seraphim are closest. The throne or foundation of this, however, is the firmament or "crystalline sphere," which Saint John called the *sea of glass*, by which he denoted something earthly, subtle, and watery; by carefully considering these extremes, the intermediate natures are very easily understood. 

Elements are also found everywhere in the ethereal heaven, the mass of which is called the <term>fifth essence<note>original: "quinta essentia"; the "quintessence," a celestial substance distinct from the four terrestrial elements, thought to be incorruptible.</note></term>, because they are exalted above those four elements of the lower heaven in dignity, subtlety, and location. For in this region there is the solidity of earth without the thickness of water, the agility of air far removed from flowing away, and the heat of fire—incombustible, incorruptible, preservative, and bright—giving life to all the bodies of the lower heaven by its warmth, since it is derived immediately from the spiritual heaven. 

The creatures of this region also participate in its elements, since its stars, both fixed and wandering <note>The "wandering stars" refer to the planets.</note>, claim for themselves more eminent dispositions from them. Thus, among the planets, *Mars* and the *Sun*, and among the fixed stars, *Aries* and *Leo* are called fiery constellations because of their heat joined with dryness. *Jupiter* and *Venus*, *Gemini* and *Libra* are called airy, because they retain hot and moist natures. *Saturn* and *Capricorn* are called earthly, from their melancholic disposition. Finally, the *Moon* is recognized to have an aquatic faculty, as she is called the "mistress of humors" by the Philosophers; and so too among the signs of the Zodiac are *Cancer* and *Pisces*. 

It is for this reason that astrologers first observed the "triplicity" <note>In astrology, a "triplicity" is a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element.</note> of this heaven, without the observation of which they can accomplish nothing in their art. Through the qualities of these elements, each planet produces various effects upon these lower realms, as we shall declare below in the proper place. 

Finally, that the elements, along with the "elementated" things, occupy the lowest space of the world, can be hidden from no one, not even the ignorant. But these are more impure, grosser, more subject to our senses, and more liable to corruption, being far more unworthy and simple than the other higher things. And by the same reasoning, creatures formed from the composition of these are known to obtain their temperaments from the balance of those elements. For the powers of herbs and minerals are described and distinguished by heat, coldness, moisture, and dryness, according to the dominance of the elements within them. 

Therefore, no one can deny that elements are found everywhere and in all regions of the world, but in different proportions according to the lack or abundance of the "universal form." In the lower region, they are dreg-like and thick; in the middle part, they are purer and cleaner; and at the summit or "super-celestial" part, they are most spiritual and in every way blessed. From these things, it is understood that the elements in the Archetype are the *ideas* of things to be produced; in the Intelligences <note>The "Intelligences" refer to the angelic minds that govern the celestial spheres.</note>, they are distributed powers; and in the ethereal heaven...

virtues <note>catchword</note>

<summary>This page explains how the four elements (fire, air, water, earth) exist across all levels of the cosmos, from the spiritual nature of God and the angels to the astrological qualities of the planets and the physical properties of minerals.</summary>
<keywords>elements, celestial hierarchy, astrology, quintessence, Archetype, planetary qualities, spiritual heaven, crystalline sphere, triplicity, four humors</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter IX regarding the composition of elements. The previous page established that even spiritual beings like angels possess "elemental" qualities (fiery love, airy spirit, etc.) in a refined state, and that God himself is the "Archetype" or original pattern for these elements.</meta>

...virtues, and in the lowest region, denser bodies imbued with coarser forms. We conclude, therefore, according to our intention, that the matter of an element is nothing other than a certain simple portion of the first matter of the superior sphere, embraced and now brought into act through the presence of clarity <note>The author uses "clarity" (claritas) to refer to the presence of divine light which gives form to raw matter.</note>; which portions are distinguished from one another all the more as they are found to be richer in essential form. For, as we said above, the nature of light is to purify, to make subtle, and to sublimate to the summit of the superior heaven, if possible, since it naturally tends upward and intends to carry with it the substance it inhabits as much as it can—that is, to make it more worthy—until it has led it to the height of perfection. For then this nature is said to rest. Of these portions, I say, in any given heaven there are four kinds and no more; for that portion of the matter of any heaven which has more essential form (that is, light) and less matter is called **fire**; whereas that which has less form and more matter is called **earth**. From the mixture of these portions the intermediate elements are produced, of which one participates more in earth, namely **water** (since both are cold, because they have less form and more matter), while the other participates more in fire, namely **air** (since both are hot, and accordingly have more form and less matter). From which it is manifest that the elements are made from the varying action and passion of simple natures, since from them every portion of matter called an "element" is derived and distinguished.

# CHAPTER X.

## Concerning Chaos, and the principles of the creatures of the lowest heaven.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'M' with ornate floral and foliage patterns within a square border.</image-desc>
That the <term>prime matter<note>The fundamental, undifferentiated substance from which all physical things are created.</note></term> of things is manifold is manifest both from what precedes and what follows. Of these, that which we called <term>Hyle<note>original: "Hylen." A Greek term (ὕλη) used in philosophy to denote the primordial, formless matter of the universe.</note></term> above is the most remote from composition. The second is the matter of the elements themselves, distinguished by the reason of the predominating cold or heat, and called by various names because of its various locations and operations. The third is <term>Chaos<note>In this context, Chaos is not mere disorder, but a "confused mass"—a primal state where the elements are mixed together before being separated into their proper layers.</note></term> or the confused matter and undigested mass, in which all things—namely, the four elements—were confused and mixed together, because from these, earth and water (which are heavier than the others) rose upward as far as the sphere of the moon; while fire and air, being lighter, descended down to the center of the earth. 

And this, according to some, was that formless mass of the matter of heaven and earth gaping from the beginning, from which all things afterwards came forth one by one through various species and their own proper forms. For without doubt this formless mass on the first day of creation, in both the lower heavens, was reverberated under a veil of blackness by the light acting upon it; and on the second day it was compressed into the lowest heaven, which we can more rightly define as a formless mass of darkness or of waters lurking in the darkness, supplying the matter for the entire workmanship, with a small portion of the bright rays of each heaven retained in a dark prison by the viscosity of said waters. These portions of light, however, could form nothing without the aid of the perennial fountain of light (which they received gradually on the third, fourth, fifth, and sixth days—that is, more and more—by the divine will of the Creator). For as yet nothing held its proper place; for the portion of light due to the <term>Empyrean heaven<note>The highest, most spiritual part of heaven, associated with pure light and the presence of God.</note></term> possessed the center of the dark mass against its nature, and that other portion which was proper to the middle heaven was higher up—that is, above the imprisoned light of the Empyrean heaven—towards the con-

<meta>catchword: As</meta>

...the hollow surface of its region was retained in that undigested mass <note>original: "mole illa indigesta"—a reference to Ovid’s description of Chaos in the Metamorphoses</note>, so that, contrary to the order of nature, the denser and heavier portion of light occupied the higher place, while the more subtle and lighter portion occupied the lowest parts. For that reason, because of this inverted position and arrangement, plant life could not yet be formed <note>The author uses "informari" to mean the process of giving "form" or life to raw matter.</note> by the light of the middle heaven. This, finally, is that matter about which both ancient and **chemical** <note>original: "Chimici"—referring to alchemists</note> and modern philosophers, as well as poets, have sung and written in their books; it is the matter from which they insisted various natures must necessarily be drawn, both for the perfection of the *macrocosm* <term>Macrocosm: The "Great World" or the universe as a whole, often viewed as a reflection of the human "microcosm."</term> and for the completion of the **Philosophical Work** <note>The "Philosophical Work" (Opus Philosophicum) refers to the alchemical quest to refine matter and create the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. 

But the fourth and final *first matter* <term>Materia prima: The primordial, formless substance from which all physical things are created.</term> is what the Philosophers have called that which is closer to physical bodies, and from which the compounds of the lowest heaven are immediately generated and multiplied. In animals, this is recognized as **sperm**; in plants, as **seeds**; and in minerals, as **sulfur** and **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum vivum"—literally "living silver."</note>. We shall deal with these more extensively below. 

Moreover, we describe the **Philosophical Chaos** according to the common concept of these thinkers as a state where "hot things fought with cold, and moist with dry" <note>This is a classical description of Chaos, where the four primary qualities—heat, cold, moisture, and dryness—are in constant conflict before being balanced into elements.</note>.

### TREATISE I. BOOK I. ###

->Our<- <note>original: "Nostræ"</note>

Although we ourselves have been eyewitnesses to the fact that, in the anatomization <term>Anatomisatio: A term used here to mean the chemical or philosophical dissection of a substance into its core components.</term> of any part of that mass (for according to *Aegidius de Vadis* <note>Giles de Vadis, a medieval alchemist known for his works on the transmutation of nature.</note>, only a certain part of that matter remains in the world, well enough known to all), we can sketch out a better demonstration of this matter. For, in the outer part of that same mass, I clearly saw at first glance at the home of a certain friend of mine a foul, very black, and heavy substance of earth. By its blackness and horrendous veil, all the remaining elements—indeed, even the substance of the Sun and the sky itself—were so obscured that no natural order could be found within them. Indeed, I discovered everything there placed contrary to the laws of nature; for I perceived that those things which were light were depressed toward the center, while those that were heavy resided near the periphery of the mass.

Indeed, by anatomizing this deformed body with nature's most flaming knife—namely, fire—I saw fluid water touching this deformed and utterly black crust of earth appearing on the outer part of the mass. Thus, that middle part of the water, being more involved and joined with this earthy nature, flowed out first of all, appearing clear and raw according to that earthy coldness which it held within itself. But I perceived another part of that element, closer to the fire and the center of the mass and everywhere tinted and mixed with the spirit of air, distilling after that raw nature. Thus, I observed the air to be equally mixed with the water in the lower part of the aqueous sphere, that is, tending more toward the center of the mass. Yet, the moisture of this kind of air was not free from the rebellious dryness of the black earth; for this earth had spread its power not only at the periphery of the mass (where it held its chief and more royal seat in the confusion), but also throughout the entire mass, dispersing its vapors through it, so that no part of the whole mass existed pure and free from that darkest pollution.

After the airy water, fire rose up from the abyss of that chaos, foul and ill-smelling because of the terrestrial pollution. From this, it is certain that this fire had a continuous war and conflict with that part of the aqueous substance which was imbued with air; for while that fire naturally sought and attempted to be refreshed by its airy food <note>Fire was traditionally thought to "feed" on air to maintain its flame.</note>, it repeatedly experienced the constant injuries of that hostile water mixed with the said air. 

But from the middle and innermost center of the entire unformed mass, as if from the very point of the abyss, I saw the solar substance of the mass rise—wretchedly, as if from purgatory or rising from the dead—still laboring under the impurity of the mass and appearing as if sick. I watched it be refined <note>original: "sublimari"</note> by a marvelous operation of wit and philosophical industry. And some Philosophers have called this dark part of the mass the **Central Sun** <term>Central Sun: In alchemical cosmology, a hidden source of light and life trapped within the "darkness" of matter, which must be liberated through the Work.</term>. Indeed, by its operation, we are taught in what manner at the beginning of the world our celestial Sun was exalted to its incorruptible place—that is, into the heart of the ethereal sky—after a purification was first made in this lower place, just as we shall demonstrate more clearly in Book 4 of this treatise, in the chapter on the rising of the Sun.

Once these precious gems had been extracted from that blackest mass, and snatched away as if by violence, I found a certain dreggy and very black matter left at the bottom, whose blackness was manifest, but whose redness was hidden, having a metallic nature. This, like **Lucifer** <note>Meaning "Light-Bringer"; here it refers both to the fallen angel and the alchemical "scoria" or dregs that tried to usurp the place of light.</note>, fallen from heaven, was cast down to the lowest abyss, because it had even striven to preside and predominate over celestial matter.

<summary>The author describes a first-hand alchemical experiment where the "primordial mass" is separated by fire, revealing a chaotic mixture of elements and a "Central Sun" buried within the black earthy dregs. This process serves as a microcosm for the biblical creation and the ordering of the heavens.</summary>

# BOOK THE SECOND.

## On the Structure of the Macrocosm. <term>Macrocosm: From the Greek *makros* (large) and *kosmos* (world), this refers to the universe as a whole, which Fludd views as a giant living reflection of God and a counterpart to the "microcosm" of man.</term>

### Contents of the second book.

<image-desc>Hierarchical diagram of the regions of the Macrocosm.</image-desc>

There are three regions of the Macrocosm, namely:

*   **The Supreme**, in which two things are to be noted, specifically its:
    *   Three parts, namely the heavens:
        *   Of the Trinity without limit, concerning which see chapters 2 & 3.
        *   The Empyrean <note>The Empyrean was traditionally considered the highest heaven, occupied by the purest element of fire or light.</note>, concerning which see chapter 3.
        *   The Crystalline, concerning which see chapter 4.
    *   Constitution, for it is fashioned from:
        *   Essence-forming light <note>original: "Luce essentifica"—light that bestows existence or essence.</note>, and the most simple of all,
        *   Spirit, most pure, most thin, and incomprehensible, } concerning which see chapter 5.

*   **The Middle**, called the Ether, concerning which see chapters 6, 7, & 8, whose:
    *   Eight parts are the seats of the stars:
        *   The Fixed stars,
        *   The Wandering stars <note>The planets, which were observed to "wander" across the sky compared to the fixed backdrop of the stars.</note>,
    *   Constitution, which is from:
        *   Substantial light of a middle grade,
        *   Spirit neither too coarse nor too subtle: Concerning which see chapters 6, 7, 8, & 9.

*   **The Lowest**, concerning which see chapter 10. In which two things are observed:
    *   Three parts, of which:
        *   The Extremes, namely:
            *   The Upper, which is the tabernacle of fire, concerning which see chapter 11.
            *   The Lower, which is the seat of the earth, concerning which see chapter 12.
        *   The Middle is called the sphere of humidity, divided into the regions of:
            *   Air
            *   Water } concerning which see chapter 13.
    *   Constitution, toward which two things concur:
        *   Light of the third degree and the coarsest of all,
        *   Spirit, the thickest of all and full of dregs <note>original: "fæculentus"—referring to the sediment or "feces" of the primordial matter that settled at the bottom of the universe.</note>, } concerning which see chapters 14 & 15.

# CHAPTER I.

## On the world and its division

<image-desc>A large ornamental drop cap 'M' within a square border. The letter is highly decorative, intertwined with leafy vines and architectural scrolls against a hatched background.</image-desc>

MARTIANUS <note>Martianus Capella (5th century AD), a writer whose work "The Marriage of Philology and Mercury" was a standard textbook for the seven liberal arts in the Middle Ages.</note> understands the term "world" in a threefold way: namely, for that which is called the Archetype <note>The "Archetype" refers to the original model or "blueprint" of the universe existing in the mind of God.</note>, whose substance is incorporeal, invisible, intellectual, and eternal, according to whose example and divine image the beauty and form of the real world was fashioned, as *Boethius* <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), a Roman philosopher whose "Consolation of Philosophy" deeply influenced medieval thought.</note> testifies; and this world remains permanent in the divine mind. Or, he takes it for the corporeal heaven, which is that "greater world" <term>Macrocosm: the universe as a whole.</term> which is bounded and contained by the concave surface of the First Mover <note>The "Primum Mobile" or First Mover was the outermost sphere in the geocentric model of the universe, which gave motion to all other spheres.</note>; and this world is also eternal by the will of GOD. Or finally, he takes it for man, who is called the "lesser world" <term>Microcosm: man, viewed as a miniature reflection of the entire universe.</term>, and is called perpetual in respect to his form, but corruptible in respect to his matter. We, however, not departing much from the opinion of *Martianus*, shall treat of a twofold world; namely, of the Macrocosm and the Microcosm, distinguishing the former from man (or the Microcosm) by regarding that whole space of primary matter as the world, cosmos, or macrocosm, which the spiritual light or the Spirit of the Lord, encircling the waters, encompasses in its circular embrace.

This circular portion of the substance of the abyss, distinguished into three parts differing in their formal nature by a distribution of various concurrences of light and darkness—that is, through degrees of purity and impurity—is divided as follows: The superior part is that space of the world in which the fiery spirit was carried, and the primary substance of light is contained, extending itself from the concave extremity of the sphere of the Trinity all the way to the convex surface of the starry heaven. And indeed, the substance of this part of the world is so subtle and pure on account of the abundance of its form, that in respect to us it is considered insensible and entirely imperceptible; hence the Philosophers called it intellectual and spiritual in the highest degree. The middle part is decorated with the fixed and wandering stars <note>The "wandering stars" refer to the planets.</note>; for it possesses that entire concavity of the universe which is contained between the convex surface of the orb of the moon and the concave of the First Mover; and the substance of this region is corporeal in respect to the superior part. Finally, the inferior part of the world is all that space which the concave surface of the orb of the Moon embraces. From this it becomes clear that the mass of the world is distinguished into two principal parts: one of which is incorporeal, spiritual, most pure, and most subtle, namely the superior; while the other is corporeal. And this corporeal part is again subdivided into two parts: one of which is subtle, thin, and incorruptible, namely the middle part; the other indeed is impure, gross, and subject to corruption; and this is the lowest portion of the world, which we shall hereafter call the sublunary and elemental region. Both the ancient Philosophers and the sacred Writers called these regions of the Macrocosm "Heavens." For they named the superior region the Empyrean heaven <note>From the Greek "pyr" (fire), the Empyrean was the highest heaven, composed of pure light or fire.</note>, and as it were, fire, because it is filled on every side with spiritual fire or the substance of light. And this was that third heaven into which *Saint Paul* confessed himself to have been snatched in the Spirit, which he himself called Para-

TREATISE I. BOOK II.

...named it. <note>Completing the verb *appellavit* from the previous page.</note> However, all writers generally refer to the middle part as the **ethereal heaven**, which is, as it were, luminous; they have called this "heaven" in its own right. Finally, the Apostle *Matthew* seems to call that entire airy mass between the ether and the earth "heaven" when he speaks these words: *The birds of the heaven build their nests, etc.* <note>original: "Volatilia cœli nidos suos condunt, &c." Refers to Matthew 8:20 or 13:32.</note> But since the refinement of earth is water, and the refinement of water is air, and the refinement of air is fire (for there is a rotation of one element into another), it is necessary that this entire region also be called "heaven." For Saint *Peter* confesses that the matter of the firmament and the earth is the same. <note>Referring to 2 Peter 3, where the heavens and earth are described as being formed of the same word and stored up for fire.</note> *Ezekiel* also describes the natures and locations of these three heavens in his vision expressed in *chapter 1*: for there, a great cloud surrounded by fire describes two heavens—namely, one above the firmament and another below. The lower heaven, however, is all that space delineated beneath the cloud. From these things, therefore, it is clearly evident that the entire substance of the world is nothing other than these three heavens, differing in <term>subtlety: the fineness, thinness, or spiritual quality of matter</term> and <term>thickness: the density, heaviness, or material quality of matter</term>—that is to say, differing in their purity and impurity.

# ON THE STRUCTURE OF THE MACROCOSM.

## CHAPTER II.

->*That GOD, before the creation of the world, shone only for himself: On the*<-
->*gift of love, which GOD gave to that part of the primal matter [hyle] which forms the*<-
->*world: From whence comes the spherical shape of the world?*<-

<image-desc>A large, decorative historiated woodcut initial 'M' depicting a landscape scene with figures and classical architecture within the frame of the letter.</image-desc>

THE **MONAD** <note>The One, or the supreme Unity of God.</note> according to Hermes Trismegistus (who is the light of the world) brought forth the monad, that is, the GOD of fire, before the ages, and reflected his own heat back into himself. For this Father of Lights, before the creation of the world, shone only within himself, and did not wish to reveal his brightness until he meditated upon the creation of the world. (Psalm 18, verse 11: *He made darkness his secret place; his tabernacle around him was dark waters and thick clouds.* <note>original: "Fecit tenebras locum suum secretum, tabernaculum suum aquis obscuris fecit, nubibusq; densis obvelavit."</note> From this, the portion of <term>hyle: the primordial, unformed matter of the universe</term> outside the world is signified: *Clouds and darkness are round about him.* Psalm 97, verse 2.) For then, says Hermes Trismegistus, he changed his form—that is, he manifested his hidden essence, by which he suddenly revealed all things. (Psalm 18, verse 12: *At the brightness that was before him, the thick clouds and dark water passed away.* <note>original: "splendore ejus præsentia nubes & aqua tenebrosa fugerunt"</note>) Therefore, this Almighty GOD, freely and not by compulsion, determined in his ineffable wisdom to build the Macrocosm, and before its construction, to mystically determine its matter, essence, figure, dimension, and the connection of its parts. He chose matter for this structure from the womb of infinite <term>hyle</term>, choosing that which was fit to be adorned with various virtues and essential forms. He wished to freely impart to it a holy and amatory gift, proceeding immediately from himself. For in the act before creation, GOD breathed, and the Spirit of his mouth—that is, the passive breathing proceeding from him by way of a gift of love—was borne over the waters. This means he moved over the watery sphere, or rather moved around and over the entire corporal matter of the world, namely over the two lower heavens as yet unformed, whose primordial matter was water, as Saint Peter testifies (2 Peter 3). I say he was borne, without doubt, in a circular motion in the world through the spiritual presence of that same Spirit acting upon the gathered surface of the material world. This motion is the most noble among all types, through which the material of the bodily world and its simplest spiritual substance is distinguished from that mass of infinite <term>hyle</term> or unformed heap thrust outside either world. Thus, the corporal world received its convex limits or boundaries, and its shape—the most capacious and noble of all—namely, the spherical shape. Indeed, this is that sublime gift of the divine essence which GOD gave to it, having a most full aptitude by the power of its procession to be given, and proceeding only by way of a gift of love, in which all other things are given. For all common gifts are said to be given by reason of the love of that Holy Spirit, which is infinite love proceeding from the Father and the Word. By his simple act, therefore, all virtues and essences are distributed to every part of the world as gifts, according to the command of the divine will and the sentiment of the Royal Psalmist: *By the word of the Lord the heavens were made firm, and all their power by the spirit of his mouth.* <note>original: "Verbo Domini firmati sunt cœli, & spiritu oris illius omnis virtus eorum." Referring to Psalm 33:6.</note> Saint Paul also seems to confirm this same thing: *Through the Holy Spirit,* he says, *all things are searched out and each is distinguished by its own degree.* This Spirit of the Lord, therefore, which is a fiery love, was borne over the waters, imparting to them a fiery vigor as the divine will so commanded. From this, the first creature of the world—the most subtle, simple, noble, and active of all—first shone upon the world. Even the Pagans recognized this Spirit, for the *Chaldean Oracles* <note>A collection of mystical texts from the 2nd century AD, often cited by Renaissance Neoplatonists.</note> called it the "fiery love" proceeding from the Father and the Word.

<summary>This page explains that before creation, God existed in hidden self-reflection, eventually manifesting his essence through a 'gift of love' (the Holy Spirit). This divine breath moved in a circular motion, shaping the unformed primordial matter (hyle) into the most perfect and noble shape: a sphere.</summary>

<keywords>Monad, Hermes Trismegistus, Hyle, Primal Matter, Holy Spirit, Fiery Love, Circular Motion, Spherical Shape, Creation, Chaldean Oracles, Macrocosm</keywords>

### TREATISE I. BOOK II.

<meta>catchword: CA-</meta>

...proceeding, as **Iamblichus** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote on the mysteries of the Egyptians and the nature of the divine.</note> testifies; **Hermes Trismegistus** calls Him the GOD of fire and the divinity of the Spirit. The Gentiles, as is read in the works of **Plutarch**, described Him as the intellectual and fiery Spirit itself, having no form of His own, but transforming Himself into whatever He wishes and making Himself equal to all things. Speaking of Him, **Zoroaster** and **Heraclitus** maintained that all things were begotten from a single fire. 

Hence also **Orpheus** said that the middle heaven, illuminated by the light of this fiery Spirit, was called the **ether** —as if it were *aithaēr* <note>original: "æthaërem," a play on the Greek 'aithein' meaning to burn or kindle.</note>, that is, a "burning spirit"—and he called it <term>pyripnon: a Greek-derived term meaning 'fiery breath' or 'fire-breathing'</term>, as was said before. The **Platonists** called Him the **soul of the world** <note>original: "animam mundi," the vital force that the Platonists believed animated the entire universe.</note>, and they recognized light alone as being closest to it in nobility, saying that there is nothing within the circle of the firmament besides the soul that bears a greater resemblance to GOD than light itself. Indeed, some more recent philosophers claim that any given thing may possess as much divinity as it is capable of receiving light. 

And this is no wonder, since GOD himself, as is passed down in the Holy Scriptures, calls himself the "Light of the World" and the "Father of Lights." By His holy gift or fiery spirit—which appeared to the Apostles in the form of fire—worldly light (that is, the fiery vigor of the waters) was honored on the first day of creation. It was from His essential substance that the heaven in which the spirit moved, as well as both the lower heavens and their creatures, received their beauty through divine and metaphysical means. 

And hence it was that **Democritus**, **Orpheus**, and many of the **Pythagoreans**—because they perceived the power of this divine nature to be present in all lower creatures to a greater or lesser degree—believed that "all things are full of gods." They established divine honors for these things, dedicating prayers and sacrifices to them, and venerating them with divine worship. Furthermore, they reduced all these gods to one, **JUPITER**; by which they most clearly signified that all the streams of heat, brightness, life, and essential forms flow from a single, supernatural, shining source, and obtain their growth and multiplication from His everlasting and spiritual water.

<summary>This page explores the ancient philosophical consensus that God is a fiery, intellectual spirit and the 'soul of the world,' manifesting physically as light. It connects classical thinkers like Orpheus and Plato with Christian scripture to argue that all life and form derive from this single divine source.</summary>

### CHAPTER III.

->*On the first three days of creation.*<-

The understanding of the arrangement of the days established before the creation of the Sun seems difficult, since among the Theological Fathers <note>The early influential teachers and scholars of the Christian Church.</note> themselves no small difference of opinion arises regarding the nature of those days. For **Basil**, **Damascene**, <note>Saint Basil the Great and Saint John of Damascus, two "Greek" Fathers who shaped early Christian cosmology.</note> and other **Greeks** maintain that the light created on the first day caused "day" through the emission of light, and "night" through its retraction, performed by the divine will. But the **Latin** Doctors...

<sig>G</sig>
<meta>catchword: Latin</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the theological debate regarding the first three days of Creation, specifically how "day" and "night" existed before the Sun was created on the fourth day.</summary>
<keywords>Macrocosm, Let there be, Creation, Genesis, Basil, Damascene, Light</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the first days of creation, transitioning from the opinions of the Greek Fathers to those of the Latin scholars, and proposing a systematic reconciliation between spiritual and physical light.</meta>

# TREATISE I. BOOK II.

The Latin scholars plausibly suggest that that light performed a circulation similar to that which is now made by the Sun, and that it illuminated the hemisphere in which it was present, and thus darkness was brought into the opposite hemisphere because of the obstruction of the grosser, thicker, and blacker matter which existed in the middle of all the unformed matter. We, however, shall put forward a twofold interpretation of those days, of which neither, perhaps, when considered somewhat more exactly, will seem to stand far from the limit of truth. The first interpretation is this: that the days before the creation of the Sun can be regarded as primary, secondary, or tertiary light extending itself through the transparent universe; in such a way, naturally, that night is understood as the dominion of darkness in that part where no light has yet shone forth. And thus the first day is taken for the brightness dispersed generally through the whole Empyrean heaven <note>The highest part of the heavens, believed in ancient cosmology to be composed of pure fire or light.</note>, namely, after that light had driven the darkness downward from its region and had purified and refined the spiritual matter of its heaven according to the proportion of its action. The morning of this day was the first penetration of divine light into its darkness, and the beginning of the action of light in the upper darkness; just as among us, that time is called morning when the rays of the Sun begin to penetrate into the obscure and dark mass of the night. Similarly, the evening is the lowest surface of this heaven, in which dark vapors still exist, as if on the surface of the middle region, and there they still revolve in an oblique revolution and terminate the action of the first day; and so it is with the others. Certainly, this exposition is not to be entirely rejected, since both day and night must be considered with respect to the subject to which the aforementioned dispositions of the two times are applied. Yet the Empyrean heaven, after its creation, was entirely bright and clear; for which reason it received its name from fire <note>The word "Empyrean" derives from the Greek 'pyros', meaning fire.</note>, and therefore neither night nor darkness was tolerated in it in any way immediately after its creation. And this same thing can be said of the substance of the ethereal heaven.

However, our second interpretation, which seems to us closer to the truth, as it agrees directly with the Latin Theologians, will clarify itself from the following declaration. For it is probable that the Divine Spirit, sent forth and borne over the waters before any creation, circled them with a triple revolution, and each of its courses produced a spiritual or supernatural day. Thus, by its first circuit, it established the first day, in which it perfected the Empyrean heaven and drove the darkness downward by one degree. By the second revolution, it brought forth the second day, in which, by the double presence of divine power, the darkness was depressed by a second degree; and in this way, the divine light and power shone forth in the middle and ethereal region of the world. Finally, by its third and last revolution, the darkness was dispelled by the power of the third divine impression through a third degree and to the final limit from the circumference. And so the tabernacle of the entire world was completed, and all the powers and essences of its regions were produced by the power of the Divine Spirit. Here, therefore, were finished those three spiritual days, measured by the act of the Divine Spirit in no other way [than by his movement], and related to the number of persons of the Trinity, all of whom acted toward the creation of the world. *For God the Father spoke; and by his Word the heavens were made firm; and by the Spirit from his mouth all their power.* <note>original: "Nam Deus Pater dixit; & Verbo ejus firmati sunt cœli; ac Spiritu ab ore ejus omnis virtus eorum." This is a paraphrase of Psalm 33:6.</note> Therefore, in the space of these days, and after their completion, the heavens were produced and given essence by divine operation, in which the entire Spirit of the macrocosm <note>The "great world" or the universe as a whole.</note> received form and being.

Soon after, the solar days, which are concerned with the more corporeal things of the world, obtained their origin; for the production of these kinds of creatures, the first three of these solar days came forth, just as three spiritual days first shone out for the fabrication of the dwellings of the three heavens. For on the first solar day, which is the fourth from the beginning of creation, the stars or wandering planets and the creatures of heaven were created; on the second, all irrational animals; and on the last day, the lesser world or Microcosm <note>The "small world," referring to humanity as a reflection of the universe.</note> was created and completed; in the same way that the Macrocosm was brought to its perfection on the last spiritual day. Who, therefore, can almost doubt,

<meta>catchword: but [that]</meta>

<summary>The author describes two ways to understand the first three days of creation before the Sun existed: one as a gradual penetration of light into matter, and the preferred second view as three spiritual revolutions of the Divine Spirit corresponding to the Trinity.</summary>
<keywords>Creation, Empyrean, Trinity, Macrocosm, Microcosm, Ethereal Heaven, Divine Spirit, Genesis, Solar Days, Spiritual Days</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the Sun acting as a physical representative of the Divine Spirit's initial movements. It bridges the gap between the purely spiritual events of the first three days and the physical rotation of the solar body established on the fourth day.</meta>

# ON THE STRUCTURE OF THE MACROCOSM.

Why should the motion of the Sun after the world's completion not be the same as the motion of the Spirit was before its perfection, since God, after the limit of the three spiritual days, established his tabernacle in the Sun itself? Therefore, through the Sun itself—as if by the only demonstration from effect to cause <note>original: "à posteriori"</note>—we can arrive at the true motion of that divine Spirit by which it moved on any given day regarding the composition of the world. For no one will doubt that the following days in creation were equal in dimension to the preceding ones, and consequently, that the divine Spirit moved in its revolution in the same time as the Sun now moves in its own. Indeed, the solar body itself seems to be, as it were, a mirror and the heart of the world, into which the supernatural rays of the Spirit shining from the Trinity are infused; by whose radiant power, beyond all human reason, that solar body is made visible, and it always maintains a constant and immutable motion by the divine will, producing days of the same length at all times as those in which creation was completed. It seems, then, that the Holy Spirit, which appeared to the Apostles in the form of a radiant and gleaming dove, circled the waters in a spiritual form, illuminating their darkness with its motion and infusing its rays into them, leaving those rays behind after its departure so that the waters might be formed by them. By its presence, it clarified and illustrated all things spiritually in an ineffable way, just as the Sun does physically by its presence. And (to move to the opinion of the Latin Theologians) that uncreated light, by infusing light into created things, performed the same circulation during the first three days of creation which the Sun thereafter completes every day by its own motion; and it illuminated its sphere in which it existed no differently than the Sun illuminates its own. Similarly, darkness was created in the opposite hemisphere because of the placement of coarser, thicker, and more opaque matter in the middle of the whole unformed mass or horrific shadow. We spoke of the motion of the divine Spirit over the waters in the preceding chapter, at the end of which we also depicted its motion on the first day of creation according to human understanding; the reasoning for that emblem we have explained near the end of this fifth chapter.

## CHAPTER IV.

->*Concerning the creation and nature of light, and its operation, and the constitution of the Empyrean heaven or the first region of the world.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative initial letter D featuring ornate scrollwork, foliage, and floral patterns.</image-desc>
The Lord said: *Let there be Light, and it was made* on the first day, the highest heaven being most full of light. For the content of this heaven is nothing other than the most essential and spiritual substance of light, composed of a large portion of soul and a tiny bit of the thinnest Spirit; which is therefore called the Fiery Spirit and the Empyrean heaven <note>The highest part of the heavens, believed in ancient cosmology to be composed of pure fire or light.</note>—that is, fiery and burning, as *Isidore* <note>St. Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), an influential scholar whose works helped preserve classical knowledge for the Middle Ages.</note> says—not because it burns like elementary fire, but because it is suffused on all sides with the splendor and clarity of light. Therefore, I do not agree with the opinions of those who thought this heaven to be immobile and at rest; because these properties are entirely contrary to the substance of light from which this heaven is composed, since light is the first and simple act, than which nothing is faster in its motion. (*His lightning illuminated the world, and the earth—that is, the darkness—seeing him, was afraid and fled:* Psalm 97, verse 4.) By the presence of this creature or fiery vigor, the darkness and gloom of that still unformed and dark upper part are driven downward through a slanted revolution, and this for two reasons: the first of which is because the hidden and potential quality of the shadow is now revealed by the fiery activity of light and takes the name of coldness; whose disposition, since it is in every way contrary to the quality of lucid heat, is to flee and turn away from the operations and object of light. It followed from this that the cruder and thicker part of the matter, because of...

<sig>G 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: predomin-</meta>

<vocab>Macrocosm, Divine Spirit, Sun, Trinity, Let there be Light, Empyrean Heaven, Isidore, Psalm 97</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the Sun as a physical mirror for the Holy Spirit's movement and begins Chapter IV, detailing the creation of the Empyrean heaven as a realm of pure, moving light.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, creation, Holy Spirit, Empyrean, solar motion, divine light, Latin theologians, Isidore of Seville</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the macrocosm's structure, moving from the physical Sun (as a representative of the Spirit) to the nature of the highest, spiritual heaven—the Empyrean. It explains the physical and metaphysical properties of this region using the concepts of "form" and "matter."</meta>

# TREATISE I. BOOK II.

...coldness predominating in it, it withdrew itself more and more toward the center of the universal globe, which is located further away from the habitation of the primary light. The second reason is that there is the same relationship between light and darkness as there is between form and privation <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "privation" is the absence of a quality that should naturally be present, like darkness being the absence of light.</note>: wherefore, avoiding the sight of light like a "horrible shadow"—to use the words of Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with a body of wisdom texts (Hermetica) combining philosophy, alchemy, and magic.</note>—it glided away in an oblique revolution and sought the center of the circumference. It is for this reason that this heaven is purified and cleansed to the highest degree, separating the <term>subtle from the dense</term> <note>original: "subtile à spisso"; a core concept in alchemy and early physics referring to the refinement of matter.</note>, and is illuminated and shines beyond measure by driving all gloom from its margin toward the center.

Therefore, this heaven, or the highest and supernatural region of the world, is the habitation of the lucid fountain and, as it were, the motherland and native soil in which the first-created light—the primordial beginning of things—was first placed. Hence it is called the "foundation of the world," by whose formal presence the appetite of the matter of this entire heaven is fulfilled to the highest degree. For it is certain that the matter of this heaven is exceedingly thin and subtle, since its entire substance is "formal." For according to Aristotle in the 4th book of his *Metaphysics*, where there is more form, there is less matter, and consequently, the thickness of matter argues for a scarcity of form. Indeed, the multitude of the lucid form, through its actions, continually thins out the purer part of the matter of the abyss—which was retained in this heaven by divine will—and extends its mass further through this thinning out <note>rarefaction</note>, just as is perceived in the chemical art <note>Alchemy</note>. There, if a dense substance in a sufficiently large glass vessel, hermetically sealed, is thinned by a more vehement fire, its thinned parts naturally seek a wider place; thus the glass is broken so that the thinned vapors may find space more freely. In the same way, the clear matter of this heaven extends itself very widely, thinned by the lovely heat of this heaven. 

And this is why, according to Basil in the 2nd book of his *Hexameron* <note>St. Basil the Great’s 4th-century series of homilies on the six days of creation.</note>, this heaven extends itself more widely than all other heavens. We conclude, therefore, that this heaven is the most noble form of all, spiritual and simple beyond all imagination, the greatest in quantity of all because of the thinning of its matter, the most lucid in quality, and most filled with fiery vigor. It is the most active in its action and the swiftest in the motion of its lucid substance, the sweetest in the heat derived from its motion, most temperate, lovely, neither burning nor destroying, but connecting all lower things in harmonious peace with proportional bonds. It preserves them in its own spirit—which can scarcely be perceived by the imagination due to its subtlety—moving and living through itself, and existing in its own natural seat without the touch of anything foreign or the impediment of any coldness. In shape it is spherical, which it received from the motion of the Spirit that "moved over the waters" and from the lucid power proceeding from it by divine will. It is called by the name **Empyrean**, meaning "fiery," as stated above. It is also called **Olympus** from its clarity. It is also called **supernatural**, because it is spiritual beyond the laws of nature as opposed to corporeality; and **intellectual**, because it is not visible to us, but only intelligible, and perceivable only by demonstration from effect to cause <note>original: "à posteriori"</note>, or because it is most full of angels and intelligences.

### CHAPTER V.

->*On the base of the spiritual heaven, its composition and nature, and the utility of its position.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial Q with floral motifs and foliage contained within a square frame.</image-desc>
