# ON THE FABRIC OF THE MACROCOSM.
->G 3<-
<meta>catchword: of heaven</meta>

...most like crystal; by which name he does not seem to mean the entire space between the sphere of the moon and the concavity of the crystalline sphere—where the waters are separated from the waters, as is written in Moses <note>A reference to Genesis 1:6–7, where God divides the waters "above the firmament" from the waters "below."</note>—but rather to denote the sphere itself that is continuous with the **Empyrean heaven**. We shall demonstrate below that this very sphere is the one that others call the "starry sphere" and the **firmament** (incorrectly distinguishing them as two separate things). For this sphere is of the most noble condition in its kind, because it shares in the nature of the clarity and thinness of the Empyrean heaven, of which it is the base. Hence, it is introduced by both the Prophet and the Gospel in their spiritual visions when explaining the glory of God, and it is placed under the likeness of a throne or seat, existing as a medium between the nature of spirituality and corporality. Around its outer (convex) surface, it shares more in the nature of the spiritual heaven, but in its inner (concave) surface, it is more corporeal in its intervals, as will be explained in the discussion of its simple composition, or the arrangement and assimilation of its parts. 

For just as the thicker part of the elements of the lowest heaven resides tangibly in an earthly disposition, so too must it be conceived intellectually that the denser part of the most translucent spiritual matter of heaven—already purged of impurity and darkness by the presence of light—is driven toward the base of this heaven by the action of benevolent heat, though imbued with an insensible and scarcely credible coldness due to its density. For in a certain way, though lightly and gently, coldness suffers the actions of light because of its smallness, since it is a quality of matter and a "friend of darkness." From this, it is clear that the greater portion of its substance is more spiritual than corporeal. 

Similarly, it must be understood that the rays of the **primary light** <note>The original light created at the beginning of the universe.</note>—whose nature is to penetrate the dense—quickly pursued the "horrendous shadow" in the beginning, penetrating downward beyond the limits of the Empyrean heaven. Because of the heavy resistance of the dense and dark matter slowly tending downward, these rays recoiled and were reflected. By these reflections, vapors are refined and elevated as "nebulous exhalations" by the motion of the recoiling rays toward the concave surface of the watery sphere; there, they are constricted and frozen in a glandular manner by dry coldness. For the reflected light rays, exciting accidental heat by their motion, preserve and increase the benevolent cold enclosed in the <term>icy matter</term> <note>original: "materia glacialis"</note> in the concave part of the sphere by means of <term>antiperistasis</term> <note>A term from ancient physics meaning "opposition" or "counter-action"; the process where a quality (like cold) is intensified when it is surrounded by its opposite (heat).</note>. This is no different than how the solar rays, reverberated from the earth of the lowest heaven, strengthen the cold of the middle region of the air; and in the outer (convex) part of that same crystalline sphere, the heat derived essentially from the action of the first light performs the same office, just as the cold of the middle region of the air is strengthened by the power of the sphere of fire in the lowest heaven. 

Therefore, this sphere, because of the constant action of **Forms**, is as if fixed and sublimated to the highest peak of its perfection into a crystalline or "adamantine" form, embracing nebulous and dark globules. These globules have a magnetic power to attract the passing invisible rays to themselves and to eject them into the **middle world**. For Form <note>In this context, "Form" is the spiritual or organizing principle of an object, as opposed to its "Matter."</note> desires their matter no differently than a female desires a male; yet they are never subject to corruption, because there is such an abundance of Form in them that the appetite of their matter is entirely fulfilled. Nor can the presence of coldness find any putrefaction in that place, because it is "earthy"—that is, joined with the essential nature or quality of the earth, namely dryness, which is most friendly to a fiery nature. 

It was not in vain, by the decree of the Divine Will, that this crystalline and dry collection of waters was placed in this region, since its position brings great benefits and no small utility both to the spiritual heaven and to the corporeal medium. For it is not only held to be the medium by which spiritual and supernatural things are distinguished and separated from corporeal and natural ones; but it is also like a certain rare "sieve," through whose diaphragm the rays of spiritual light pass in divine proportion, are sifted, and are poured as if through a funnel into these lower regions. Furthermore, the heat from...

...the violent motion of the middle heaven is tempered by the presence of this cold sphere and made far more mild, as Bede <note>Bede the Venerable (c. 673–735), an English monk and scholar who wrote extensively on nature and time.</note> testifies. And in this way, the work of the first day was completed—namely, the **Empyrean heaven**, whose base we have said is the **crystalline heaven**. But the work of the second day was to separate this mass of spiritual waters from those lower waters, which were burdened with excessively thick matter and subject to corruption due to a lack of form; and to adorn the medium of separation—namely the **ethereal heaven**—with the most noble essences, as we shall declare below.

### Explanation of the picture representing the deformed and dark mass illuminated by uncreated light and surrounded by a circular motion of holy splendor, which is drawn at the end of the second chapter of this book.

But the holy splendor flourished, which led the elements beneath the sand and moist nature, and the heavy things submerged in darkness settled beneath the moist sand.* — **Trismegistus, Sermon 3 of the Poimandres.**

What can be said more clearly than this? Immediately after the word of the Lord, **LET IT BE**, the spirit proceeding from His mouth before the origin of the world surrounded that part of the primordial matter <term>hyle|The Greek term used by early philosophers to describe the fundamental, unformed substance of the universe.</term>—now distinguished by the name of "world"—with its circular motion. By the reflection of its immense splendor, it erased that ineffable deformity of the part of the abyss on the convex surface of the entire world (before the existence of this present world) and made the waters, both the thicker and thinner ones, originally visible, and essentially gave them form.

### Explanation of the following figure.

Their purely formal reflections, mixed with the thin upper spirit or most subtle water, give the name of **Light** to the most spiritual substance of that high region. Indeed, because of its brightness, theologians have rightly called it the **Empyrean** <note>From the Greek 'empyros,' meaning 'in or on the fire.' It was considered the highest, most spiritual part of heaven.</note> and **fiery** heaven, as was said above. TREATISE I. BOOK II.

# CHAPTER VI.

->Concerning the middle heaven and its various names, and concerning<-
->the reasoning behind them.<-

The middle region of the world, completed on the second day of creation, possesses various names because of the downward-tending operations of the matter of light. When it is considered in itself—that is, in respect to its own proper matter—it is called the **middle spirit** after the scattering of the darkness. However, in respect to the superior heaven—that is, the shining substance—and its mixing with the spirit of that realm, it is called the <term>ether<note>original: "æther"; from the Greek word for 'upper air' or 'bright sky.'</note></term>. Furthermore, insofar as it relates to the four lower elements <note>Earth, water, air, and fire.</note> appearing and distinguished on the third day, it is called the <term>Fifth Essence<note>original: "Quinta essentia"; the 'quintessence' was believed to be an incorruptible, celestial substance distinct from the four earthly elements.</note></term>. 

Considered in itself, then—that is, in its own substance revealed on the second day and cleansed of its gloom—it displays the form of the subtlest waters, and of a certain vaporous and never-resting smoke. For this reason, among the Platonists, it is called the **middle spirit of the world**, which we previously called the receptacle, bond, and vehicle of the essential light and of the **soul of the world** in Chapter 5 of Book 1, where we also demonstrated the reasoning for that name. But insofar as this spirit is the bond of the light descending from the spiritual source, by whose action life-giving and natural heat is excited, it is to that extent called ether—that is, "shining," according to *Isidore* <note>St. Isidore of Seville, an influential 7th-century encyclopedist.</note>—because there is perpetual clarity and brightness there. 

Alternatively, it is called "fiery" according to *Anaxagoras* <note>A pre-Socratic Greek philosopher.</note>, who affirmed that everything that burns is called ether, since ether is nothing other than inflamed air. Thus, according to Greek grammar, it is derived from *aithō* <note>original: "αἴθω"</note>, which means "I burn"; spirit-air as if it were *athaēr*, that is, a **burning spirit**. For this reason, *Orpheus* calls it *Pyripnon* <note>original: "Pyripnon"; from the Greek for 'fire-breathing.'</note>, that is, a **fiery breath**, and *Porphyry* <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher.</note>, along with other Platonists, called it the spirit in the midst of which they placed the **soul of the world**. 

And it is from this that, according to *Marianus* <note>Likely referring to Marcianus Capella, the late Roman author of a famous treatise on the cosmos and the liberal arts.</note>, day is perpetual in the ether, because darkness never approaches that place; although its simple substance, divided from the stellar bodies, is invisible—that is, not perceivable by mortal eyes—as we shall declare more extensively in the following chapter. Finally, it is held to be a single, unique element, just as *Aristotle* wishes in his book of *Meteorology* <note>original: "libro Meteoron"; referring to Aristotle's 'Meteorologica.'</note>, where he asserts that the ether of that place is nothing other than a unique element. Whence it is necessary that this unique element be outside the number of the four lower essences soon to be produced in the lowest region; and from this it is also called the **Fifth Essence** by the Philosophers, in which no corruption is found, since the two contraries that lead to putrefaction <note>In Aristotelian physics, corruption and decay are caused by the conflict between opposing qualities like hot/cold and moist/dry.</note> are not found in that same element. 

From the reasoning behind these names, it is therefore manifest that the matter of this region—formerly primary matter, but now made secondary through its reduction into action—is nothing other than a most pure vapor, or a certain most subtle mist, generally called **spirit**; and its form, by whose presence it is brought into action, is that light or clarity flowing essentially from the perennial source of the divine and super-celestial spirit, totally filling the spiritual appetite of this middle matter, as will be declared below.

ON THE FABRIC OF THE MACROCOSM. 57

## CHAPTER VII.

->*Why is the composition of the middle heaven called the "sphere of equality"? And why is this region called the corporeal heaven in relation to the upper one?*<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'V' with a face in the center surrounded by ornate foliage.</image-desc>

**Just as** the Empyrean heaven was created on the first day from a single thing—namely, the substance of light—which is composed of three-quarters of the thinnest fire and one-quarter of the subtlest spirit; because of this, it is entirely "formal" <note>In the philosophy of the time, "form" was the active, spiritual principle that gave shape and life to "matter," which was passive and heavy.</note> compared to the lower regions, and it is called "fiery" because fire predominates in it. In the same way, the matter of this second heaven, since it is more corporeal than that of the first and higher heaven, corresponds to its own shining form in that proper proportion by which matter is usually weighed against its form in equal scales. Therefore, it is composed of one part of fiery and thinnest light (descending from the fountain of the Empyrean heaven through the <term>crystalline sphere's diaphragm<note>original: "diaphragma"; a dividing membrane or boundary layer between the highest heaven and the middle ether.</note></term>) and an equal quantity of "primary matter" which was already then shaped and given the name of "spirit." This is also what <term>Pythagoras<note>A Greek philosopher (c. 570 – c. 495 BC) whose theories often blended mathematics with the structure of the cosmos.</note></term> seems to assert when he writes that the sun, moon, and other stars are forged from fire and air. 

Hence it is also why <term>Artephius<note>original: "Artefius"; a legendary figure in medieval alchemy and Hermetic philosophy, often cited as an authority on the longevity of life and the nature of the elements.</note></term>, with the support of the ancient philosophers, called this region the "heaven of equality," in the center of which the wise placed the "sphere of the soul." From this, it seems likely that the constitution of this heaven consists of two quarters of middle spirit and two quarters of the most beautiful, shining soul-substance—which is well-suited to satisfy the appetite of that same spiritual matter and preserve it from corruptible changes. 

That this region is more corporeal than the highest one can be proven by many reasons. One of these is that where there is less "form," there is more "matter"; but in this second heaven, there is less form than in the Empyrean heaven, whose entire substance is completely formal. Therefore, in this heaven, there is more matter than in the Empyrean heaven. The "major premise" <note>The primary statement in a logical argument or syllogism.</note> of this argument is taken from the fourth book of [Aristotle’s] *Metaphysics*. Another reason is that the mass of primary matter, before the appearance of light, always maintained one and the same state; for it lacked an "agent" <note>An active force or being that causes change in a passive substance.</note> whose actions could bring it from one disposition to another. Hence, it could be called neither thinner nor thicker, since all its potential and imaginable parts must be considered to be of the same condition, both in the region of the "potential world" and in the other. 

However, since the nature of light is such that nothing more subtle exists, and nothing penetrates and opens bodies more effectively, it must always seek to thin the parts of the thicker matter so that it can act and move through them more readily without hindrance. Thus, the more "formal" a thing is, the more subtle its substance must be. The reason for this is surely purely philosophical: because formal light moves by its own act, and by its motion it excites heat, the nature of which is to thin, dilate, and scatter. From this, it follows that the eternal spring of light created on the first day in the upper region of the world immediately revealed the hidden matter of that region and, bringing it into "act" <note>Moving from a state of "potential" to a state of "actual" existence.</note>, formed a thin and most subtle part according to its own nature. It pushed the thicker portion of that matter downward toward the center into the middle region, so that the simplest substance of spirit (which it reduced, as it were, into a universal form) could govern and carry itself through all the hidden places of the world without any obstacle. And this is why the half-corporeality of this shaped region, reflected back into the middle, [makes] the middle more corporeal...

In the Pythagorean tradition, this mathematical ratio corresponds to the musical interval of a perfect fifth.</note></term>, since matter ascending upwards and form descending downwards in this middle heaven—or <term>ether<note>original: "æthere"; the subtle, refined substance that fills the upper regions of space beyond the clouds.</note></term>—cause a consonant <term>fifth<note>original: "diapenten"; from the Greek 'dia pente', meaning 'through five'. It is one of the most stable and harmonious sounds in music.</note></term> to resonate, as it were, as we shall demonstrate more fully below. ## CHAPTER VIII.

*Concerning the reflection of the dual darknesses of the middle heaven into the lowest region of the world, and the arrangement and effect of its light—that is, the heat arising from it—and that light shines invisibly by and within itself.*

<image-desc>Large decorative drop cap V containing a human face and surrounding foliate ornamentation.</image-desc>

The power or brightness of the supernatural heaven, descending through the openings or rather the <term>breasts of the watery sieve<note>original: "cribri aquei... ubera"; This refers to the "Firmament" described in Genesis, which Fludd envisions as a celestial strainer or nourishing source that filters divine light as it passes from the Empyrean (the highest heaven) into the lower realms.</note></term> by the ordinance of the Creator, and pouring itself downward from its perennial source into the middle heaven (which was still potential and unformed), dispersed the gloom and darkness of this part of matter by its very presence. It drove those shadows toward the lowest region, which was still invisible and hidden, thereby making the substance of the middle heaven visible and formed—pure, subtle, thin, and far excelling the mass of the lower heaven in perfection and beauty. Indeed, this is that luminous essence springing from the first super-celestial source through infinite rivulets into every part of the celestial sea, which the ancients called <term>ether<note>original: "æthera"; the refined, fifth element (quintessence) that makes up the heavens, distinct from the four earthly elements.</note></term>; but we call it *Secondary Light*, because it is derived immediately from the first root. *Thus, we shall also call the rays of the Sun and stars that penetrate into the lowest region of the world tertiary light.* 

This light, therefore, although secondary, is filled with such active and most vivid power that it frees this entire heaven from its primary impurity, dispels its darkness, and (like a horrific shadow) causes it to glide away in an oblique revolution into the lowest part of the world. It thrusts down its more imperfect and thicker parts along with the darkness to the <term>lower regions<note>original: "inferos"; while often meaning "hell," in this cosmological context it refers to the sublunary or earthly world, which is the "bottom" of the cosmic hierarchy.</note></term>, separating the subtle from the dense. It illuminates its substance and makes it ethereal; it fills its material Spirit with its own perfection and satisfies the entire "appetite" of matter, so that it is not subject to corruption or decay and is least afflicted by contraries, because all its parts are of the same kind. 

Finally, this was that secondary essence which, for three days from the creation of the world, performed the role of the Sun until, on the fourth day (as <note>original: "Dionysius"; referring to Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian philosopher who wrote extensively on the celestial hierarchies and the nature of light.</note>Dionysius maintains), it passed into the substance of the Sun. Indeed, this is that simple act by whose operation this ethereal heaven is most skillfully adorned with glowing fires. From its motion, various differing directions arise—namely, East, West, and along the latitude (which they call "trepidation"). Finally, a benign heat, most friendly and entirely necessary to the life of creatures, is kindled. This heat is more intense and more sensible in this place than in the higher heaven because of the thicker substance of this middle heaven; for the denser the matter, the more vehemently it resists the agent, and consequently, the agent acts more strongly upon the "patient" or recipient. 

But the matter of this heaven, as has been said, is thicker than that of the <term>Empyrean heaven<note>original: "cœli Empyrei"; the highest, most spiritual heaven composed of pure light and fire.</note></term>. Therefore, light acts more strongly upon it, and it likewise resists, and consequently produces hotter effects to repel the injuries of the cold—which was once hidden but is now manifest because of the presence of heat—since it is the nature of cold to always accompany thicker matter. Thus, just as this heat is more intense than the supreme heat, so in respect to the lower world, it is far more temperate, subtle, and simple, because it follows the disposition of the "form" (light) from whose action it immediately proceeds. 

We conclude, therefore, that just as the substance of the "form" of this heaven was born from an immortal parent and is in no way corruptible—being noble and exalted in dignity because of its origin, breathing life and soul into worldly creatures and fostering them with its light—so also its heat arises from...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the middle heaven's properties, specifically how its light acts as a life-giving force (innate heat) and why celestial light remains invisible to human eyes under certain conditions. It then transitions into Chapter IX, where Fludd discusses the "imaginary" nature of astronomical constructs like poles and circles.</meta>

...derived from its action and motion, is life-giving, natural, lovely, sweet, temperate, and incorruptible; from whose fountain the <term>innate heat<note>original: "calidum innatum"; in Renaissance medicine and philosophy, this was the vital warmth believed to be the primary engine of life and metabolism in all living beings.</note></term> of animals, plants, and minerals is drawn, and by whose appearing kindness all lower bodies are generated, vivified, multiplied, and fixed until perfection. However, as to why the light of this region—though constantly clear, bright, and transparent in respect to the substance of that place—nevertheless exists as invisible when compared to lower things, the reason is ready at hand. For according to <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century theologian whose works on "Divine Names" and "Celestial Hierarchies" deeply influenced mystical philosophy and Fludd's own cosmology.</note>*Dionysius*, light is a substance existing in itself, from which the clarity, splendor, and brilliance of bodies proceed. Light, therefore, shines through itself and in itself alone; to other things, it shines only <term>by accident<note>original: "per accidens"; a philosophical term meaning something occurs due to external circumstances rather than its essential nature. Here, it means light only becomes visible when it strikes an object.</note></term>—namely, by communicating light and splendor to those bodies which are found to be aptly disposed for illumination, such as those that are more opaque and endowed with little or no extraordinary transparency. *Augustine* also seems to affirm this, asserting that the substance of the heaven is most lucid and holds the primary place among bodies due to its clarity, and yet it does not shine visibly to us at night, but is present invisibly insofar as it shines within itself. In this way, light is said to be everywhere—that is, in any place and in anything contained—shining invisibly within opaque and transparent bodies, and visibly upon the surface of opaque ones, as is apparent in the stars and the lower opaque bodies that receive the reflections of the stars. Indeed, for the continued existence of living bodies, it is necessary that light should somehow shine upon them at night. For according to the doctrine of *Hippocrates*, if the life of the stars did not temper the thickness of the air during the night, the bodies and the spirits within them would be suffocated; and yet this impression of the stars is not perceived at night—indeed, their splendor, dispersed everywhere, is scarcely or only with difficulty distinguished, except in the lights themselves.

---

# CHAPTER IX.

->*That the poles and circles of this heaven are merely imaginary, artificial, and in no way belong to it by nature.*<-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'M' showing a landscape with buildings and figures, including one person standing near a globe or celestial sphere.</image-desc>
MANY things have those skilled in Astronomy discovered that are more useful for the <term>cosmographic art<note>The practice of mapping and describing the entire universe (the cosmos), including both the heavens and the earth.</note></term> than they are true in reality. Although these things are fictional and artificial, they are nonetheless to be esteemed as true and natural because they more readily guide the student in the art. Among these, they have placed two poles on the periphery of this heaven, opposite to one another, which they have called the ends of a certain imaginary <term>axis<note>original: "axis... dimetientis"; an imaginary line passing through the center of the world around which the heavens appear to rotate.</note></term> or diameter passing through the center of the world, and the vertices of the world. Upon these poles, they have imagined the globe to be turned. They also described *circles* upon them, both *major* and *minor*; of the *major* ones, they made some *immobile* and others *mobile*. They established only two *immobile* circles, which they called *external*; one of these they called the *Meridian*, drawn through the poles of the world and the Horizon; the other they called the *Horizon*, which divides the upper hemisphere from the lower and bounds our sight. They made this horizon more upright or more slanted according to the depression or elevation of the pole. Furthermore, they called the *mobile circles*, which they devised as *internal*, either *major* or *minor*. All the *major* circles, except one, lack width, and they distinguished them into *parallels* or *non-parallels*. The Major *Equinoctial parallel*, because of its division of the world into two equal parts...

<meta>catchword: for (original Latin: enim)</meta>

<summary>This page explains how celestial light provides vital "innate heat" to all life and why this light is often invisible to the eye. It then introduces Chapter IX, arguing that astronomical coordinates like poles and circles are useful mathematical fictions rather than physical realities.</summary>
<keywords>innate heat, celestial light, Dionysius, Augustine, Hippocrates, astronomy, poles, meridian, horizon, equinoctial</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of the "imaginary" circles used by astronomers to navigate the heavens, concluding the previous discussion on the middle heaven's structure. It then begins Chapter X, which shifts focus to the "third heaven"—the sublunary realm consisting of the elements and the Earth.</meta>

# ON THE FABRIC OF THE MACROCOSM.

(for it is equidistant from both poles) they have called the **mobile belt** <note>original: "cingulum mobile"; this refers to the Celestial Equator.</note>. They established four **lesser parallels** (which are called "lesser" because they divide the world into unequal parts): namely, two greater and as many lesser. They called the greater ones **Tropics**: one of **Cancer**, which is northern, and the other of **Capricorn**, which they placed in the southern part of the world. The lesser circles, however—one of which they devised as the **Arctic** and the other the **Antarctic**—they named the **polar circles**. Finally, the largest of the non-parallels, which has a specific width, is called the **Zodiac**, which they also call the **Sign-bearer** <note>original: "Signiferum"; a common Latin term for the Zodiac, as it "carries" the twelve signs or constellations.</note>, because the twelve signs are depicted upon it. This circle alone among the others is **oblique**: for it is imagined to extend obliquely from the **Tropic of Cancer** to that of **Capricorn**. The remaining two, which lack width, are called **Colures** <note>The Colures are two principal meridians of the celestial sphere that pass through the celestial poles and the equinoctial or solstitial points.</note>, passing through the poles of the world and intersecting the **Equinoctial** circle into four equal parts. These circles, I say, along with the two poles of the world, are merely fictitious and conceived only by the imagination as <term>technical terms<note>original: "artis vocabula"; Fludd emphasizes that these lines do not physically exist in nature but are intellectual tools created by humans to measure and understand the cosmos.</note></term>, and are in no way connected to the nature of this heaven from its origin. Nevertheless, they are intentionally drawn by Astronomers and Cosmographers upon the convex surface of the firmament and its center for the easier explanation of those arts; for they attributed to each of the aforementioned circles a certain property suitable to the art of Cosmography. Thus, in the **meridian** circle, the Sun creates midday and midnight; the **Horizon** divides the visible part of the world from the invisible; the duty of the **Equinoctial** is that, when the Sun passes through its intersections (which the **Zodiac** makes in Aries and Libra), an equinox occurs throughout the whole world—wherefore it is called the **Equator** of day and night. Both **Tropics** encompass the extreme movements of the Sun according to latitude and separate the **temperate zone** from the torrid; to the **polar circles** it is given to bound the **frigid zones** and separate them from the temperate ones; the **Zodiac** demonstrates the path of the seven planets; finally, a double duty is assigned to the two **Colures**: namely, both to mark the solstices for us (summer and winter) and to demonstrate the equinoxes (vernal and autumnal). All these things, because they belong more to technical history than to Physical history, we shall reserve for the treatise on **Astronomy**.

## CHAPTER X.

->*Concerning the third heaven, and its various names; Why is this lowest region called a heaven?*<-

<image-desc>Decorative initial A with floral and foliage patterns within a square border.</image-desc>
Before the action of light upon the third and lowest region of the world, its content was like a shadow or a most thick and foul smoke, endowed with no visible form, but in a way distinguished by the density of its substance. For it was thicker below on account of the [lack of] presence of light and heat; but afterward on the third day, when the operation of light—tending downward toward the earth to create around the <term>mathematical center<note>original: "centrum mathematicum"; in the geocentric model, this is the exact geometric point at the center of the universe where the Earth is situated.</note></term>—was completed, in this heaven (as will be said below), philosophers both modern and ancient attributed various names to it. For some, wishing to signify its dimension, called it the **sublunary region** of the world, denoting all that space measured between the concave surface of the middle heaven (or the sphere of the Moon) and the convexity of the physical center (or the earth). Others, considering its elementary orbs whose bodies fill the capacity of this heaven, called it the **elementary region**; although we declared above that the higher elements also fill the heavens in a certain way. Some...

<meta>catchword: Some</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the description of astronomical circles (Zodiac, Tropics, Equator) as useful human fictions and introduces the "Third Heaven," or the sublunary elementary region, describing its initial chaotic state before being organized by light.</summary>
<keywords>Zodiac, Tropics, Colures, Equator, sublunary region, elementary region, mathematical center, astronomical circles, geocentrism</keywords>

--- Page 64 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "third heaven" or sublunary realm. The previous page established the divisions of the celestial spheres; here, the author explains how the lowest heaven is composed of the four elements and how fire is generated through the friction of the spheres.</meta>

->TREATISE I. BOOK II.<-

Some have called that region corruptible, because its matter is prone and disposed toward seeking new forms and abandoning old ones. That it is called "heaven," however, is demonstrated for two reasons: first, because it is a portion of "heaven" taken in its general name—that is, the name by which heaven is distinguished from earth; and second, because in many places in Holy Scripture, birds are called "the winged creatures of heaven," where the word "heaven" is used in a strict rather than a broad sense. From this second naming, it is certain that there are four degrees of difference in this heaven. These were rendered distinct by the greater or lesser presence of the third-order "shining form," making them either thinner, more active, more lively, and lighter, or thicker, slower, more immobile, and heavier. For the further matter is removed from the nobility of form, the thicker, more impure, less worthy, and darker it becomes, as Aristotle testifies in his book *On Substances*. From this, therefore, the four elements arise, differing in their seat, nature, dignity, and perfection. This is the true explanation of the **Hermetic riddle** <note>This refers to the "Emerald Tablet" of Hermes Trismegistus, a foundational alchemical text which famously states, "That which is below is like that which is above."</note> which asserts that superior things are only like inferior things, that the gross is the subtle and the subtle is the gross, and that the matter of things, taken by itself, is one and the same. And without a doubt, if the light created on the first day were extinguished by the divine will, it would seem not at all impossible to the Philosopher <note>Commonly referring to Aristotle.</note> that the matter of the entire universe would return to that same state; for all matters are perceived to differ from one another only by reason of form, and without form, they possess the same condition, as they are contained under the same potentiality. Thus, any part of <term>matter<note>original Greek: ὕλης (hyles); the fundamental, formless substance of the universe in Platonic and Aristotelian philosophy.</note></term> would exist as neither worthier, nor thinner, nor thicker, nor lighter than another, nor would any other difference or quality be thought to distinguish it from the rest.

***

# CHAPTER XI.
## On the element of fire.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'M' depicting two figures in a rural, hilly landscape; one figure uses a spade or hoe to till the earth while another stands observing.</image-desc>

**MATTER** that was sooty and cloaked in darkness was cast down from the highest heaven by the power of the primary light on the first day of creation into the region of the middle heaven. Thereafter, on the second day, by the power of the secondary light, it was thrust down and contracted along with all the darkness of that region from the middle heaven into the third and lowest heaven. In this inferior region, it is now rendered very thick, dark, and rebellious, so much so that the power of the natural third-tier light (which is merely a descendant of the second heaven) is not sufficient to drive and depress that foul, formless, and black matter into the center of the universe, because of the multitude of cold lurking within it. Hence, the Great Architect <note>original: "maximus ille opifex"; a reference to God as the creator or demiurge.</note> ordained that the natural faculties of the warm third-tier light should be strengthened by "accident" <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a property that isn't essential to a thing's nature but happens to it—in this case, heat caused by motion.</note>. For He willed that a certain added heat should arise, not proceeding immediately from the light, but from the violent agitation or motion of matter. For by the circular and rapid vibration and rotation of the middle heaven from the east to its opposite side—proceeding immediately from the action of the primary light—an ardent heat is excited in the upper part of the lowest heaven. This occurs partly because of the readiness of the lower heaven's matter to receive any form (whether accidental or essential), partly because the resistance of the thick matter's cold preserves and strengthens that added heat on the convex surface of the lowest heaven through <term>counter-pressure<note>original: "antiperistasin"; a scientific theory that a quality is intensified when surrounded by its opposite, such as a flame becoming hotter when surrounded by cold air.</note></term>, and finally because the density of the matter causes a certain collision of one convexity against the concavity of the other, as well as the bouncing back of the light rays emerging from the middle heaven. This heat, however, remains added and accidental. Its presence...

<meta>catchword: presence</meta>

<summary>This page explains the formation of the four elements from a single primordial matter and describes how the element of fire is generated in the sublunary realm through the friction and motion of the celestial spheres.</summary>
<keywords>elements, primordial matter, Hyle, Hermes Trismegistus, Aristotle, fire, celestial motion, antiperistasis, creation</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of the sublunary realm, specifically focusing on the uppermost layer of the elementary world: the Sphere of Fire. ### ON THE FABRIC OF THE MACROCOSM.

By its presence, the convex surface and the entire substance surrounding it are kindled with an invisible flame. From this, the power of cold is completely expelled from that sphere, and consequently, it is not impregnated with any portion of moisture. Instead, by accident—that is, through the absence of moisture (which occurs when some cold part joins with a hot one)—it must necessarily be very dry. 

Now this heat, although it is naturally present in this part, is not of the true essence of light. Rather, it is like the final effect of light's action, flowing directly from the motion of matter. For this reason, it contributes nothing to the function of life, except insofar as it relates to the constitution of the physical body, which is the seat of life. Nor is it that <term>invisible fire of the Philosophers</term> <note>original: "ignis Philosophorum invisibilis"; a reference to the celestial or spiritual fire that alchemists and Hermeticists believed was the creative "spark" of God, distinct from the destructive fire of the kitchen or the elements.</note> from which they say all things are born. 

Instead, this is merely that fire under whose supernatural heat all things are brought to **putrefaction**. In this process, the <term>radical moisture</term> <note>original: "humidum radicale"; in medieval and Renaissance medicine, this was the essential fluid within a living being that acted as the fuel for its "innate heat." Its exhaustion resulted in aging and death.</note> or the spirit of moistures is absorbed by its unnatural heat; the viscous and watery moisture is turned into poison, and the substance of the universal composite is kindled and dried out by a feverish heat. 

Yet, as long as a proportion of natural moisture remains, along with the **innate heat** (which we call "vital") that balances it, the forces of that external quality are suppressed, so that the laws of "preternature" <note>Forces or phenomena that exist outside the normal course of nature, often associated with decay or disease in this context.</note> are prevented from taking hold in the physical body. 

Therefore, the Philosophers have called this fervor and its entire substance—that is, the *perturbed spirit*—the **element of fire**. It occupies the highest seat of the lowest heaven. By unanimous consent, and for the reasons mentioned above, they have defined its nature as hot and dry. Nevertheless, one cannot deny that celestial light exists within this element, since light cannot descend into the lower regions without first penetrating the sphere of fire. The position of the element of fire is represented in this manner:

### TREATISE I. BOOK II.

---

## CHAPTER XII.

->On the element of earth.<-

Therefore, through the union of two forces—namely, the natural and the external—matter, which is rebellious on account of its coldness, is powerfully repelled by them. Following a certain natural instinct, matter flees from those primary and secondary effects of light sent out from the periphery. In pursuing its own cold nature, it retreats from the circumference of that same light to a place as remote as possible. In the form of a dreadful shadow slipping beneath in an oblique revolution <note>original: "obliqua revolutione"; likely referring to the perceived motion of the shadows or the complex rotation of the spheres around the central earth.</note>, it has shrunken itself into a hard, solid, and opaque globe around the center point or middle of the spherical machine of the universe <note>original: "universi machinæ sphæricæ"; the author views the cosmos as a divinely constructed mechanism.</note>. For in this location, it is much safer from the actions of its adversary—namely, heat—and far more distant from the home of light, since that hostile heat arises from the action of light. Indeed, this movement of matter was not stirred up by itself, but by that aforementioned double action of light.

For just as the matter of darkness was only something potential without the manifestation of light, so also without the presence of heat (which is the essential quality of light), that natural disposition of matter—namely, coldness—was nothing in actuality; and consequently, it produced no changes. Therefore, the coldness of matter is driven downward, both by the power of the primary light and by the active virtue of the element of fire produced by the movement of matter; for without these, nothing at all is done. Moreover, that coldness, by suffering the repercussions of the heat, seems to resist in a certain way, and by resisting, to react. From these actions and passions <note>In the language of the time, "passion" refers to being acted upon or receiving an impression, the opposite of "action."</note>, other levels are produced in this lowest heaven, and intermediate regions differing in their disposition are established, which the **Philosophers** <note>Meaning the natural philosophers, such as Aristotle and his medieval commentators.</note> call the "lower elements" <note>This refers to Water and Air, which sit between the central Earth and the outer Fire.</note>.

From these things, therefore, that principal reason is explained: why heavy things <term>gravia</term> <note>original: "gravia"; in geocentric physics, "heavy things" are those whose natural place is the center of the universe.</note> cannot fall beyond the center of the world, and why they rest in it perpetually. This is because gravity exists as the companion of denser and more compact matter. The cause of this thickness was coldness, which contracted the scattered and thin matter toward the center of the world to escape the rage of penetrating heat, binding it there and defending it against the invasions of the rays of light. 

However, since the action of heat pursues coldness through every part of a thick thing, and especially where it is more dispersed around the center of its own body—and since the rays of light, whose nature it is to penetrate bodies very easily, besiege and surround it everywhere—it follows that heavy things can by no means proceed beyond that center toward the natural dwelling-place of light, unless that coldness enclosed by nature within heavy things is oppressed and violated by the violence of heat. And it is from this that a heavy object, violently thrown upward, naturally and of its own accord returns downward to the center, the seat of coldness.

It is clear, therefore, that the entire substance of darkness, by whose presence the spiritual matter of the whole world was hidden and formless, is now at last—by the power of light—reduced and gathered into a dense and opaque mass around the center of the world, which is indeed called the **element of earth**. The remaining spiritual mass of the whole of matter, now endowed with form, diaphanous and wonderfully transparent, extending from the margins of the earth even to the convex surface of the spiritual world, is known by the general name of **heaven**. Among the sacred writers, the most divine *Moses* confirms this at the very threshold of *Genesis*; "In the beginning," he says, "God created the heaven and the earth," <note>Genesis 1:1</note> that is, he separated the light from the darkness; for he made the heaven clear and bright, but the earth opaque—that is, he immersed the center of the world in darkness. The positive part of the days, therefore, its o-

It defines gravity as the result of this density and uses the book of Genesis to support the separation of the transparent heavens from the opaque terrestrial core.</summary>

<header>ON THE STRUCTURE OF THE MACROCOSM.</header>

...made its origin, and established the privative <note>In philosophy, a "privation" is the absence of a quality that should naturally be present, such as darkness being the absence of light.</note> part as the cause of nights. For if the Creator had not placed the Earth in the center of the world, the world would not be defiled by any filth or impurity; all things would be luminous, and a perpetual day would shine forth. It is therefore not at all surprising that so many and such great corruptions and changes of things happen in this lower world; for in its center or heart, that most corrupt mother of ruin and privation lurks—the mournful and dark seal of the separation of forms and essences from composite creatures. There, the truthful witness and messenger of the unnatural tyranny of the element is perpetually stationed within physical things once they have been formed. Hence the most holy Job says:
> *And the dark land is covered with the shadow of Death;* <note>original: "Et terra tenebrosa cooperitur cum obscuritate Mortis"; a reference to Job 10:21.</note>
and elsewhere:
> *the land is said to be of misery and darkness;* <note>original: "terram de miseria & obscuritate esse dicitur"; a reference to Job 10:22.</note>
indeed, this is, as it were, the place of perdition, in which Adam with Eve, banished from the joys of Paradise, received for himself and left to his heirs the sign of inevitable death. The element of fire, however, is like the fiery sword of the Cherubim, brandished by a celestial spirit to block the entrance to the tree of life—that is, to that divine and immortal nectar—and to bring sudden or at least unexpected death to all who thirst too greatly for immortality. Finally, this central mass is a dire companion of sadness and error on account of its blind and stealthy condition; it is an enemy not only of men, but of God Most High and the celestial intelligences, because its horrendous rebellion is enclosed within the very middle of its belly. Therefore, woe to us wretched ones on earth, for whom such a crowd of miseries lies in wait daily, and for whom such great danger to body and soul is always imminent! But you, oh celestial creatures and sweetest inhabitants of the true Paradise—you, I say, who are thrice-blessed and more, happy beyond human understanding, you are free and immune from such miserable chains of distress through the ineffable power of light!

As for the arrangement of this central mass, although nearly all Philosophers assert that its natural quality is dryness, wishing it to predominate, and they plainly state that its coldness is accidental and acquired <note>original: "accidentalem & adscititiam"; meaning it is not inherent to the earth but taken from something else.</note> (inasmuch as it is derived from the natural disposition of water); yet, if we consider our preceding intention and the possibility of nature itself a little more accurately, we shall see that nothing wanders further from the mark of truth than that fantasy of theirs. Since the essential seat of natural light exists at the periphery of the world, and the origin of heat depends on the source of that same light, it is necessary that darkness (which is the contrary of light) and coldness (which is opposed to heat) should obtain a place as remote from each other as possible. Consequently, just as the seat of original heat is in the highest heaven, so the primordial dwelling-place of cold is in its physical center, as if in the most remote location. From this it follows that coldness naturally predominates in it, while dryness is more adventitious <note>external or added later</note> therein; that is, the parts of the earth are called "dry" because of the absence of natural moisture in that place. This is because moisture is created from the tempering of coldness with a portion of heat, which heat, for the reasons stated above, has no power or possession in that location. Furthermore, I think no one will be so foolish as to not call that body "colder" which has no portion of heat reaching it at all. It is certain that some part of airy heat is present in the element of water, since it derived its moisture (from which comes its fluidity) from the air itself, which is most certainly known to be moist and hot. But it is certain that the earth participates in nothing of the air, because it has no fluidity within itself; nor does it have any commerce with fire, both because it is most remotely distant from its sphere, and also because water (which they make contrary to fire) is placed between the earth and fire. Finally, since the earth is far thicker than the rest of the elements, and it is the nature of coldness to condense things both heterogeneous and homogeneous, it is proved infallibly by the effect that coldness is more abundant in the earth.

We have outlined the regions of earth and fire in this way.

## CHAPTER XIII.

->*Concerning the middle sphere, which is called the sphere of humidity, and concerning the element of water and of air.*<-

THE SPHERE of humidity, situated in the middle between the center of the earth and the convex surface of fire, is called the *sphere of equality*, because it is forged from an equal part of both extreme elements <note>The "extreme elements" refer to Earth (absolute cold/dryness) and Fire (absolute heat/dryness). The middle sphere—containing water and air—is seen as a balance of these opposing forces.</note>; for neither the coldness of the earth by itself, nor indeed the heat of fire alone, is able to produce humidity; rather, the conjunction of both—and, as it were, the sexual union of the male with the female—constitutes it. For just as terrestrial coldness alone constricts the <term>middle spirit</term><note>In this cosmology, "spirit" refers to the subtle, pervasive substance (ether or pneuma) that fills the universe and acts as the medium for elemental transformation.</note> to which it is present, so it belongs to the heat coming upon it to dissolve that <term>icy spirit</term><note>original: "spiritum glacialem"; the primordial substance in its frozen, crystalline state before being acted upon by light or heat.</note>, to liquefy it, and to make it fluid. Similarly, heat by itself—that is, when joined to its own dryness—absorbs the juice of that spirit...

It uses the metaphor of male and female union to describe how these opposing forces combine to liquefy the primordial icy spirit into a fluid state.</summary>

# ON THE FABRIC OF THE MACROCOSM.

...absorbs it, or certainly by its own quality dissipates it into imperceptible parts; hence it deserves to be called a separator of humidity rather than its producer. Therefore, the violence of heat is blunted more or less by the meeting of a greater or lesser degree of cold; as a result, the intense operations of both are hindered, and the <term>spirit</term><note>In this context, "spirit" refers to the universal, subtle substance that fills space and takes on the form of the elements.</note> is by no means permitted to be coagulated by the cold because of the presence of heat. Nor is it possible for the heat, according to the property of its nature, to dissipate and scatter the parts of the moistening spirit, which happens because of the resistance of the present cold; thus the spirit is dissolved by the assistance of the heat; but being dissolved, it is not dispersed, nor is it thinned almost to nothing, which is entirely achieved by the presence of cold; but it remains in the middle, naturally flowing, and through its fluid nature and liquidity, it moistens all intermediate things, either more corporeally and visibly—namely, if it adheres to a greater portion of cold—or it moistens more spiritually and imperceptibly—namely, if it embraces a larger part of heat. And it is from this, from the disposition of this moisture, that two intermediate elements are derived, as we shall immediately demonstrate. 

Therefore, the gross matter, as was said before, having been driven down from on high to the center and held there because of the immense coldness with which it is endowed, desires to rest there; but truly, because it is the nature of heat to always attack coldness in a hostile manner and to pursue it with a certain natural <term>antipathy</term><note>A common Renaissance concept where natural forces were thought to have "likes" (sympathies) and "dislikes" (antipathies) that dictated their movement.</note>; and likewise, as it is the nature of any subtle thing to penetrate, therefore this active quality [heat], driving that passive quality [cold] downward, pursues it there and constantly attempts to penetrate its mass. And because the end of any thing is its opposite, and not only rest, but also motion is opposed to motion; therefore, to this motion intended downward toward the center or middle, another motion has succeeded, namely from the bottom upward toward the element of fire, stirred up by the resistance and opposition of the cold set against the injuries of the heat and by the reflection of the rays of light. Thus, two parts of terrestrial coldness ascending upward to repel the injuries of the heat met with just as many parts of heat descending in the middle path between the center and the sphere of fire to expel that coldness. And by the union of these parts, the sphere of humidity is established, the middle part of which exists as simply and equally humid: for the forces of heat in its middle are proportional to those of cold, so that they are weighed as if on an equal balance leaning toward neither side, and they possess the same strength and power of acting on both sides; that is, they tend neither more downward nor more toward the side, and consequently, while they move, it is necessary that they be carried in a circle. 

Now, the dimension of this middle sphere tending downward toward its concavity is called the **element of water**, which, because of its proximity to the element of earth and its greater distance from the sphere of fire, appears more terrestrial, thick, visible, and sensibly moistening. For its <term>temperament</term><note>The specific "recipe" or ratio of primary qualities (heat, cold, moisture, dryness) that defines a substance.</note> is composed of three-quarters of terrestrial cold (for it is much more contiguous to the earth) and one-quarter of the heat of fire; in which temperament, although the smallest portion of heat is present, it is nevertheless sufficient to hinder the coagulating disposition of the cold and to preserve this part of the sphere of humidity in its fluid quality. And this is the same reason that we put forward above; so that it may be most clearly evident that coldness exists much more intensely not in water, but in the earth, since water is frozen into ice by the absence or reduction of that fourth part of heat—that is, it is reduced to a more terrestrial and non-fluid substance. 

The other half of the aforementioned sphere, bulging upward toward the concave surface of fire, because it is further from the earth and because of its proximity to the element of fire, partakes greatly of heat and contains very little coldness in itself by which its fiery heat might be tempered; therefore it is much more subtle, spiritual, and invisible than the lower part of the same, and because of its subtlety it penetrates more and moistens more deeply. From which it is to be...

...it must be concluded that its <term>temperament</term><note>A "temperament" or "complexion" refers to the specific balance of the four primary qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) that defines a substance.</note> coalesces from three parts of fiery heat and only one part of terrestrial cold. Philosophers have called this portion of the <term>sphere of humidity</term><note>The vast intermediate region of the cosmos between the Earth and the heavens.</note> the element of air. We conclude, therefore, that through the <term>action and passion</term><note>In natural philosophy, "action" is the influence exerted by an active quality (like heat), while "passion" is the reception of that influence by a passive quality (like cold or moisture).</note> of the two primary qualities naturally inherent in the extreme elements, several intermediate motions are generated. One of these moves from both extremes toward the middle, by which the middle sphere of humidity is produced; another tends toward the middle point, descending from there even to the universal center, from which the sphere of water arises; and a third proceeds to the point by ascending from the aforementioned middle point to the sphere of fire, and from this, the element of air is generated. 

From these things, it is manifest that the lowest heaven is divided into three parts: namely, the sphere of fire, the sphere of humidity, and the center of the universe. The elements of air and water, however, are produced almost <term>accidentally</term><note>In this context, "accidental" means they are secondary results of the interaction between the primary extremes of Fire and Earth, rather than being fundamental, independent divisions of the universe.</note>—specifically because of the proximity of the extreme elements, which provide varying force and virtue to each part of the sphere of humidity. Having considered these things more precisely, it is clearly evident that coldness can achieve nothing at all unless it is provoked by the action of heat; consequently, light—whose effect is heat—is the cause of every difference, both in the lower heaven and in the higher ones, as well as the variety of those same places. 

And here it must also be observed that the transparent substance between the two extreme elements is the <term>spirit</term>; regarding which, as much as may suffice, we mentioned in the first book. Its substance is the matter in which the simple qualities, opposite to each other in place and nature, produce their various effects. The entire humid sphere is described here with its twofold division.

## CHAPTER XIV.

### On the form of the elements.

The <term>form</term><note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "form" is the active principle that gives a substance its specific characteristics and identity, as opposed to "matter," which is the passive potential.</note> of this heaven is weaker than that of the higher heavens, because of its great distance from its origin; this is most easily proven by its effect. For indeed, our earthly fire receives its power of heating from the action of light; yet the further it is removed from the object it acts upon, the more weakly it usually heats. Consequently, it is certain that the <term>matter</term><note>The physical "stuff" of the universe. In this geocentric model, matter becomes denser and less refined as it moves toward the center (the Earth).</note> of this heaven, the more it approaches the center, the thicker, more impure, and less worthy it is. This is according to that saying of <term>Aristotle</term><note>The ancient Greek philosopher whose theories on the elements and the cosmos dominated medieval and Renaissance science.</note> in his *Metaphysics*: <note>original: "ubi plus est materiæ, ibi minus est forma, & è converso"</note> *where there is more matter, there is less form, and vice versa.*

From this it is clearly evident that, because of the weakening of its form, the matter of this heaven predominates. As a result, this matter constantly "desires" a new form every day, for there is not enough form available in this lower region to satisfy or fulfill the appetite of the matter. From this deficiency, therefore, arise the constant changes in things; and from this also arise corruptions of every...

For this reason, this heaven is not undeservedly called "corruptible" by the Philosophers. Although this formal nature is very slight, it is nonetheless so effective in its motion that without its essential heat, nothing at all could be perfected in this heaven. Indeed, *it is that invisible fire of Heraclitus*<note>Heraclitus (c. 535 – c. 475 BC) was a Greek philosopher who maintained that fire was the fundamental material of the universe.</note> by which like things are procreated from like, all things are generated and nourished, and in any mixture whatever, they are brought forth according to the weighted scales of justice; thus, a form suitable to its species is imprinted, by which any thing is distinguished and separated from the rest. For <term>Aristotle</term><note>The influential Greek philosopher whose physical theories were standard in the 17th century.</note> asserts this in his book *On the Five Substances*, where he says that *form is that by which one thing differs from another*. The <term>Platonists</term><note>Followers of Plato who emphasized the role of the Sun and the "Soul of the World" in mediating divine light to the earth.</note> affirm that the form of this heaven proceeds immediately from the Sun—into which, on the fourth day, the light of the second heaven was contracted—wishing to agree with the other sects of Philosophers that the sun is the fertile mother of all things in this heaven. For they placed the <term>soul of the world</term><note>The *Anima Mundi*, an intrinsic spirit believed to enliven the entire universe.</note> in the sun, since from it is derived every power of generating and of preserving things already generated. For the Sun, by its light, breathes life and warmth into the lower things, and turns all things toward itself by its life-giving heat.

## CHAPTER XV.

### *On the substance of the aforementioned elements.*

In the preceding chapters, we dealt with the grades of the elements and their mutual dependence, and how they differ from one another in respect to the various qualities of the mixture of earth with that of the element of fire. Now, however, we shall relate a few things concerning their principal matter or substance. The spirits of darkness, which were cast out from the supreme region into the center and retained no portion (or a very slight one) of the primary light, emerged from the earth through its open pores after the flight of the solar spirit. These then possessed the upper place of this lower region, acting as the thicker masses or dregs of that material from which the purest body of the sun was separated and raised into the heart of the middle heaven. Indeed, these aforementioned parts of the spirits are compelled to dwell in that location—namely, in the sphere of fire—and to be carried in a circle, both because they lack a sufficient "soul" to raise them higher, and because the form of the middle heaven cannot completely inform a greater quantity of matter. Finally, this is because its mass is thicker and more impure than the matter of the [higher] heaven, even though it is the most subtle of all things in the lower heaven. Furthermore, those spirits of darkness in the middle region of the world, which enclosed some sparks of its secondary light, were reduced into vegetables and plants on the second day. The rest of its parts, which possess almost no portion of the ethereal rays, succeed the matter of the element of fire since they are naturally contiguous to the super-celestial spirit, and they fill the place of the air. Finally, the spirits of darkness of the third and lowest region of the world, which are the thickest and heaviest of all, follow in the order of nature because they are contiguous and nearest to the <term>ether</term><note>The "ether" here refers to the subtle fluid or "spirit" that fills the space between the elements and the heavens.</note>. Hence, they possess the region of water and are called "water"; and if parts of these spirits include any portion of the light of this heaven, they produce minerals. And in this manner, in their retreat, the thicker spirits of darkness, having been thrust out of the <term>Empyrean heaven</term><note>The highest, most spiritual heaven, composed of pure light and fire.</note> as the supreme region of all, constituted the element of fire; while those cast down from the middle region or ether turned into the substan-

- soul of the world (anima mundi): The vitalizing spirit of the universe.
- spirits of darkness (spiritus tenebrarum): The unrefined, dark material left after light was gathered into the sun and stars.
- Empyrean: The highest heaven of pure light.

...[the substance] of the air; and finally, having been expelled from the lowest region of the world, they provided their essence to the water. Earth, however, is like the dunghill of all these, the receptacle of their superfluities, and—to speak with the <term>chemical Philosophers</term><note>The author is referring to alchemists, who viewed the creation of the world as a grand chemical process.</note>—the <term>caput mortuum</term><note>Latin for "dead head"; in alchemy, this refers to the useless, solid residue remaining after a substance has been distilled or charred.</note> or dregs of the entire spiritual mass. Water follows the thickness and impurity of these dregs, being like a more cast-off part of the air, just as the air follows fire, and fire follows the <term>Quinta Essentia</term><note>The "Fifth Essence" or ether, the purest substance that makes up the celestial bodies, superior to the four earthly elements.</note>. 

This, therefore, is the chemical extraction of nature, the more excellent part of which is that Fifth Essence, exalted to the heavens with its soul; this is no different than what we have experienced in the purification of <term>spirit of wine</term><note>An early term for concentrated alcohol or ethanol produced by distilling wine.</note>. First, the thicker part of the spirit is sublimated, which they call "burning water" <note>original: *aquam ardentem*</note>; in this, there are three parts, as it were, of the fifth essence and one part of "phlegm" <note>the watery, inactive residue</note>, which is compared to the thicker part of the spirit of darkness in the middle heaven that constitutes the element of fire. Following this is that weak liquor which they commonly call "water of life" <note>original: *aquam vitæ*</note>, where there are two parts of the spirit of wine and an equal amount of water; this is compared to the element of air. In the last place, the "phlegm" is distilled, in which there is almost no spirit, but it is considered entirely as "pituitous" <note>phlegm-like or mucus-like</note> water, which is compared to the element of water. The dregs or *caput mortuum* of all these things, found at the bottom of the <term>cucurbit</term><note>A gourd-shaped glass vessel used in distillation.</note>, is not improperly compared to the element of earth. 

In this way, the admirable operation of nature in the selection, purification, and ordering of the matter of the elements is clearly explained. We conclude, therefore, that **Earth** is the dregs of the spirits of every heaven and a heap of substantial darkness. **Water** is that grosser spirit of darkness of the lowest heaven, almost void of light; for the spirits of that heaven that embrace light are converted into various metals, according to the greater or lesser proportion of light contained in them; hence their "radical moisture" is found by the Philosophers to be a thick and viscous water. **Air** is that spirit of the second heaven that escaped from the earth through a process of refinement; its denser part, embracing a secondary light, produces warmer or colder vegetables according to the suitable proportion of light and that "fat" spirit. And finally, **Fire** is the spirit of darkness of the <term>Empyrean heaven</term><note>The highest, most fire-filled part of the heavens, believed to be the dwelling place of the Divine.</note>, whose thicker substance, filled with primary light, constituted the solar body, as will be declared below.

->*An experiment with wine, from which the nature of each of the elements can be gathered.*<-

We have performed an experiment with the most excellent wine itself, which clearly indicates to us, like a pointing finger or a rule of certainty, the substances of the elements and of the Fifth Essence itself. For once the spirit was extracted from the wine, by a method that is not new, I extracted from it an oil floating upon the spirit. Then, from the dregs of the wine, I extracted another oil and brought it to the highest purification through various distillations. Afterward, I frequently purified the "phlegm" that was distilled even after the extraction of the spirit. Finally, I cleansed the dregs of the whole wine from its muddy filth through many washings. I then placed all these into a round glass vessel in equal proportions and sealed it <term>Hermetically</term><note>A "Hermetic seal" involves melting the glass neck of a flask shut to make it completely airtight, a common practice in alchemy.</note>, as they say. Leaving this vessel to rest throughout an entire night, I found five regions in the morning: the lowest was black and veiled in darkness; upon this resided the portion of phlegm; next, the oil extracted from the dregs covered that; in the fourth place above that, the spirit of wine ascended; and finally, the oil extracted from the spirit covered it in the fifth order.

# CHAPTER XVI.

## *Concerning certain experiments with waters in the formation of the aforementioned heavens, demonstrating various arrangements through the operation of light.*

It is certain that fire is included under the name of light and originated from light itself; for nowhere in the universal description of creation did our divine leader <term>Moses</term><note>The author treats the Book of Genesis as a literal scientific account of the universe's chemical origin.</note> mention that most noble creature, fire, except when speaking of light. This is more clearly explained in Book 6, Chapter 8. Furthermore, no one will doubt that air and water were part of the substance of that mass—namely, the <term>universal waters</term><note>In this cosmological view, the "waters" mentioned in Genesis 1:2 are the primordial matter from which all elements were distilled.</note>—since the Holy Scriptures testify that all things were forged from it. Now, however, because the aforementioned operations are undoubtedly held in suspicion by those who rely more on their senses than on reason, it remains to demonstrate these operations to their eyes through the effects derived from those two primary causes—namely, water and light. They will very easily perceive these things visually if they carefully observe the following experiments.

### *The first experiment concerning fire and air, or subtle water.*

When a fire is lit in the upper part of a concave and perfectly sealed instrument, at the bottom of which (opposite the fire) a very small hole or tube is placed, the internal air is <term>attenuated</term><note>Made thinner or less dense through heating.</note> the closer it approaches the location of the fire. The thicker part of this thinned air, which is colder, moves away from the operating fire toward the lower part of the vessel—as if fleeing from its enemy. This is clearly visible to the eye, for the air is perceived to exit through the tube or hole of that region gradually in the form of a moist vapor.

**B. C. D. E. F. G. H.** is a vessel made in the shape of an altar; on whose upper surface, if fire **A.** is lit, the air enclosed within is thereby thinned <note>The Latin *subtiliatur* suggests the air is being refined or made less dense.</note>, and its thicker part, fleeing the fire, seeks an exit through pipe **I**, and is perceived to emerge through the outer opening **L** like a moist vapor. These things are demonstrated more fully in *Hero of Alexandria’s Pneumatics* <note>original: *Heronis Alexandrini spiritualibus*</note>, specifically in experiment 11. Here, however, we have depicted the movement of the air according to the diameter of a circle, because it pertains more to our present purpose. 

In order to make this experiment even more evident, I took a hollow glass egg <note>A common oval-shaped alchemical flask.</note>, filled only with air and <term>hermetically</term><note>An airtight seal made by melting the glass neck of a vessel; the term derives from the legendary alchemist Hermes Trismegistus.</note> sealed, and placed it in ashes; then, I gently heated the glass with the fire of a lamp. Once this was done, the thicker part of the air enclosed in the glass—separated from the thinner part by <term>rarefaction</term><note>The process by which a substance increases in volume and decreases in density, usually through heating.</note>—fled the fire, and resolving itself into vapor, finally clung to the top of the vessel in the form of drops.

# TREATISE I. BOOK II.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a spherical vessel (likely a retort or globe) resting upon a cylindrical brick furnace. A fire is burning within the base of the furnace. From this, therefore, we conclude that thick air, fleeing the fire because of the coldness contained within it, tends toward the region most remote from the fire’s presence. But the center is the place most distant from the circumference. Accordingly, when the fiery power <note>original: *virtus ignea* — the active, heating quality of the fire</note> occupies the surface of some hollow globe, the air (or thin water) retained and enclosed within it will send its thicker parts toward the center. Indeed, all parts of the air contained in the hollow globe are gradually thicker the further they are from the vigor of the fire, as is explained by the following experiment.

->*Experiment II.*<-

Therefore, let a certain glass or copper globe be made with three distinct regions, so that one encompasses the entire concavity of the other; and let these regions have certain openings or tubes protruding in the manner depicted below, and let the outermost surface of the globe be surrounded on all sides by a most lively fire. When this is done, it will be visible to the eye that the exterior fire imparts its more lively vigor to the uppermost region, and the air contained within it is wonderfully thinned <note>original: *attenuari* — to be made less dense or more "subtle"</note> due to its proximity to the living fire. And the second, middle region also receives the fiery vigor from the first; but nevertheless, because the heat is milder there than in the upper region, the air existing within it is less subtle and thin, the fiery force being naturally unable to <term>rarefy</term><note>To make a substance less dense; in this context, turning "thick" air into "thin" or "subtle" spirit.</note> it further. Finally, the fiery power hidden in the middle region will also infuse some force, albeit small, into the lowest region. From this, the preceding proposition—and also that which is expressed after the end of chapter seven—demonstrates that the thick air of the upper region (where there is a greater quantity), and that of the middle region (where the proportion is smaller), and finally even that of the lowest region (which is the fewest of all), tends toward the center and gathers there into drops. The demonstration follows.

# ON THE STRUCTURE OF THE MACROCOSM.

<image-desc>A circular cosmological diagram set within a rectangular frame. The outermost ring is composed of stylized, radiating flames. Within this fiery border are concentric circles representing different spheres. In the middle band, several small funnel-like openings, labeled with the letter "o," point inward toward the center. Various letters (including P, q, f, d) are placed at intervals within the rings. The innermost center of the diagram is a solid black circle.</image-desc>

The upper sphere, on the surface of which the primary **power of fire** <note>original: *ignis virtus* — refers to the active, heating energy of the element fire</note> resides, is marked **A.B.C.D.** The second sphere is **E.F.** The third is **I.H.** The line measuring the furthest point of distance to which the **cold nature** <note>original: *frigida natura* — the elemental quality of coldness, which Fludd views as a passive, contracting force</note> (hiding within the thick air and driven by the fiery power) can move, is **L.M.**; this is the semi-diameter of the larger circle. **O. o. o.** are the openings contained within the curved surfaces of these regions, through which the thicker part of the air is driven downward toward the center.

->*Experiment III.*<-

There remains now the third experiment, from the demonstration of which it is clear that thick air, necessarily driven from the circumference to the center, must form a body that is very thick, dense, and, because of that density, **opaque** <note>original: *opacum* — Fludd is using this experiment to simulate how the solid, dark Earth was forged at the center of the universe by the pressure of celestial heat pushing "thickness" inward</note>.

<meta>catchword: EXPE-</meta>

<summary>This page describes a diagram of concentric spheres where heat from the outer edge forces cold, thick air through openings toward the center. This process demonstrates Fludd's theory of how density and opacity (solid matter) are created at the center of a system through external thermal pressure.</summary>
