### CHAPTER VIII.

->*Of the various names of the soul, and the uncertain opinions of the ancient Philosophers regarding its essence.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'I' featuring woodcut foliage and floral motifs.</image-desc>

The lowest species of spiritual creatures is called the *Soul* <note>original: "Anima"</note>, insofar as it grants life to all things. Whence some of the Pagans called it wind and spirit. It is also called the *Mind* <note>original: "animus"</note>, insofar as it possesses some will; *Reason*, insofar as it judges directly; likewise *Spirit*, in respect to breathing; and finally *Sense*, while it feels, as *Isidore* <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), an archbishop and scholar whose 'Etymologies' served as an encyclopedia for the Middle Ages.</note> testifies in *Book 15, Chapter 14*. But so that it may be more fully understood by everyone what this last species of intelligences is—since the ancient *Greek*, *Arab*, and *Latin Philosophers* who labored in the exploration of its nature brought forth opinions about it that were not so much certain as they were diverse and wavering—we therefore think it worthwhile to first explain their opinions in this place. For some were pulled this way, some that way, and all, distracted by diverse opinions, brought forth varying judgments about it. 

*Plato*, therefore, defines the soul as an essence living in itself; *Aristotle* as the act <note>Often translated as 'entelechy' or the realization of potential.</note> of a physical organic body having life in potential; *Zeno* as a number moving itself; *Xenocrates* as a number moving in itself; *Aristoxenus* as the principle of number; *Pythagoras* as harmony and the concord of melody, with whom *Dinarchus* agrees, asserting it to be the principle of the four elements, bringing to the body with which it is joined their most beautiful temperament and harmony; *Hipparchus* as fire or a fiery power; *Heraclitus of Pontus* as light; *Another* of the same name <note>Referring to Heraclitus of Ephesus.</note> as a stellar sub-

# ON THE CREATURES OF THE EMPYREAN HEAVEN.

...of a certain spark; Posidonius calls it an *idea*, insofar as it is the form of an animated species; Asclepiades imagines it as if it were a certain chord, providing the faculty for the five senses to carry out their functions; Hippocrates considers it a subtle spirit, dispersed through all the passages of the body; Democritus <note>The Latin text writes "Democrates," but refers to the famous atomist Democritus.</note> judges it a subtle, uncertain spirit, consisting of the smallest and indivisible particles, which he calls atoms, running together and clinging to each other. Tertullian and Avicenna <note>Avicenna (Ibn Sina) was a Persian polymath; Tertullian was an early Christian author from Carthage.</note> claim that the soul is born from a soul just as the body is procreated from seed, and that it is multiplied in the same way that the light of many candles is kindled from a single flame. Thales of Miletus calls it the "motive power" of every thing; and from this is derived his opinion that a magnet is animated because it draws iron plates to itself. Alcmaeon compared it to the celestial bodies because it is a thing most mobile—indeed, always in motion—from which disposition, he says, its eternity can most easily be discerned. Parmenides wishes it to be made from earth and fire; Epicurus from fire and air; Empedocles and Ericrates say it is the most powerful thing in the body. Finally, some feel it is the brain, others the blood (as did Critias); others believe it to be vapor or humor; some even go so far as to deny altogether that it is a substance. We shall clearly declare from the writings below that, although the assertions of these ancient philosophers seem for the most part to disagree very seriously with one another, some nevertheless approach closer to the truth than others—and indeed, several even agree with the Truth itself.

## CHAPTER IX.

->*What is the Soul? And its properties.*<-

<margin>Agrippa, Book 3, Chapter 37.</margin>
<margin>John of Damascus, homilies, and Bernard.</margin>
<margin>John of Damascus, ibid. Seneca, Augustine.</margin>

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'S' featuring floral and scrollwork motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>
According to the opinions of Augustine, John of Damascus, Isidore, Bernard, Remigius, Calcidius, and almost all philosophers—both Pagan and Christian—we gather that the nature and disposition of the soul is explained in two ways: either it is considered by itself (that is, separated from the body), or insofar as it is joined to the body and gives it form. Therefore, as it is understood in its own proper and free disposition, it is described thus: I. To be a certain divine light, created according to the first exemplar—that is, in the image of the uncreated light <note>The "uncreated light" refers to God himself.</note>—and adorned with a beauty that is almost angelic. II. A spiritual substance, emanating from divine fountains, simple, indissoluble, immortal, free, incorporeal, and invisible, lacking quantity, figure, weight, and color. III. A rational intellect. IV. An intellectual spirit, partaking in reason, always moving, and tending toward blessedness by its very nature. V. Finally, a divine mind, understanding all things and assimilating itself to all things. And in this description, its properties are precisely delineated. One of these is to illuminate knowledge: hence, in the *Theological Resolutions* <note>original: "resolut. Theolog. tract. 2. part. 2. por 3. quæst. 2."</note> the intellect is called the light and reason of knowing, because it is the active principle of knowledge. Another is to lead the <term>Microcosm</term> <note>From the Greek 'mikros' (small) and 'kosmos' (world); the concept that a human being is a miniature version of the entire universe.</note> back to God by its presence, as is held in the first chapter of Genesis; hence we have defined the soul as a light created in the image of the uncreated light. Its third property is that, since it is a substance most pure, simple, spiritual, invisible, and incorporeal, it has communication with the "intelligences" <note>In this context, "intelligences" refers to angels or higher spiritual beings.</note> and—to speak with Iamblichus—through the harmonies of its essence and its actions, it sometimes reconciles itself with the divine. The fourth is that, while it is enclosed in the body, it never permits it to die, because it is life itself; nor is it ever destroyed by the violence of death (as Porphyry most excellently says). Thus, with the Sages, we call it immortal and indissoluble. But it is also marked by other properties...

### TREATISE I. BOOK IV.

...[the soul is structured] so that it always remembers itself, and never fails to understand itself. For it knows nothing so well as that which is present to it, and nothing is more present to the mind than the mind itself; as **Aurelius Augustine** testifies in his *De Trinitate*, books 10 and 14, chapter 10. And it is from this that we have defined it as a rational understanding and an intellectual spirit, because all things are understood by the mind. Because it assimilates itself to all things, **Augustine** calls it the "likeness of all things" in his book *On the Spirit and the Soul*, or because it retains within itself the likenesses of all things which it intends to understand; for all knowledge occurs by way of assimilation <note>In medieval philosophy, "assimilation" is the process by which the mind becomes like the object it is perceiving in order to understand it.</note>. Hence, through its very essence, it is likened to the earth; through imagination, to water; through reason, to air; and through the intellect, it is likened to the firmament <note>This creates a ladder of the soul's faculties corresponding to the four elements and the heavens.</note>.

The **sixth** property follows, which is, according to **Bernard**, that it is the companion of reason. The **seventh** is that it tends by its own nature toward bliss <note>original: "beatitudinem"</note>, which **Porphyry** calls the divine life itself; the contemplation of this thing fuses the contemplator into one with that which is contemplated, as can be seen in the aforementioned author. It is for this reason that **Bernard** called the human soul a partaker of bliss and an heir of salvation. The **ninth and last** property, which we have observed from the aforementioned definition, is that it is moved always and without ceasing. Therefore, it is certain that it is a most lively form and a primary act <note>term: "primary act" (Latin: *actus primus*). In Aristotelian philosophy, this refers to 'entelechy,' or the soul as the fundamental life-force that makes a body functional.</note>. Because of this, it is called a "self-moving number" by **Plato** in the *Phaedrus*, and an "acting number in itself" by **Xenocrates**; for the soul moves within its own spirit. From these things, therefore, it clearly appears that the first of the aforementioned properties agrees with the opinions of **Hipparchus**, both **Heraclitus** and **Epicurus**. Through the third property, the wisdom of **Plato** and the depth of **Alcmaeon** shine most clearly. Finally, the opinions of **Thales of Miletus**, **Aristoxenus**, and **Xenocrates** agree with the ninth property.

Furthermore, insofar as the soul is joined to the body, we have described it in two ways: either with respect to the **Microcosm** <note>The "Microcosm" refers to the human being as a "little world" that mirrors the entire universe (the Macrocosm).</note> alone, or insofar as it relates to animals and plants. As it is joined with the body of the Microcosm alone, the soul is defined with its "vehicle" <note>Neoplatonists believed the soul possessed a "vehicle" (spiritus) or subtle body to bridge the gap between pure spirit and heavy matter.</note> as an incorporeal principle, a proportional number, and a primary act suited to our bodies, by whose presence we live, feel, move from place to place, are endowed with reason, understand primarily, and are capable of good or evil will. 

From this description, we have observed certain properties of the soul regarding the disposition of the human body. The **first** of these is that it is a principle connecting the four elements in a composition so aptly and congruously that it imparts to them a most beautiful temperament and a sweet harmony. The **second** is that, insofar as it is a "proportional number," it has the power to rightly arrange the parts of the body for the purposes of life, sense, motion, and action through a certain harmonic consensus. This kind of proportion in the human body produces **Human Music**, which is nothing other than concord, through which the soul loves the body and its parts. Hence, the opinion of **Pythagoras** was not insane when he judged the soul to be a concordance of melody. The **third** property is that it is necessary for the body where this soul is found to live, feel, move, and reason. The **fourth** is that in its motion, it is always capable of good or evil will; for, although when free and separate from the body it never ceases to remember and understand itself (as has been said), yet insofar as it is involved in earthly things which it drinks in through the senses, it is sometimes accustomed to perform that [self-understanding] less exactly. 

And here indeed we think the opinion of **Iamblichus** should by no means be rejected, by which we are taught that the passions of animals do not certainly reside in their souls, but in the body, which possesses a certain quality under the soul by which it acts and suffers. The **last** property is that it universally rules and governs the body, according to the opinion of **Remigius**, no differently than a sailor governs a ship; or according to **Chalcidius**, just as a spider staying in the center of its web is perceived to move the entire web by its own motion, so also the soul, hiding in the heart—which is the center of the body—is accustomed to move the entire mass of the body. Furthermore, as this spiritual creature relates to beasts, the definition...

of it, applies equally to all these creatures as well as to humans; except that it does not bring them intellect and reason. This is the <term>specific difference</term><note>In logic, a "specific difference" is the characteristic that distinguishes one species from another within the same genus.</note> that separates humans from the "brutes," which are called irrational due to their lack of these traits. Hence, through the presence of this soul, they live, feel, and move locally in the same way as humans; but they understand nothing, instead only distinguishing and recognizing certain things through their senses. Similarly, the soul in plants <note>original: "vegetabilibus"</note> is defined in the same way as in animals, except that it does not provide them with sensation. Therefore, just as the soul does not make beasts participants in reason, it leaves plants without experience of the senses. This description of the soul, then, fits any species of animal: namely, that it is a certain sensible power, or a spirit that gives life to the animated body and grants it the sense by which it perceives and discerns material, sensible things—that is, the physical forms of bodily things, whether present or absent, outside or within. Furthermore, as it applies to both animals in general and to plants, it is defined by **John of Damascus** in his 26th homily and by **Aristotle** himself as being a certain power of the heart for the life of the body, imparting growth and reproduction. That is, it is a certain virtue or <term>first act</term><note>In Aristotelian philosophy, the "first act" (actus primus) refers to the essential functioning or "aliveness" of an organic body.</note> in a living, organized physical body, converting nourishment into the substance of the being nourished, so that it may preserve it in its being, bring it to its proper size, and grant the ability to generate another like itself in species. In this description, indeed, three powers of the vegetative faculty are expressed: namely, to nourish, to grow, and to reproduce. From this description of the soul, we can gather many other properties; since these are readily known to those who study philosophy without further elaboration, we shall refrain from speaking of them in this place.

### CHAPTER X.

->Concerning the soul of the world, or the divine power dispersed everywhere in the world,<-
->and especially in its center; from which it is called the soul of the middle<-
->of nature.<-

<margin>taste:</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter M, featuring floral patterns and a central figure or face.</image-desc>
**M**any have affirmed that the world possesses not only a soul and a spirit—including all the **Platonists**, the **Pythagoreans**, **Orpheus**, **Hermes Trismegistus**, **Aristotle**, **Theophrastus**, and **Avicenna**—but they also judged it to be in some way a participant in the divine mind. With these thinkers, **Marcus Manilius** and **Virgil**, and similarly **Boethius** and **Augurellus**, both Gentile and Christian poets, seem to agree. The first of these, in his *Astronomica*, says: <note>original Latin: "Hoc opus immensi..."</note>

> This work, constructed from the body of the vast world,
> is ruled by the power of a divine soul.

The second, in the sixth book of the *Aeneid*: <note>original Latin: "Spiritus intùs alit..."</note>
> An inner spirit nourishes, and a mind, infused through 
> its limbs, moves the entire mass.

The third: <note>original Latin: "Tu triplicis mediam..."</note>
> You connect the middle soul of a triple nature,
> moving all things, etc.

The fourth, in the first book of the *Chrysopoeia*: <note>original Latin: "Nonnulli, quicquid..."</note>
> Some report that whatever is spread throughout the heavens,
> the air, the lands, and the level surface of the broad sea,
> is driven from within by a soul, by which they believe all
> things of the world live, and the world itself leads this life.
> But since the soul is not entirely devoid of body,

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul, moving from the individual souls of living creatures to the concept of the World Soul (Anima Mundi), which animates the entire universe.</meta>

# TREATISE I. BOOK IV.

> ->But the world, and the parts of the world also consist of body,<-
> ->Between these a middle Spirit is formed, which they call<-
> ->Neither body nor soul; alone sharing the seat of both,<-
> ->It brings these extremes back together into one.<-

The world therefore has a soul, as do the heavens, the stars, and the elements, with which they generate life in those lower and mixed things. To use the argument of **Augurelli** <note>Giovanni Aurelio Augurello (c. 1441–1524), an Italian humanist and alchemist.</note>, these are perfect bodies, and it is necessary that their perfection comes from a certain soul. What doubt is there then, that the world lives, as it were, and moves with a natural motion? Since it is a most noble body, it must consequently have a most vibrant soul, providing it with life and motion by means of a most pure spirit. This spirit acts as the vehicle and medium by which the soul is transferred to the lower world. 

But as to the nature of this World Soul <note>original: "anima mundi"</note>—although we have already spoken extensively in the chapters concerning universal form and the description of the Empyrean heaven <note>In ancient cosmology, the highest part of heaven, composed of pure light or fire.</note>—we shall nevertheless briefly examine here what others think about it, while touching lightly upon our own opinion. According to some, this World Soul is a certain essence filling all things, penetrating everything even to the center, and binding and connecting all things together to make the mechanism of the whole world a single unit. It becomes like a single **monochord** <note>An ancient musical instrument with one string, used by philosophers to represent the mathematical harmony of the universe.</note> resonating for the three types of creatures with only a single breath and a single life. In the details of this definition, both the opinions of the ancient philosophers and those of more recent ones are excellently portrayed, as is explained more broadly in the previous chapters on the soul. 

According to others, the World Soul is described as being the power of **GOD**, and as it were the **GOD** of nature itself, or the "middle soul" of nature. Through this power, **GOD** is said to be everywhere in the world, filling it, governing it, and continually preserving it. This is that divine virtue which **GOD** assiduously rains down liberally upon created things from the perennial fountain of His Spirit, by which all things live, are sustained, grow, and multiply. Many believed that this World Soul is located especially in the **Sun**. 

For very many philosophers claim that the solar body received its extraordinary splendor and beauty from this source. They report that there is nothing in heaven that more clearly displays a likeness to **GOD** through its light and clarity; indeed, they believe that any thing, in so far as it is capable of receiving light, claims that much of **GOD** for itself. We conclude, therefore, that this World Soul is, as it were, the activity and essence of the light created on the first day. This light descends from the Empyrean heaven through the "sieve" of the Crystalline heaven into the lower regions. On the fourth day, it gathered itself in the **Sun**, as if in the center and heart of the ethereal heaven, and from there it shoots and extends itself circularly everywhere. Indeed, **Augustine** in his *Book on Free Will* <note>original: "lib. de lib. arbit."</note> seems to argue that this formal nature of light is the "form" of every thing, and the soul itself, for this reason. For he says that light holds the first place among physical bodies, and that light is the very disposition of the soul. Furthermore, in his *Literal Commentary on Genesis* <note>original: "libro super Genesin, ad literam"</note> and elsewhere, it is said that light is of such nobility that even physical things are judged more noble and perfect the more they participate in light; this is seen perfectly in the elements. 

There is nothing in the whole world more worthy than light, since it is a simple act that converts all things to itself with life-giving heat. It permeates all beings, carrying their qualities and virtues through all things, and finally dispelling all darkness and gloom. It is for this reason that the **Sun**, because of the excellence of its clarity, is placed in the middle of the world like an ethereal King and Emperor of the physical world, holding the scepter and the helm. It is illustrated with an extraordinary brilliance above the rest of the world's creatures, 
->inspiring and pouring life, sense,<-
->motion, and growth into the lower things through its rays.<-

### CHAPTER XI.

*That the intellectual soul, although it is composed of parts just as many Angels are, nevertheless differs from them in certain respects; and concerning the immortality of the soul.*

<image-desc>A large decorative drop-cap 'A' begins the first paragraph, featuring an ornate square border with scrolling floral and vine-like motifs surrounding the letter.</image-desc>

Nearly all philosophers, both ancient and modern, testify that the **soul** is composed of **matter and form**. But this kind of composition does not occur from pre-existing matter, as happens with things that are generated and corrupted <note>Meaning physical objects that are born and eventually decay or die.</note>, but rather from "nothing"—that is, from matter that did not exist before and was produced through creation. From this act of creation, the **Empyrean heaven** <note>In ancient cosmology, the highest part of heaven, composed of pure light or fire.</note> and its creatures (among which souls are numbered) are derived, as is explained more extensively in the *Theological Resolutions*, treatise 2, part 2, portion 3, question 3. And although the composition of souls and angels is the same, it has been discovered by both philosophers and theologians that these two "pre-celestial" creatures differ in species for many reasons, as stated in the aforementioned treatise.

The *first* of these reasons is because the nobler a created form is, the more it is multiplied into specific grades; this is also confirmed by **Iamblichus** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who wrote on the soul and the divine.</note>, who distinguishes the "intelligences" into four grades. But the intellectual creature is the most noble; therefore, it has many species. Consequently, an angel and a soul are not of the same species. The *second* reason is because their way of understanding is different: it is greater in angels and lesser in souls. The *third* is because the soul is sometimes **discursive** <note>term: discursive; the process of reasoning from one premise to another to reach a conclusion.</note> insofar as it resides in a human body, whereas an Angel is always **intuitive** <note>term: intuitive; the ability to know a truth immediately and completely without needing steps of logic.</note> and functions without discourse. The *last* is because the soul is the "perfecter" of the body; an Angel, however, brings nothing to the perfection of a body. From these things it is manifest that the soul is a lower species of intelligence, closest to humans and most suited for governing their bodies, yet it is immortal and survives forever after the decay of its physical body.

For although the power of composed matter is naturally corruptible and ages with time, the operation of the rational soul and mind is not at all consumed by the passage of time or made older; rather, it seems to grow younger and flourish again in a wonderful way. For wisdom flourishes in the elderly, and **prudence** <note>Practical wisdom and the ability to govern oneself by use of reason.</note> increases with the lapse of time. Furthermore, since understanding is a rational operation of the soul that does not depend on the body (because no bodily organ determines it), it is certain that the soul itself is incorruptible. It is also incorruptible insofar as it naturally desires **beatitude** <note>original: "beatitudo"; the state of perfect, eternal happiness or supreme blessedness.</note>, as was said before; this natural desire is not in vain, since the soul is capable of possessing such happiness. Wherever there is beatitude, there is security; where there is security, there is immortality and incorruptibility, as found in the *Theological Resolutions*, treatise 2, portion 3, question 2. In these arguments, authors sometimes seem to mean the "mind" <note>original: "mens"</note>, sometimes the "rational spirit" <note>original: "spiritus rationalis"</note> insofar as it desires beatitude, and not rarely the mind together with the spirit, from which the composition of the rational soul is made.

We conclude, therefore, in addition to the aforementioned reasons, that the soul is incorruptible because of the nature of its composition. No contrary elements meet in its composition, since the "spirit," which acts as the matter of the soul, is airy; but the "mind," which is the "form" of the composite, is fiery, for it is of the spiritual substance of light. Since air and fire are most friendly to each other—one being like the food for the other, and the other like the life and soul for its nourishment—they are joined together by a natural marriage as if by perpetual bonds. They act upon each other without the destruction of either, just as a man and woman coming together. Furthermore, the abundance of the light-form has so filled the desire of the spiritual matter that it does not desire a new form at all. Instead, it is so fundamental in its substance that the Wise have written that it even divinely gave form to the thick little body of the **microcosm** <note>term: microcosm; "the little world," referring to the human being as a miniature reflection of the entire universe.</note> made from earth and mud.

<summary>This page argues that the human soul is a distinct species of intelligence, lower than angels but uniquely suited to the body, and demonstrates its immortality through its ability to grow in wisdom and its desire for eternal happiness.</summary>
<keywords>intellectual soul, angels, immortality, Iamblichus, discursive reason, intuitive knowledge, beatitude, microcosm, matter and form, Empyrean heaven</keywords>

# BOOK FIVE.

->*Concerning the Creatures of the Ethereal Heaven.*<-

## The Contents of this Book.

<image-desc>A hierarchical schema or branching diagram organizing the chapters of the book into categories and subcategories using large curly braces.</image-desc>

Among the creatures of the ethereal heaven <note>The "ether" was historically considered the "fifth element," a pure substance that filled the universe beyond the terrestrial sphere.</note>, three things are to be observed, namely:

*   **Composition** <note>What these celestial bodies are made of.</note>, concerning which see Chapter 4. They are fashioned from:
    *   **Spirit** <note>original: "Spiritu"; here referring to a subtle, airy substance.</note>, which is neither too thick nor too thin, concerning which see Chapter 1.
    *   **Secondary light** <note>original: "Luce secundaria"; light that is reflected or derived, rather than being the primary source.</note>, with the exception of the Sun, concerning which see Chapter 5.
*   **Difference** <note>The classification or distinction between types of celestial bodies.</note>; they are either:
    *   **Fixed stars**, concerning which see Chapter 3.
    *   **Planets**, concerning which see Chapter 9. In these, three things are noted, namely their:
        *   **Nature**, Chapter 10.
        *   **Influence** <term>Influence: In historical cosmology, this refers to the "flowing in" of power from the stars that was believed to affect human health and earthly events.</term>, Chapter 12.
        *   **Motion**, Chapters 13, 14, and 15.
*   The **Method of retention** <note>original: "Modus retentionis"; how each celestial body is held or maintained in its proper position.</note> of each in its own place, concerning which see Chapter 4.

### CHAPTER I.

->*On the substance, nature, and arrangement of the stellar body.*<-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'O' depicting stylized foliage and floral motifs within a square frame.</image-desc>

Almost all philosophers agree on this point: that the stellar bodies <note>The stars and planets.</note> are made of the specific substance of the middle heaven. Aristotle also, in his book *On the Heavens*, maintains that the stars are composed of the matter of the heaven in which they are placed. We, however, do not entirely agree with them; we say that the solar body is derived from a dark mass driven out from the higher heaven, as we shall declare in its proper place below. As for the constitution of the other stars, it is necessary that some degree of density and opacity <note>The quality of not letting light pass through; the opposite of transparency.</note> be present in the stellar body, so that its presence may be made visible. This density could by no means be found in the thin substance of the heaven itself. And this is exactly what those most skilled in the science of **Optics** have maintained.

For Al-Ghazali <note>original: "Algazel"; an influential Persian philosopher and theologian (c. 1058–1111).</note> says in his work *On the Nature of the Mirror*, *that no part of the heavenly sphere is an object of the power of sight, nor capable of receiving and retaining the light of the sun, except for its denser part, which we call the star itself.* He provides the reason that because the rest of the sphere is more "rare" <note>Thinner or less dense.</note> than the light projected toward it, it does not become bright—that is, it is not illuminated—because it is not subject to color, and consequently is not visible. For Alhazen <note>original: "Hatten"; referring to Ibn al-Haytham (c. 965–1040), a pioneering physicist often called the "father of modern optics."</note> says in Book 1 *On Optics*, *that which has no color is not visible, because,* he says, *a visible thing exists only by virtue of the light in the thing seen, and visible light only occupies that which is colored.* 

Furthermore, the aforementioned Alhazen gives another reason why the thinner part of the sphere is not seen: namely, because it is necessary that the thing seen be "resistant" to sight. That is, it must not be transparent; or if it is, it must be much thicker than the transparency of the air between the eye and the object. But such a subject cannot exist unless it possesses color or something similar to it. 

From these points, it is certain that a body which is somewhat opaque and colored is required to receive and retain rays of light. The thinner matter of the middle heaven could not be of this kind, as it was created on the second day <note>A reference to the Biblical creation account in Genesis.</note>, because of its excessive transparency. Rather, after the Sun was positioned in the "heart of heaven" on the fourth day, the nature of the Sun converged to form all the other **wandering stars** <note>The planets; the word "planet" comes from the Greek "planētēs," meaning "wanderer."</note>. For the Sun attracted the more subtle parts of the lowest heaven upward toward itself and joined them with the substance of the middle heaven, gathering together the bodies of the other planets that differ in their positions, as we will teach a little further below. 

But because some matter is more suitable than others for receiving light, we believe it must be asked what kind of substance nature especially chose as being most fit for forming stellar bodies. It is necessary that they lean toward the nature and arrangement of the place where they are enclosed; hence, the substance must be free from all earthly filth and opacity (except for a small amount of darkness to blunt its excessive transparency). Although the solar body is in some way forged from the spiritual matter of the earth's shadow, it must nevertheless cast off its putrid and elemental form, so that it may better and more easily take on a new and heavenly one. For we see *decaying wood*, most of which is known to be air or "spirit" because it floats on water, attracting to itself in the nighttime the light invisibly dispersed through the air, as if they were seized by a most greedy desire to take on a luminous form; which indeed previously—

# TREATISE I. BOOK V.

before they were affected by that putrefaction, they could in no way provide light. Similarly, the *scales* of certain fish do this same thing; for on account of their moisture, which easily rots in the heat of the air, they make manifest the invisible light by a certain magnetic force. There are also other bodies which, because of the complete perfection of their substance, naturally attract solar rays to themselves, and firmly retain those they have attracted. For *according to the merit of the matter*, as *Plato* says, *the form is given*, since form acts according to the disposition of the matter. <note>original: "secundum meritum materiæ... datur forma"; a philosophical principle suggesting that a substance's physical state determines what higher qualities it can receive.</note> Therefore, *gold* and the *Carbuncle* <note>A legendary red gemstone, often identified as a ruby or garnet, believed to emit its own light.</note> are of this species. There are still some other bodies which, though they are not perfect, nevertheless have a certain spirit within them, so close to the body of the Fifth Essence <note>The "Quintessence" or ether, the pure, incorruptible matter of the heavens.</note> because of the purity of their disposition, that they are perceived to shine at night; such is that *Glow-worm* whose part makes its whole worm-like body visible in the darkness. Other bodies are found which, although they are transparent, are accustomed to attract solar rays so evidently that they pass from a crystalline form to a likeness of a Ruby; such a spiritual humor I myself have extracted from a most noble body, and I observed its wondrous transmutation in the Sun; indeed, I remember seeing that spirit, because of its natural heat acquired from the sun, ascend into the head of the alembic <note>A chemical vessel used for distillation.</note> like a most shining Ruby, by means of its newly assumed tincture, without any violence of elemental fire. But, to speak truly, after the entry of the solar light into that Spirit—or rather, the mating of the male with the female—it had been rendered somewhat thicker and more opaque: Whence the ignorance of those is plain, who have thought the light of the sun to be a mere accident <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a property that does not exist by itself (like the color of an apple), whereas the author argues light is a material "substance."</note> and not entirely material.

But so that we may approach the matter even more closely, and ascend as if by three steps to the summit of the middle heaven, we shall deal briefly first with the *meteor*, which they call a falling star; second, with the matter of the *Comet*; and finally, with the substance and constitution of the *Milky Way* or *Galaxy*.

Now, while I was once walking through the fields at night under a clear sky, I saw a *meteor* star falling near me; after a wakeful search, I finally found its substance, handled it with my hands, and kept it for the next day; then, looking at it carefully at the following dawn, I observed that it was composed of a very viscous and slippery substance, and had the consistency of frog spawn. For its matter was whitish and transparent in an obscure way, stained with many tiny dark spots. Without doubt, therefore, the substance of this thing was made from vapor enclosing a terrestrial spirit; which was not set on fire, as some would have it; but, receiving the brightness of the stellar rays in the manner of a mirror—just as stars represent the brightness of the spiritual light of heaven and solar clarity—it represented the true image of a natural star and its brightness. And without doubt those spots, which were included in its cloudy transparency, made that transparent matter more fit for receiving the rays of light into its substance; indeed, if I am not mistaken, they serve as wicks to the transparent humor, providing light to it in no other way than those wicks provide flame to the oil of a lamp. For this same thing can be seen in the substance of the moon; in whose body denser and darker parts appear even to our eyes, not much unlike the branches of some tree.

Does that which the Philosophers tell of the substance and constitution of the *Comet* differ much from this? saying, *a Comet is a subtle exhalation, not set on fire, but shining from the light of the sun and stars incorporated within it*; <note>The author is challenging the Aristotelian view that comets are burning vapors in the upper atmosphere, suggesting instead they are reflective or "light-bearing" matter.</note> which exhalation they say is generated in the bowels of the earth, especially when the pores are constricted by cold, but exits when the aforementioned pores are loosened and opened by heat.

Finally, what do the Physicists say about that *milky path* stretched across the convexity of the middle heaven through the feet of Gemini and the beginning of Sagittarius, which they name the *Galaxy*? Which part of heaven, if it were as thin and transparent as its other parts, by reasons...

He then transitions to explaining the nature of comets and the Milky Way as reflective terrestrial exhalations.</summary>

Similarly, if this white path <note>original: "via hæc alba"; referring to the Milky Way.</note> were as thick as the bodies of the fixed stars placed near it, it would undoubtedly shine through its whole substance no less than any of the aforementioned stars. It is manifest, therefore, that this Galaxy <note>term: "Galaxia" — the Milky Way.</note> holds a middle dimension and opacity between the body of the crystalline heaven itself and the stars fixed in its concave surface; for this reason, it is sufficiently visible, colored, and moderately bright. And from this it must be observed that the more a stellar body tends toward transparency, the more obscure and less bright it is. This is manifest in fixed stars of the fifth magnitude, which are called "nebulae" <note>In early modern astronomy, "nebulae" or "cloudy stars" referred to faint, blurry stars or clusters visible to the naked eye, rather than the gas clouds we recognize today.</note> because of the darkness resulting from their excessive transparency; for they are not much denser than the Galaxy, and therefore they do not greatly excel in clarity. From these points, the reason is readily apparent why some fixed stars appear brighter than others. We conclude, therefore, that the matter of a falling star <note>original: "stellæ cadentis"; a meteor.</note> is the same in kind as that of a Comet; and that of a Comet the same as the Galaxy; and that of the Galaxy the same as the stars, both fixed and wandering <note>term: "erraticarum" — the planets, which appear to "wander" against the background of fixed stars.</note>; and that all their differences are taken from a greater or lesser degree of density, transparency, and purity. We have frequently emphasized above that these varieties of matter flow from a greater or lesser portion of "form" <note>In this philosophical context, "form" refers to the organizing principle or "light" that gives matter its specific properties.</note>.

## CHAPTER II.

->That the stellar bodies are not corruptible.<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut drop cap 'Q' featuring a central rose or floral motif surrounded by intricate leafy flourishes and scrollwork.</image-desc>Although every substance of this world, both visible and invisible, arises from prime matter <note>term: "materia prima" — the fundamental, formless substance from which all physical things are made.</note> now brought into action, which is suited by its nature to desire "form," and for this reason is always changeable and corruptible where that form is found to be weaker; nevertheless, if it ever reaches a place where it finds a form sufficient to fulfill its desire, then it certainly rests—that is, it desires nothing further. For every natural thing has an inclination toward perfection, in which its desire finds its end and completion. But truly, since the measure of all perfection consists in the essence of formal light, matter rejoices in its embrace and is, as it were, snatched up into heaven; for unless matter is in heaven, its desire is by no means fulfilled. Hence, I say, it is that although the bodies of the stars—and indeed the very substance of the entire heaven—might be judged corruptible in themselves (since they were formed from the dark substance of universal *hyle* <note>original: "hylæ"; a Greek-derived term for the primordial "stuff" or matter of the universe.</note> and brought into action by the presence of light), they are nonetheless preserved. Because of the presence of a proportional form—where there are two parts of light and just as many of matter—it is necessary that the thickness of that substance be fulfilled in its desire and rendered perfect. Just as also, where the proportion of form exceeds the matter, it is said to be "more than perfection" and spirituality; a substance of this kind is found beyond the convex surface of the etheric heaven. From this it is clear that both the form of the middle heaven, called "secondary light," and that of the spiritual heaven, protect whatever substances are found in their regions from rot and the injuries of corruption, preserving them always in the same state and making them perpetual according to the laws of nature; although it is necessary that the continuation of every thing depends on the eternal will of GOD, to whose command and disposition alone all things are subject.

<meta>catchword CA.</meta>

TREATISE I. BOOK V.

# CHAPTER III.

->*That it is probable the fixed stars were made on the second day of creation;*<-
->*Of their origin, location, and diversity, both in size*<-
->*and in splendor.*<-

In the preceding sections, it was demonstrated that light was created on the first day, filling the entire concave surface of the highest heaven. In this way, the light inhabiting the supreme region—which we have called primary light—was separated from the darkness, which that light condensed in the lower parts. It did this by driving that darkness of the upper region toward the lower parts and the center of the whole through its presence and action. Thus, the first day (that is, the first light) occupied the Empyrean heaven <note>The Empyrean was considered the highest, most spiritual part of the universe, often associated with the presence of God.</note>, and night (that is, darkness) occupied all that space placed between the base of this heaven and the center of the whole. Then, since the light in its spiritual heaven was already established as most active and never resting, it acted most sharply on the second day against its opposite—namely, the dark matter thrust below the base of the supreme heaven—hostilely repelling it downward from its boundaries as its greatest adversary. Therefore, innumerable parts of this primary light penetrated the diaphragm <note>original: "diaphragma"; here used to mean a dividing membrane or partition between celestial layers.</note> of the crystalline heaven, disturbing the rest of the darkness in the middle region. Although this darkness resisted the invasions of the lucid rays strongly enough, it was nevertheless compelled to descend little by little in an oblique revolution down to the concavity of the lower heaven, where it joined with those darknesses. Consequently, the mass of darkness in the lowest region was made very thick, dense, and opaque, because the darkness of the other heavens was mixed with it.

And so on the second day, there was "day" in both upper regions, which are now called the heavens; but "night" possessed the lowest region. That light which we have called secondary—being immediately derived from the primary light—filled the entire concavity of the middle heaven; hence the substance of that region is called ether <note>term: "Aether" — believed to be a refined, fifth element that filled the space of the heavens above the terrestrial sphere.</note>. Finally, on the third day, some rays of the secondary light now illuminating this region—namely, a third part of it—penetrated into the lowest region which was as yet unformed. These rays reverberated all its sooty and even blackest vapors around the center of the universal machine <note>original: "machinæ universæ"; a common early modern metaphor for the structured universe.</note>. With the assistance of the element of fire (arising accidentally from the motion of the heavens and the bending of the rays), these vapors were constricted into a hard mass by the power of the cold which the matter contains within itself.

Now, therefore, a great doubt may arise: by what means was that ethereal heaven illuminated on the second day, and the lowest heaven on the third, since we have stated elsewhere that light is invisible in itself? We have said that light is abundantly present in this heaven as well as in the middle heaven—existing copiously in every part of it—even at night, and yet it is in no way apparent to the eyes. Therefore, so that a distinction may be made between darkness and light, it is necessary that an object of sight be required, both privative <note>A "privative" object refers to the absence of something (like darkness being the absence of light).</note> and positive; this cannot be had in invisible things. For this reason, it is necessary that that invisible light be produced visibly. For this to happen, certain bodies are required to act as organs and polished mirrors, manifesting the form that lurks invisibly in the medium or common spirit. The substance of these bodies must be fit and naturally disposed to gather together and multiply the multitude of secretly lurking lucid rays. Such are the bodies of the planets and stars, as we explained in the first chapter of this book. Since, however, in the middle heaven—

<summary>This page explains how primary light created the Empyrean on the first day and how, on the second and third days, it pushed darkness toward the center to form the lower heavens and the Earth. It introduces the problem of light's inherent invisibility and argues that stars were created as "mirrors" to make this light visible to the eye.</summary>

<keywords>creation, second day, Empyrean, ether, secondary light, celestial mirrors, visibility of light, fixed stars, physics of darkness</keywords>

<header>CONCERNING THE CREATURES OF THE ETHEREAL HEAVEN.</header>

...middle region, immediately after its purification from its dark filth, no such bodies are found except those of the stars, due to the thinness of the other parts. It is necessary, therefore, that certain stellar bodies serve as the organs and instruments through which that secondary light—which is otherwise only luminous to itself—may visibly show itself to the other substances of the world that are to be illuminated. For this reason, it does not seem far from the truth that the fixed stars were made on the second day, before the completion of the creation of the ethereal heaven <note>The "ethereal heaven" or *caelum aethereum* was the region above the moon where the stars and planets resided, thought to be made of a fifth element called ether.</note>, since it does not seem consistent with reason that a visible day should appear in this heaven without them. 

For they are like breasts, through which divine Nectar, super-celestial Ambrosia, or luminous milk, is poured down into the lower regions, as if from a nipple overflowing with light. Thus the "son of the world"—whom they call the Sun or Phoebus <note>original: "Phœbum"; a classical name for the sun god Apollo, used here as an astrological metaphor for the Sun.</note>—is nourished and suckled after his birth. Indeed, this kind of celestial milk is prepared in the breasts of the upper heaven, and its teats swell before the birth of the Sun, just as a woman's breasts swell before she gives birth.

*But here someone will object that it was not so necessary for light to appear visibly on the second and third day, since sensible beings <note>Beings capable of perception through the senses, such as animals or humans.</note> had not yet been created?* To this, we can respond in three ways: *First*, that day could not be distinguished from night, nor light from darkness, without a visible manifestation of light; *Second*, because the celestial light descended visibly and sensibly toward the center to expel the darkness and to provide for the growth of plants rising from the earth—since three things are required for their essence: light, heat, and influence; *Third and finally*, because the Sun appeared visibly in the heaven even before the production of animals, and consequently, it is not necessary to require the presence of a sense [a perceiver] for a thing to be visible. From these points, it is manifest that light was manifest and visible before the creation of seeing animals. 

Nor indeed will it suffice for such doubters to assert that the holy Scriptures of Moses <note>Referring to the Book of Genesis.</note> are contrary to this opinion, in which it is found that **GOD** made two great lights on the fourth day, and that the other stars were also made on the same day? For here it is likely that the Holy Overseer [Moses] meant the other Planets or "wandering stars" <note>original: "stellas erraticas"; the planets were called wandering stars because their positions change relative to the fixed stars.</note>, since immediately before he had mentioned the two principal ones. *Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary figure representing ancient wisdom; his "Sacred Discourse" was highly influential in Renaissance philosophy.</note> also seems to verify this in his sacred discourse, where he says that when all things were in a state of confusion, the light elements were carried to the higher regions by a fiery spirit, and immediately the heaven shone forth in seven circles;* from which it was manifest that the seven Planets were made immediately after the ascent of fire into the highest region; however, he makes no mention of the other stars. Furthermore, the order would be irregular if the Empyrean heaven <note>The highest, most spiritual heaven.</note> and its creatures were created on the same day, and similarly, the earth and its creatures were brought forth together, yet the middle heaven was granted no inhabitants on the day of its creation.

But perhaps someone will doubt the manner in which such a mass of stars was forged; *namely, by what path or from what substance their bodies could be produced, and raised on the second day into that place which is called the firmament <note>The sphere of the fixed stars.</note>, and there remain fixed as if inherent;* similarly, by what reason they are distinguished by such diversity of light and size of body? To which, so that I may respond to all points probably and philosophically, it must be known that immediately after the penetration of light through the diaphragm of the crystalline sphere <note>original: "sphæræ cristallinæ"; a celestial layer believed by some to exist between the firmament and the Empyrean.</note>, as if through a sieve, a great conflict arose between the rays of light and the matter of dark shadow found there. This happened both because of the actions of the one substance and the inertia <note>original: "stupiditatem"; in early physics, this refers to the lack of inherent motion in matter.</note> and forced movement of the other, and also because of their contrary qualities. For the rays of light acted strenuously upon the passive substance of darkness, pushing it downward and following it so greedily and vividly that sometimes some part of the light was submerged in the bowels of the shadow. It was captured as if in the snares of a certain viscous Spirit, which is the true matter of shadow. The adjacent rays, trying to redeem and, as it were, free their brother from prison, raised it up on high by the aid of this...

It also reconciles this timeline with the Book of Genesis and Hermetic philosophy, explaining that Moses' 'fourth day' refers specifically to the planets.</summary>

# TREATISE I. BOOK V.

...reason, both from the resisting matter and from the attractive faculty of its own heat. That part of the imprisoned light, although it was liberated from the entire mass of darkness by the magnetic virtue of light, was nevertheless so imbued with that thick and viscous Spirit that it could never be freed from it. Since matter naturally desires its own form <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "form" is the organizing principle or "soul" of an object; matter is the raw substance that "hungers" for form to become a specific thing.</note> and retains what it has attracted, this matter—along with the very part of the light it contains—is carried on high, like a body moved by its soul. It rises like the substance of a **Comet** to the concave surface of the **crystalline sphere** <note>The crystalline sphere was traditionally the ninth sphere in the medieval cosmos, thought to be made of a clear, water-like substance.</note>. There, by the freezing power of that sphere, it is coagulated and hardened into the form of a dark crystal, or perhaps with even darker spots of shadow tending toward opacity (just as we find in the matter of a **falling star**). It adheres to the substance of that sphere like ice. Because of its thickness, the darkened rays of light passing through the "sieve" of that sphere strike against these icy and crystalline globules in their passage; they are then presented to the visual power as if in a mirror.

Similarly, **formal light** <note>original: "lux formalis"; the essential, spiritual light that gives shape and existence to things.</note> is attracted from every part of the middle heaven to fulfill its desire. Thus, as the nature of the middle heaven is, so too is its luminous form. The composition of this heaven is forged from an equal proportion of the material heaven and the spiritual heaven. Therefore, if the matter of the heaven is invisible, it is necessary that its light also be invisible; for "being visible" proceeds from the visibility and appearance of light, and the visibility of light comes from a colored material object. But because every part of the middle heaven is forged from an equal substance of matter and light, it is necessary that where the matter is invisible due to its thinness, the light also shines there invisibly and hiddenly. Such matter is too transparent and bright, and consequently has no color. However, the thicker the matter is, the more visible its light will be. For every dimension of this heaven never ceases to desire until its appetite is filled with an equal part of light.

Therefore, every part of it—even the thicker ones—has a portion of light similar to itself, by which it may be led toward the goal of perfection and freed from the door of corruption. If, then, the matter is visible because of its dimension, it is necessary, I say, that its formal light also appear to the eyes. And this is why those stellar bodies are more or less luminous according to their greater or lesser thickness, and are more subject to the visual power than the other parts of the **ethereal heaven** because of their clarity. The probability of this is shown by a common demonstration: for thin air is invisible by itself because of its subtlety; but if it is condensed into a cloud or mist by the force of cold, it takes on a visible form. It sometimes becomes bright and gleaming with radiance, sometimes whiter, and sometimes fiery, or tending toward blackness or a leaden hue, according to the greater or lesser dominance of light within it. 

The fact that the body of one star is larger and denser than another happens according to the quantity of the viscous matter enclosing the light; just as we see in this lower heaven larger or smaller **vapors** or **exhalations** rise up, according to the more effective power of the light (or soul) enclosed in them, which is capable of lifting a larger or smaller weight upwards. Why the substance of one star is less bright than another we explained the reason above: we said that it is either brighter or darker because of its greater or lesser opacity, according to the nature and disposition of the celestial matter. The position of the **fixed stars** on the concave surface of the crystalline heaven is described in this way:

### CHAPTER IV.

->*On the bodies of the Planets, on their positions, and how they are always sustained in the same place—that is, why they do not tend further downward at any time, since, on account of the density of their matter, they are in some way heavy?*<-

To follow in the footsteps of the preceding arguments, it is certain that not only the **fixed stars**, but also those wandering ones, which are called **Planets**, are forged from a certain condensed **spirit** <note>In this context, "spirit" refers to a subtle, ethereal substance that acts as a bridge between pure soul/light and physical matter.</note> containing a spark of light, though they are formed by a different method and a dissimilar action.

For we recall from above that the **fixed stars** were made from the conflict of light pursuing the dark shadow, combined with the thicker, colder, and somewhat obscure matter of that same shadow. We declared their position and site to be in the lower part of the **crystalline heaven** <note>Also called the "watery sphere," this was considered the ninth sphere in the traditional geocentric model, located above the stars.</note>, where they are fixed and, as it were, frozen. Because of the "earthy" disposition of that sphere—namely, its coldness and dryness—their moist bodies are firmly glued, like ice, toward the concave surface of their sphere. For this reason, those stars are perceived to move according to the motion of the **watery sphere**, and in their motion, they always maintain the same distance from one another; for which rea-

# BOOK V OF THE TREATISE.

...now called fixed. But truly, the bodies of the three superior Planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>, and likewise the three inferior ones <note>Venus, Mercury, and the Moon</note>, arose immediately from the creation of the bright, flashing crown of their own King <note>Fludd refers here to the Sun as the "King" and heart of the solar system</note>. For after the purer **Spirit of darkness**—that part of the shadows driven toward the center—had become impregnated and heavy with a most splendid portion of the light of the same region, it gave birth to and produced an offspring, as if animated, by the aid of super-celestial and celestial rays. 

That earthly opacity—born from rays of light invisibly dispersed in the world-spirit as well as those visible rays of the fixed stars (which were then only shining obscurely because of the thickness of the **Spirit of the middle heaven**) —is attracted upward. Partly by the power of its own soul, or the spark of its essential light—whose nature is to seek only its own native origin—it is sublimated as far as the royal throne in the midpoint of the middle heaven. There, it is exalted in dignity, splendor, and clarity due to the multitude of invisible rays gathered to it. Through the presence of its thick and corporeal spirit, it is manifested to the world and held as the most beautiful creature of the entire universe; for without its benevolent operations, no life, sense, motion, growth, or multiplication would exist in creatures. 

By the presence of this **royal Planet** <note>The Sun</note>, I say, and by its light and the operation of that light, the globular bodies of the other Planets are forged from the thicker spirit of the middle heaven. Step by step, one above the other in a position and site that can in no way be altered, they are sustained in this middle heaven. According to the variety of their position and site, and depending on their proximity to or distance from the solar body, their natures vary. From this, therefore, comes the clarity and transparency of the celestial spheres; and from this comes the thickness of the astral bodies and the collection of the denser matter of each of the aforesaid spheres into a single round mass, as we shall declare more fully in its proper place.

But here a great controversy may arise: *namely, why do these orbicular bodies, since they are thick and consequently heavy, not tend downward but always remain in the same state and position?* To which it must be answered: **firstly**, that the body of every star hangs in its own proper place and is sustained by the divine power of **GOD**. For thus the divine will wished: to unite the matter and spirit of every sphere in such proportions that each was destined to occupy its own natural place. Hence, if the aforesaid bodies were lighter and more subtle, they would tend further upward; if, however, they were too ponderous, they would fall further downward. 

Wherefore, each of these bodies has a **luminous soul**, by whose immortal and never-decreasing power the body is exalted and raised in the same state and the same height. Because of the permanence of the luminous soul within it, the body shall never be depressed or corrupted, except at the very end of the world’s age. We see this also in that **artificial fire** which they call a rocket <note>original: "fusum", likely referring to a pyrotechnic projectile or firework</note>; by the force of a certain fiery spirit by which it is moved upward, it is sustained fixedly in that same place in the air until its artificial soul—which is destined to perish quickly—fails. For then its body returns downward. 

This same thing is also seen in a **cannonball** <note>original: "globo bombardæ"</note>, whose upward motion is perceived as longer or shorter according to the greater or lesser vigor of the artificial fire ejecting it. Indeed, if its motive power were not transitory but fixed, it would never return from the target toward which it is moved and sent upward, but by the power of its moving soul, it would remain firmly and constantly on high. But when that power fails, the metallic matter, having been raised upward against its nature, naturally tends toward the center of the whole. From these things, therefore, it can be directly gathered that there are two natural causes for the elevation or depression of a Planetary body made in the ethereal heaven on the fourth day of creation: namely, the proportion or weight of the **spiritual matter**, and the abundance or lack of the **luminous form**. For a tiny amount of light will not elevate or keep a body fixedly raised unless there is only a tiny amount of matter; but a multitude of form <note>In Aristotelian terms, "form" here provides the active, rising principle, while "matter" provides the passive, heavy principle.</note> will sublimate a great quantity of matter as if with fiery wings. If, however, the matter...

## CHAPTER V.

### *That every Spirit of darkness carries away a small portion of the bright essence of its region; Concerning the generation of minerals and vegetables; That the work of the third day is like a prelude to that of the following day.*

**O**n the first day of creation, immediately after the production of the essential light in the higher heaven, it was necessary that the darkness of that same region should give way to the substance of that light (which was contrary to it) and be thrust downward by its life-giving activity into the depths of the second and third regions. This happened in such a way, however, that some part of the aforementioned light was held captive in the belly of that darkness, due to its deep penetration into the spiritual matter of those shadows. Likewise, on the second day of creation, the darkness of the middle region was driven down into the lowest region by the active power of the bright rays penetrating them (which we called "secondary light" above); and for the reasons mentioned, they also hold a portion of those rays imprisoned in the lap of their spiritual matter. Finally, on the third day, the three species of darkness, joined and gathered together in the lower place—constituting the **Chaos of the Philosophers**, <note>A term from alchemy referring to the raw, unrefined matter of the universe before it is organized into specific forms.</note> rude and undigested—were reflected back toward the mathematical center of the machine by the power of the third light, with the help of the **sphere of fire**. <note>In the Aristotelian and Fluddian cosmos, the sphere of fire is the highest of the four elemental layers, situated just below the moon.</note> They were then constricted into a heavy, dense, and opaque mass called the "physical center of the world" <note>The Earth</note>. Moreover, the evacuation of not only the darkness but also the matters included within them from the aforementioned heavens was entirely necessary; for without it, the operations of light—which are to purify, illuminate, and refine—could in no way have been completed in those regions of the world. Therefore, from the retention of light in the spiritual matter of the lowest darkness (which matter is the coarsest of all), metals and minerals of every kind are procreated. In the same way, from the greater or lesser sparks of that same light in the spiritual matter of the darkness of the middle region, different species of vegetables arise, and growing plants spring up from the earth. For because the light of the middle heaven was not as effective as that primary light which is enclosed in the spirit of the darkness of the first region, it consequently does not have the same benefit of heating, refining, and dispersing; therefore, the thickness of the earth and the moisture of the water resist it more. But, since it is the nature of every bright thing to strive upward as if toward its natural fatherland, and because the rays of every heaven (both visible and invisible) join themselves by penetration with the imprisoned rays—by which union the strength of both is made stronger—it follows that this **light-soul** <note>original: "animalucida." A term likely coined by Fludd to describe the vital, luminous spark trapped in matter that drives growth and upward movement.</note> enclosed in the spirit of the middle darkness gradually strives upward and raises itself from the lap of the earth. Since this light is most familiar with the element of fire, and bound to it by a certain proximity as if to its "bastard brother," it is necessary in its ascent that a portion of the element of fire should accompany it. Furthermore, because the sphere of fire is contiguous to the middle spirit, which is the vehicle of the aforementioned light, the element of fire adheres to it by the bonds of nature and is raised above the earth with it. And because the element of air is contiguous and annexed to the sphere of fire, therefore no [ascent] could...

Finally, since the earth, to which water is naturally linked, is very heavy and resistant to motion, it must be that it is the chief obstacle holding back the plant’s soul, preventing it from ascending higher toward its natural seat; although the thickness of the other elements also seems to perform this in some way. Finally, with all these elements coming together for the composition of the plant, a certain impurity of the earth is joined, infecting the whole body of the plant more or less, by which it is rendered dense and opaque. This happens because the quantity of fruitful light included in it was not sufficient to remove those impurities of the constituent elements; if it had perfected this, it would have ascended higher in the manner of a solar spirit <note>In this context, the "solar spirit" refers to a refined, light-filled substance capable of rising to the heavens, unlike the heavy, "infected" matter of common plants.</note>. This, then, was the end of the work of the third day, and as it were a preparation for the work of the following day.

# CHAPTER VI.

->On the rising and origin of the Sun, and the spiritual purification of its body in the unrefined earth, and its ascent or elevation into the heart and midpoint of the heaven.<-

That immortal light, which we spoke of above, veiled in darkness along with the excess and thicker spirit of the Empyrean heaven <note>The Empyrean is the highest part of the universe in ancient and medieval cosmology, believed to be filled with the element of fire or pure light.</note>, was snatched away to the center of the universal machine—specifically when the raw and unrefined chaos, forged from a triple mass of darkness with a triple kind of light, was collected at the center of the machine. In its own proper vessel and vehicle, casting off every form of corruptibility, it is raised into the heart of the middle heaven, according to the opinion of *Hippocrates, who speaks in his book On Principles in almost these words: The greatest part of that immortal thing, which we call heat, when all things were disturbed, withdrew to that supreme region, which the ancients seem to call the aether.* <note>original: "Plurima illius immortalis pars, quod calidum vocamus... ad supremam illam regionem recessit..." Hippocrates was a famous Greek physician, but writers of this period often cited him as a philosopher of nature.</note> And certainly it was necessary for such deformed spirits to descend to the center, since they could deposit their obscurities and impurities in no other place except in that sole seat of corruptibility and darkness, namely the unrefined mass which we called earth. Similarly, it was also necessary that the bright soul be contained in such a spirit, since without its action that spirit or body could not have been purified and regenerated from its horrible filth, nor raised into its ethereal seat. Nothing is made entirely clean, or raised to the height of perfection, if portions of light are either altogether lacking or present in too small a quantity. Hence it is that *Trismegistus in his sacred discourse writes thus: When all things were unrefined, all things began to be distinguished and weighed, and light things were carried by a fiery spirit, and flew forth into the high region, and immediately the heaven shone forth in seven circles.* <note>original: "Cum omnia essent indigesta... leviaque igneo spiritu vehebantur... et immediatè emicuit cœlum septem in circulis." Trismegistus refers to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy and Hermetic philosophy. The "seven circles" are the orbits of the seven classical planets.</note> For these reasons and others of this kind, it is probable that by the will of infinite nature, the denser part of each heaven, overwhelmed by darkness with some particles of its own light enclosed in the hiding places of its shadows, fell headlong to the center of the universal structure, the rays of the primary light acting upon it, and was condensed into an earthy mass by the constrictive power of cold. In this mass, the lighter spirit of the darkness of the ultimate heaven is considered purer and simpler than all the rest, both because of the excellence of the place from which it flowed downward, and also because of the nobility of the fire or light which it includes. But truly that light, although most noble in itself, yet because of the perpetual night in the belly of the earth...

## ON THE CREATURES OF THE ETHEREAL HEAVEN.

...presence and governance before the fourth day was greatly weakened. For the cold of the earth still repelled the action and heat of the shining rays of each heaven, and resisting their penetrating power, it strongly constricted the pores of its body. This resistance of the cold, however, changed around the fourth day. This was partly due to the multitude of descending rays—which exert their operations more and more on each day of creation—and partly due to the heating of the sphere of fire, which was gradually inflamed daily by the rotation of the middle heaven. Consequently, this heat pushed the terrestrial cold more effectively toward the center of the earth's body. Finally, because of the stronger resistance of the condensed earth, the descending rays were more vehemently reflected; by this vigorous reflection, a greater effect of heat was stirred up, and the terrestrial mass was suppressed and pierced. Its pores were opened and dilated, rays were joined to rays, and strength was united with strength, so that the super-celestial light—once captured and made weak—now emerged freely from the womb of the earth. Freed from its dark prison, it was endowed with its former strength and tended freely toward its homeland in the heights.

But since this kind of vessel <note>The "receptaculum" or vessel refers to the material body that carries the spiritual light.</note> is like a medium for the shining soul, through which it was carried upward (after it was purified) as well as downward (while it was burdened by the impurity of darkness), it was necessary that this vehicle also be elevated with its ray. For the body was to the light as matter is to its form; indeed, this exaltation could not be completed until it had cast off the filthy impurity of darkness, since no unclean thing can communicate with the purest substance of the middle heaven. 

Nor could this mass be hindered in its ascent by the substances of the inferior elements, unlike that quintessence <term>quinta essentia: the "fifth element" or purest substance, distinct from earth, air, fire, and water, believed to make up the celestial bodies</term> from which plants arise. The substance of plants is naturally touching the sphere of fire and consequently is bound to all the other elements which are linked together, as we said above. Instead, this solar mass is exalted to the ethers without any hindrance from the nature of the lower heaven, because its spiritual substance was a denser part of the Empyrean heaven <note>The Empyrean is the highest, most spiritual realm of the universe.</note>. Between the Empyrean and these lower realms, the ethereal spirit or quintessence is placed. From the denser part of this spirit, combined with its own darkness and attracted to the center, plants are formed (if it includes some sparks of light) and air is formed (if it possesses nothing of the substance of light). 

And hence it is that just as in plants the thick ethereal spirit is bound to fire in its ascent, so also it is necessary that this thicker super-celestial mass be connected by the bonds of nature to the thicker part of the ethereal mass during its elevation. With this mass, the elements mix more or less, and especially Air. The reason why these substances of each heaven cling to each other in this gradual way is the extreme of desire, by which the matter of one heaven, in ascending, longs to seize and hold the form of another; for every spirit, of whatever density, naturally tends toward the goal of perfection. 

Therefore, this entire super-celestial mass was carried upward on the fourth day by means of its soul, distributing any more impure dregs of its already cleansed mass to the sphere of fire as it ascended. It was elevated to the middle of the ethereal heaven in the form of a most polished body, attracting the ethereal body from all sides by a magnetic force, which makes it appear more splendid (as is clearly explained in the experiment described below). There, it holds the scepter and rudder of the entire visible world, encompasses the world's action within itself, and acts as the Universal Emperor in that place, claiming for itself the names of **Phœbus, Apollo, Titan, and the Sun.**

<note>original: "creatio, quinta essentia, sphæra ignis, cœlum æthereum, Phœbus, Apollo, Titan, Sol, massa supercœlestis, spiritus æthereus"</note>
