### TREATISE I. BOOK V.

<image-desc>A large circular cosmological diagram dominated by a dark central sphere or void. Above this central darkness, a radiant sun with a human face emits light rays. A funnel-like shadow or passage descends from the radiant sun towards the central blackness. The surrounding space of the large circle is filled with dense, concentric rings composed of fine, irregular hatched lines that create a vibrating, textured effect, perhaps representing celestial spheres or elemental matter. Radiating lines also extend outward from the very top of the diagram behind the small sun.</image-desc>

For this mass, triumphing in the heart of the middle heaven <note>The "middle heaven" refers to the ethereal region between the supreme, fiery heaven and the lower elemental world; this is the proper home of the Sun.</note> because of the purity of its substance and the simplicity of its nature—since it is of the same condition as the spirit of the shining fountain <note>original: "fons lucidus," referring to the divine source of light</note>—avidly attracts to itself the rays distilling from that shining fountain. These rays flow through the breasts of its diaphragm <note>The author uses anatomical metaphors to describe the cosmos as a living body; the "diaphragm" here represents a structural division in the heavens through which divine influence passes.</note> and are dispersed throughout the whole middle heaven by that <term>magnetic virtue</term> <note>A natural power of attraction where matter seeks out its "form" or spiritual essence to achieve completeness.</note> by which matter desires form for its own perfection. Indeed, even these rays of primary light are drawn toward the center of the spiritual mass where their "brother" <note>The "brother" is the spark of light that was previously trapped in the darkness of the lower world but has now been purified and elevated.</note> holds his dwelling; for like naturally rejoices in its like, and there they celebrate his fraternal liberty and his return from the Tartarean <note>Tartarean refers to the dark, hellish depths of primeval matter or the center of the earth.</note> realms with a kind of fiery love.

Therefore, once a great quantity of such light has been gathered from the fountain of the supreme heaven into the center of that celestial matter (which is of a similar substance), so that one portion of light is weighed in equal scales against the other portion of spirit, it is necessary that this mass—now completely illuminated and visibly beholding the world with a daily and peaceful countenance—be moved perpetually. This movement is caused by the active quality of its <term>form</term> <note>In Aristotelian terms, "form" is the active, organizing principle—in this case, light—while "matter" is the passive recipient.</note> and the passive quality of its matter. However, since the resistance of the matter (which is agitated upward from the center) is equal to the action of the form (which penetrates downward), it is not moved downward. Similarly, it is not moved upward because the operations of the descending rays prohibit any ascent to the middle point of the vaporous matter. From this, it follows that its motion must be circular, as we shall explain further below.

# ON THE CREATURES OF THE ETHEREAL HEAVEN.

But many objections indeed could be brought against this opinion of ours; among which someone might begin by asking: *if the substance of this spiritual mass is naturally from the supreme heaven, why does it not return again to that heaven, since it is of the same nature and condition?* But an answer is most easily applied to this: namely, both because the substance of the superior heaven cannot become thicker due to the immense predominance of light within it, and also because the aforementioned collection of spiritual matter is too dense to ascend higher, yet too thin and spiritual to descend lower. For this solar mass is sustained in the middle of the heaven, partly in respect to its matter, which it received from the earth, but principally by reason of its form. For the spiritual matter of each heaven, which we mentioned before, ascends from the earth into the heaven in a pyramid-like fashion; (*we are not speaking of a mathematical pyramid having a cone and a base, but we understand a certain physical body ascending circularly from a thick central base, becoming ever sharper and thinner toward the circumference*)—it ascends, I say, in a physical, pyramidal way, in the manner of a tree. From the root of a tree, as one ascends, the dimension of its mass always decreases; the lower part of the trunk is thicker than the upper, and the branches are smaller the higher they reach; finally, the leaves and flowers are the thinnest of all its parts.

And so it is also with the **Spiritual Tree of the Heavens**; for the earth is the thickest of all, followed by water, then air, and then fire, which are compared to the small branches and foliage. The planets surrounding the Sun correspond to the flowers, and in the solar body, the true seeds of this tree are collected, through which all natural things flourish and multiply. And certainly, if we look more closely at the anatomy of a tree, we find that a certain "earthly disposition" is the only impediment that prevents its other parts from returning to their proper places <note>In Aristotelian physics, "proper places" are the natural homes of elements: earth at the center, fire at the edge of the atmosphere.</note>; for all the elements are found distinctly within a tree. Indeed, we shall even find a portion of the <term>Fifth Essence</term> <note>original: "Quintæ essentiæ," the "quintessence" or celestial ether that was believed to be the fifth element, forming the stars and the soul.</note> there along with its soul, by whose motion the plant is raised upward.

For if the great mass of any tree were cast into a fire and turned to ashes, we would find the quantity of earth within it to be very small. From this, it certainly seems quite probable that a certain spiritual tree, rising from the earth by the power of the rays of natural light, is freed from its earthly bonds—just like a burnt tree, in which the spiritual parts of whatever thickness are freed by the power of elementary or spiritual fire. By the support of this spiritual tree, the **solar seed** is retained in the heart of the middle heaven, partly in the manner of plant seeds, and partly by the "rein" of its own permanent light (the nature of which is always the same, and therefore retains its body in the same place with the same lasting virtue) because it cannot ascend higher due to its thickness.

That there is such a power in essential light, we are taught by both natural and artificial effects: for animals are moved to the operations of their souls; similarly, we recognize that by the activity of artificial fire, heavy things are carried upward and held there as long as the vigor of that fire continues, and for lack of it, they descend again downward to the natural place of heavy things. We conclude, therefore, that the Sun received its matter from the earth or the condensed and coagulated <term>Chaos</term>, but derived its form from the heaven, descending through the mass of the Sun according to the doctrine of *Hermes* <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of Hermetic philosophy, to whom the *Corpus Hermeticum* is attributed.</note>. We say further that it was necessary for its substance, since it was denser than that of the air, to descend toward the earth, where it might lay aside its shapeless darkness so that it might receive the celestial and cold form without obstacle. For since darkness in the beginning was universally dispersed through the vast gulf of <term>Hyle</term> <note>original: "hyles," the Greek term for primordial, unformed matter.</note>; and since the machine of this world could not be fabricated and informed without gathering the aforementioned deformity into a less worthy place, it was necessary that all the darkness of the world be driven to the center of the machine as the least worthy place; for it is furthest from the seat of light, from which perfection arises. But since darkness in itself is either nothing, or like a mere accident of some subject, and consequently cannot move downward without a vehicle...

# ON THE CREATURES OF THE EMPYREAN HEAVEN.

### CHAPTER VII.

->*Physical and Chemical, as well as Theological reasons defending the probability of the preceding opinion.*<-

<image-desc>Ornate square woodcut initial letter 'Q' featuring leafy, floral scrolls and patterns on a dark background.</image-desc>ALTHOUGH this opinion of ours regarding the origin of the solar body will perhaps seem new and unheard of to the Philosophers of this age; we shall nevertheless strive to demonstrate, by probable arguments and several reasons—both Philosophical and Chemical, as well as Theological—that it is well-founded. For what should be thought concerning the density of the bodies of the Planets, since they possess a dark and in some way opaque form due to the placement of many spirits, so that they might be more capable of receiving visible light? This is no different than how invisible air, when condensed, produces a visible, shining cloud. Since, therefore, according to the said Philosophers, the matter of a hail-cloud evaporates from the womb of the earth and, in its density, is made altogether like the subtle earth, why should that solar body not be fashioned from the subtlest material of the earth's belly, since the earth is called the *parent of all things* which are created after it? Do not meteors, or "corruptible Suns," which are not much different from that figure of the incorruptible Sun, appear in the upper region of the air from the exhalations of the earth? Their bodies were merely unable to ascend higher into the ethereal region because of their spurious impurity. Is it not also true, by the authority of almost all the aforementioned Philosophers, that fire is converted into air, air into water, and water into earth by a certain **inspissation** <term>inspissation: a process of thickening or condensing a substance</term>? Through this, it is also very easily seen that earth, in turn, is transmuted into water, water into air, and air into fire through **subtiliation** <term>subtiliation: the alchemical process of refining, thinning, or purifying matter into a more spiritual or volatile state</term>; and that fire has something in common with the ether. Furthermore, since all things are from water, what is the difference between thick water and that which is subtle, except that the latter is on the side of the subtlest spirit, while the former is on the side of the earth? From this, it is certain that the thickness of the Sun is derived from the side of the earth. Indeed, this assertion of ours is supported and bolstered by the very words of certain Philosophers; as it is found in the writings of Hippocrates:

> *A part of that immortal thing, which he called heat <note>original: "calidum"</note>, when all things were disturbed, departed into the highest periphery, which the ancients called ether.*

That is to say: when the threefold portion of the darkness of the threefold heaven, enclosing the bright rays and constituting a rude and unordered chaos, had been gathered into the lowest heaven, the light—which they call the *Immortal Heat*—ascended, etc. This is certainly what *Hermes Trismegistus* <note>original: "Mercurius Trismegistus"</note> seems to confirm in his sacred discourse, where he speaks thus:

> *There was infinite shadow in the abyss, and water besides, and a thin intellectual spirit was within the chaos by divine power; and when all things were indistinct, the light things afterwards flew up into the high region, while the heavy things settled under the moist sand; and once things were distinguished and balanced, being carried by a fiery spirit, the heaven shone forth into seven circles.*

For what else would that Wise Man mean by these words, other than that the light and essential spirits were in the chaos of the lower heaven through the Word of God, and that afterwards the light things—that is, the spiritual things—enclosed in the chaos, were carried up into the celestial region by a "fiery spirit"—that is, by the bright rays enclosed in the chaos? Once this was done, the Sun and the other Planets shone forth. From those words of *Trismegistus*, it also evidently appears that the "chaos" is a heap of all the darkness of the heavens, packed together into the lowest part along with captive rays. For he says:

> *light things are carried by a fiery spirit into the high region, that is, into the heaven.*

This is also understood in almost the same

# TREATISE I. BOOK V.

<center>146</center>

*...he himself impresses this sense in his Pymander <note>The "Pymander" is the first book of the *Corpus Hermeticum*, a collection of texts attributed to the legendary sage Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, where he says that a certain terrifying shadow slipped down in an oblique revolution and migrated into a moist nature; and from the moist bowels of nature, a pure and light fire immediately flew out and sought the heights, etc.*

But putting these things aside, what should be thought of the constant communication of the Sun with the earth? Since the Sun daily unites with the earth as if with his own female, or at least with some moist spirit retained in the earth—acting as a receptive patient that plays the part of a female or her womb—through whose annual coupling or coitus infinite things differing in species are produced, and each species is multiplied within itself. This, surely, could not be accomplished unless there were a great affinity between the spirit of the terrestrial and solar bodies. This affinity is undoubtedly on the side of matter, of which the earth is the source, and not from form, since its spring-head is found in the opposite part: namely, in the Empyrean heaven <note>The Empyrean was considered the highest, most spiritual part of the universe, filled with pure light or fire.</note>. Finally, since the solar body is visible, and therefore material according to its half-part, it is necessary that its more corporeal part be derived from the source of matter, which we demonstrated above to be the earth. 

What else did the **Chemical Philosophers** <note>original: *Philosophi Chimici*—this refers to alchemists.</note> intend, who almost with one consensus said that most noble offspring of the Sun, which they called the **Son of the Sun** and the **Terrestrial Sun**, received its substance from the earth and the other elements, but its form from heaven? Did not **Mehunius** <note>Jean de Meun, a 13th-century author famous for the *Roman de la Rose*; here Fludd refers to the *Lamentation of Nature* (*Lamentatio Naturae*).</note> also call the earth, in the *Lament of Nature*, the mother and nurse of all things, who conceived in her womb from fire—that is, from light? 

Finally, to approach the reasons of Holy Scripture, if we consider more diligently the time of the creation of the earth and the solar body, we shall perceive a great mystery in the order of creation. For on the third day of creation the earth was made, and its merely vegetable creatures; and after the creation of the earth, on the fourth day, the spherical body of the Sun is produced, into whose dimension the rays dispersed everywhere in the heaven are gathered on that same day. Since, therefore, the Sun is a body to be placed many infinite degrees above the earth, why would nature not have made it an infinity of time before the fabrication of the earth? For creation began from the most perfect creature, namely light, and the ethereal heaven was perfected before the creation of the earth—of which heaven the Sun is a far more excellent creature. Why, unless nature preferred to choose the bulk of the Sun’s body from the earth to receive so noble a form, and to make it most fit for rendering the invisible and hidden rays of the middle heaven manifest and visible? 

Indeed, this creation of the Sun is highly mystical and is an evident sign of the future **uncreated good** <note>original: *boni increati*—referring to Christ/The Word.</note> that would come to men. For just as the super-celestial light, created from heaven, descended into the earth (that is, into the shadow) by means of the spirit, and there remained for three days from the creation of the Empyrean heaven, buried in the abyss of darkness and earth like a vegetable body; and then, by the assistance of the higher rays, casting off the corruptible uncleanness of the earth, it was glorified with its own body and, as if regenerated, ascended into the ethereal heaven on the third day after its inclusion in the shadow—from which it rains down life, form, and the power of growth upon all vegetable bodies, being gifted every year with a shining scepter and diadem: 

So also that **uncreated light** <note>original: *lux increata*</note>, the true and only offspring of the uncreated Father, by divine will descended into the earth, took on the nature of a human body, and died according to the corruptible flesh. But through his divine and uncreated essence, he rose again on the third day after death, with his body now purified from all mortal corruption and glorified beyond human intelligence. From there, he ascended into the Empyrean heaven with inexpressible splendor, where, exalted at the right hand of his Father and brighter than all clarity, he daily and liberally rains down spiritual gifts upon human souls, illuminating them with his divine light and defending them from the rage of **Evil Demons** <note>original: *Cacodæmonum*</note>. Indeed, he washes away perishing men with the water from the fountain of life, regenerating them by spiritual washing, and redeeming and saving them from the corruption of sin. Therefore, no one should consider it an impossible thing that this created solar essence descended to the earth, so that it might there lay aside the opacity and uncleanness of its vehicle or body, into which it descended...

We believe this by faith and see it with spiritual eyes—that this most divine and uncreated nature performed this very thing; just as the Holy Scriptures testify with these words: *Let the earth be opened, so that it may bring forth the Savior.* <note>original: "Aperiatur terra, ut illa Salvatorem producat." They reject and condemn everything that is not perceptible to the senses, and they consider the opinions of those who try to teach things beyond the reach of the common senses to be nothing but nonsense. TREATISE I. BOOK V.

### CHAPTER VIII.

->That the entire substance of the middle heaven was denser and darker before<-
->the production of the Sun than after its<-
->appearance.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'L' featuring floral and vine patterns within a square border.</image-desc>

That light, the first creature—although it performed the role of the Sun for three days before the production of the luminaries, as *Dionysius* <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian theologian and philosopher whose works were highly influential in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> testifies—was nonetheless more imperfect than the Sun, as is affirmed in the *Theological Resolutions, treatise 2, part 2, portion 2, question 1.* For it was dispersed and scattered within a greater quantity of matter; hence, on account of the vastness of that matter, it could not manifest its effects—namely light and heat—so powerfully to the world, since the disposition of matter always opposed the actions of form. <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "form" provides the active qualities of a thing, while "matter" is the passive potential that can resist or obscure those qualities.</note> Hence it was that, before the creation of the Sun, that light did not possess such an effective power (flowing from its heat) to thin out or refine matter and to dissipate condensed substance, since a united and gathered force is always stronger. For this reason, the matter of the heaven was denser before the fourth day than after it.

Wherefore it is manifest that the "day"—which the divine Philosophers <note>Theologians or mystical philosophers who study the nature of God's creation.</note> held to be characterized by quality alone (that is, by mere light) before the rising of the Sun, and not by quantity or time (inasmuch as time only took its origin from the fourth day, as the *Theological Resolutions* testifies in the place cited before)—was far darker and more obscure due to the thickness of the ether <term>ether: the celestial substance filling the space of the universe above the sphere of the moon</term> before the Sun's creation than after its production. For after the third day, that entire mass of secondary light, which had been diffused through the vast capacity of the middle heaven, contracted itself into the solar body. Indeed, according to *Dionysius* in his work *On the Divine Names, chapter 4*, it is asserted that this light was the very substance of the Sun, but not yet fully formed. However that may be, it is certainly probable that this light, once dispersed everywhere, was gathered into that purified and exalted solar mass (for the reasons declared in the penultimate preceding chapter) and thus produces more vehement effects in that part of the world through which it passes than when its light was scattered everywhere. Hence it heats, thins, scatters, and illuminates more effectively. And by actions of this kind, the thicker parts of this heaven, which were equally diffused before the Sun's rising, are now, after its manifestation, reverberated both downwards and upwards in the same region, to a greater or lesser degree. Through the resistance of these parts, and by the action of this solar body, the various spherical bodies of the Planets are produced, as we shall declare below.

<header>CONCERNING THE CREATURES OF THE ETHEREAL HEAVEN.</header>

# CHAPTER IX.

## *Concerning the production of the other Planets.*

<image-desc>A decorative square drop cap "I" featuring intricate floral and vine-like ornamentation within a double-lined border.</image-desc>

IN the chapter on the constitution of the Sun, we described how the Spirit of darkness—which occupied the highest region of the world while still retaining a portion of that same light—was reflected back toward the center of the world-machine along with the darkness of the lower heavens. There, it cast off its foul and black impurity and was exalted into the heart of the ether by the power of celestial light. We also noted that the rays dispersed on the second day throughout the vast capacity of the ethereal heaven are gathered and collected into its substance. This happens partly through the magnetic power of the solar matter (which seeks form) <note>In this philosophy, "form" is the active, life-giving principle that matter naturally desires to unite with.</note> and especially because of the luminous soul retained in that place, which summons its surrounding "brothers" to its aid. It acts no differently than a most cunning fowler who, in order to deceive passing birds, shuts a small bird in a cage, so that by its presence and songs others of that same species might be enticed into those same snares.

Therefore, once a multitude of luminous rays has been gathered and occupies the mass of that Spirit equally, the thicker parts of this heaven are dispersed by the power of those united rays (whose nature it is to refine and thin out matter). These thicker parts are thrust down gradually—that is, more or less significantly—toward the outer parts of this heaven. For this reason, the parts of this heaven further removed from its center (where the solar body is suspended) are colder and, consequently, coarser and denser—especially that extremity which tends more downward, toward the center. And this is why the Planets positioned in those extremities produce cold effects, owing to the dense disposition of their location.

Thus, in the lowest hollow of the middle heaven, the thicker part of the ether is reflected back by the power of the Sun's essential rays. Because of its proximity to the sphere of moisture, it is very humid; and because of its contact with the sphere of fire—beyond which it cannot descend due to the purity of its own substance—its coldness is strengthened by <term>antiperistasis</term> <note>original: "antiperistasin"; a technical term in medieval science for the intensification of a quality when it is surrounded by its opposite, such as cold becoming more intense when trapped by surrounding heat.</note>.

Therefore, this lower sphere of the middle heaven stands in relation to the middle part (where the solar body moves) just as the element of water in the lower heaven stands in relation to the element of fire. Consequently, the coldness of the thick ether in this sphere, by resisting the actions of the solar rays, stirs up an upward motion to the midpoint between this sphere and that of the Sun. At that point, another motion meets it: namely, the motion of the descending solar rays. Because these descending solar rays are equal to the rising vaporous matter, the battle between both natures is proportional. Therefore, the power of the light is not sufficient to prevent the rising of the vaporous Spirit to that midpoint, nor are the forces of that rising exhalation strong enough to repel the acts of the luminous rays (which descend to that middle point) any further upward. Thus, they fight at that same point with mutual and proportional forces, and by fighting, they are moved in a double circular motion: namely, from west to east according to the appetite of matter, and from east to west following the progress of the primary light.

And from this it happens that, by the swift motion of one nature against the slow (though highly resistant) motion of the other, the thicker substance of that sphere is gathered, added to, and assimilated into a single globe—not unlike how two opposing winds blowing together create a cloud by thickening the air. This same process occurs in each sphere of this heaven immediately before the birth of its Planet. Therefore, by the action of the descending rays and the resistance of the rising vaporous Spirit, a double circular motion is created at the midpoint between the lowest sphere of this heaven and that of the Sun; and from there is born the

It details a celestial mechanics where the struggle between rising vapors and descending light creates circular motions that condense matter into planetary globes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of planetary formation begun in Chapter IX, moving from the general principles of solar light to the specific creation of the inferior planets (Mercury, Venus, and the Moon) and introducing the mechanics of the superior planets (Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn) located above the Sun.</meta>

### TREATISE I. BOOK V.

That bright globe which we call the Planet Mercury is a body into which solar light is visibly gathered by a certain magnetic force; it is by the presence of this light that its body becomes apparent to our eyes. Similarly, through the constant reflection <note>Original: *resultatione*</note> of rays from this spherical Mercurial body, certain thinner vapors, stirred by its body, are driven upward to the midpoint between the solar body and the Mercurial one. At that exact point, their movement is met by another force that prevents the further ascent of this aforementioned vaporous Spirit—namely, the descending rays from the solar body. Therefore, the equality of the nature of the *Sun* with the equality of the nature of *Mercury* generates a middle orb between them. By its double circular motion, its thicker matter is gathered into a single spherical mass, which Astrologers have called **Venus**. This is the reason (specifically, the proximity of the nature of the matter of these two Planets to the essence of the Sun) why their natural motion differs less from the solar motion than those of the other Planets, as we shall declare more at length below.

Another motion also occurs from that lowest sphere upward toward the globe of *Mercury*, and another from the body of *Mercury* downward toward that sphere, such that in the middle of the path, the motion of one is opposed to the motion of the other. By the equal struggle of these forces, a double motion is excited in that middle orb; by its application, that spherical mass is clustered together from the equal nature of the Mercurial body and the moist disposition of the lower sphere. This is called the lunar body: *the Moon, Phoebe, Diana, Cynthia, Lucina, etc.* <note>These are various classical names for the Moon-goddess used by Renaissance scholars to add poetic weight to their scientific descriptions.</note> Because of its proximity to that region of corruption <note>The "region of corruption" refers to the Earth and the sublunary world, where things are subject to decay, unlike the eternal heavens.</note>, and also because it receives only a tiny amount of lucid form from Mercury, its nearest neighbor, its body hungers more and more for the solar form. Although it is adorned by this form equally—that is, in the same proportion—nonetheless, according to its distance or proximity to the solar body, the illumination of its body appears more or less visible to us. From this, it is manifest that the greater part of it is in some way opaque, and consequently, a certain opacity is required for the visibility of the bodies of the stars. Likewise, it is certain that this body is forged from the denser substance of this heaven, since it cannot be totally penetrated by the solar rays. And in this way, we have three Planets moving in their own proper orbs, arranged at a proportional distance and order from each other between the convex surface of the lowest heaven, whose bodies must be considered thicker and denser the further they exist from the solar body.

Since just as many Planets are found above the solar body—that is, between the place of the fixed stars and the orb of the Sun—it must be diligently investigated how and by what reasoning they could be produced in their distinct orbs in that place. We have said, therefore, that one part of this heaven, thicker and moister, is driven downward from its heart by the presence of the *Sun*, and the other part, namely the drier part, is driven upward toward the concave surface of the aqueous or crystalline sphere, in which we declared above that the fixed stars are congealed. In those boundaries, cold also abounds (as it does in the opposite place) through <term>antiperistasis</term> <note>Antiperistasis is an ancient philosophical concept where a quality is intensified when surrounded by its opposite, such as cold becoming more concentrated when surrounded by heat.</note>; that is, because of the gentle warmth of the most spiritual heaven hanging above and touching the crystalline sphere, and the vigorous heating of the solar body. By the operation of this heat, dryness is shaken out from the solar orb toward those margins, and its dryness comes from the congealing disposition of the crystalline sphere—which all have called the <term>glacial heaven</term> <note>Original: *coelum glaciale*</note> for this reason—and the "crystalline" quality arises from a multitude of "dry waters." The action of the solar rays upon this overarching subject is, therefore, the sole reason and origin for the Planets suspended step-by-step above the Sun. For just as we demonstrated in the birth of the elements regarding the production of the sphere of moisture in the lowest heaven, the constrictive nature of the earth meets and concurs with an equal proportion of fire in the middle path; so also, by the upward motion of the solar rays and the downward agitation of dry cold (intended to repel the assaults of said rays), in the middle path between the solar body and the convex surface of this heaven...

This page shifts the focus upward to the superior planets—Jupiter, Mars, and Saturn—and the mechanical forces that gathered their mass in the higher ethereal regions.</meta>

### ON THE CREATURES OF THE ETHEREAL HEAVEN.

In this part of heaven, a conflict arises between equal parts of different natures. By their varied motion—namely, from the East to the West and from the West to the East—a spherical mass is gathered together, which they call the <term>Jovial body</term> <note>Original: *corpus Joviale*. The body of the planet Jupiter.</note> or **Jupiter**. It is forged from two portions of solar nature and just as many parts of this colder and drier heaven. From the reverberation or reflection of the Sun's rays bouncing downward from the Jovial body, a more subtle vapor is stirred up, which accompanies those reflecting rays; and these, halfway between the Jovial and solar bodies, are met by new rays of the Sun seeking to rise. By the double and opposite motion of these rays, a new double circular motion arises, by which the density of that entire region is whirled around and reduced into one globe, which they name the <term>Martial body</term> <note>Original: *corpus Martiale*. The body of the planet Mars.</note> or **Mars**, forged from an equal disposition of **Jupiter** and the **Sun**. 

Similarly, towards the upper motion, a movement occurs from the lucid nature of **Jupiter**, which is set against a motion contrary to it in the middle of the path between **Jupiter** itself and the <term>convex surface</term> <note>The outer "shell" or boundary of this specific celestial sphere.</note> of this heaven. From their conflict, a double circular motion also arises, whereby the thicker portion of that interval is gathered into one round body, which we call **Saturn**. And in this manner we have the bodies of the seven Planets <note>At this time, "seven planets" included the Sun and Moon, along with Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> exalted to their own proper seats, having various qualities and dispositions according to their position and composition, as we shall clearly explain in the following chapter. The production of the Planets is thus described:

TREATISE I. BOOK V.

## ->C H A P T E R  X.<-

### ->On the natures of the Planets, and their dispositions, by which they act upon these lower regions.<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'Q' featuring a central flower or foliate motif within a square border.</image-desc>

ALTHOUGH it is clear enough from what has come before that the bodies of the three inferior planets and the three superior ones <note>The "inferior" planets are those between the Earth and the Sun (Moon, Mercury, Venus), while "superior" planets are those beyond the Sun (Mars, Jupiter, Saturn).</note> proceeded from the **Sun's power**; nevertheless, we shall here produce another reason. By this, we shall declare both that the creation of the solar body was prior in order to the bodies of the other wandering stars, and that it also received the formal arrangement of light earlier. For almost all **Philosophers** have agreed on this: that light is in the *Sun* as if in a thing luminous in itself, but in the other planets it exists by participation <note>original: "participativè". This means the other planets do not create their own light but "participate" in or reflect the Sun's light.</note>. Furthermore, since the light of a star is its essential form—without which the stars can neither exist nor be named—and since the remaining planets received it from the Sun itself as if from their own giver of form, it is necessary that the Sun precede the other planets in the order of creation, just as a beginning precedes that which begins from it. Therefore, the light sent down from the highest heaven through its common <term>diaphragm</term> <note>A dividing membrane or boundary layer in the celestial spheres.</note> was received into the Solar body on the fourth day, and there it is guarded, as **Basil** <note>St. Basil the Great (c. 330–379), a Church Father who wrote on the six days of creation.</note> testifies. The **Sun** imparted a certain portion of this light to every planet, both above and below; he gave more or less partly according to the closeness or distance of that planet's body from his seat, and partly according to the disposition of the planetary body to which he gives. And this is why, according to **Iamblichus**, all heavenly powers reside in the Sun, and, as **Proclus** <note>Iamblichus and Proclus were influential Neoplatonist philosophers who saw the Sun as a divine central power.</note> testifies, all the forces of the heavenly beings are gathered into one at the sight of the Sun; we believe these are finally scattered into this lower world through his fiery vents. Hence the **Platonists** believed that the soul of the world was enclosed within the Sun. For this reason, **Phoebus** <note>A poetic name for the Sun or Apollo.</note> sits in the middle, shining with golden hair, holding a scepter and a helm like a King and Emperor, most full of the light of all the stars. He received this light from the <term>intelligible world</term> <note>The realm of pure thought or divine ideas, above the physical world.</note> above all other stars because his soul is more capable of receiving the intelligible splendor, as some of the **Platonists** maintain. This primary royal disposition reveals itself clearly both by reason of its composition and its effect. For it is composed of an equal part of spiritual matter ascending from the earth, and of an essential luminous form descending from the <term>Empyrean heaven</term> <note>The highest, fiery heaven, believed to be the dwelling place of God and pure light.</note>. Hence, by **Artephius** <note>A medieval author to whom various alchemical and hermetic texts were attributed.</note>, his orb is called the <term>sphere of the soul and of equality</term> <note>original: "sphæra animæ & æqualitatis".</note>. From these things it is clear that his temperament is warm in the most pleasant degree (which he received from the arrangement of secondary light) and imperceptibly moist (this proceeding from the nature of his spiritual body). But because the abundance of his form has in every way satisfied the appetite of his matter, warmth certainly predominates in his body; by the benefit of this warmth, not only did the planets receive their natural dispositions, but so too did every lower thing. It is from here, therefore, that **Mercury**, receiving one part of his nature from the Sun and another from the moist and cold mass scattered spherically below, has three parts of coldness and moisture, and just as many from the temperament of the Sun. For this reason, **Mercury** is much more moist and cold than he is warm, because in the arrangement of the Solar body, we said that one part of the body concurs with one part of the luminous soul. And this is why **Mercury** is so variable in his nature, and so inconstant, that he joins himself sometimes to good planets, and sometimes to evil ones, [and assumes] their natures...

It specifically details how Mercury’s fickle nature arises from its balanced but unstable composition of solar warmth and elemental cold/moisture.</summary>

# ON THE CREATURES OF THE ETHEREAL HEAVEN.

<note>original: "DE CREATURIS COELI ÆTHEREI"</note>

is said to take on both good and bad natures. For since it has a great deal of cold matter in its composition, and only a tiny bit of "form" <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, "form" refers to the organizing principle or essence—here, the solar light—while "matter" is the physical substance.</note> in respect to its substance, it desires to take on the natures of the other Planets; it freely receives the impressions of those it encounters or those with whom it shares an aspect. For this reason, it is very fluid in its nature, and more moist than hot. 

*The Moon*, however, since she is composed on one side from the nature of *Mercury* and on the other from the cold and moist mass of that same heaven, is extremely cold and moist compared to other celestial bodies. She possesses only one part of heat from *Mercury*, along with one part of coldness and moisture, and two parts from the cold and moist sphere itself. Therefore, she continually seeks and receives help and light from the solar body through participation. 

The nature of *Venus*, because it consists of an equal part of the Solar nature and the nature of *Mercury*, has three parts of the Solar disposition, but only a single part of the cold and moist inferior sphere which she received from *Mercury*. Thus, she is beneficial and life-giving in her nature, and fortunate for living things; for she provides more moisture than the *Sun* and more heat than *Mercury*. Consequently, she is hot in the second degree of sweetness and moist in the first. 

*Jupiter* differs from *Venus* in this: in his nature he is hotter and more masculine because of his proximity to *Mars*, to which he is adjacent. He participates in that nature in a certain way, since he was joined with the *Sun* in his composition. Similarly, he is drier than *Venus* due to the proximity of the sphere of *Saturn* in the upper part and *Mars* in the lower. Thus, his nature is clearly hot and moist, fashioned from an equal disposition of the *Sun* and that part of the thicker ether pushed toward the concave surface of the highest heaven or "crystalline sphere" <note>The crystalline sphere was traditionally viewed as a reservoir of celestial water or ice, accounting for the "moisture" attributed to certain planets.</note>. This is because the substance of that sphere—congealed by the power of the icy heaven and agitated by the power of the solar rays—meets those descending rays halfway in order to resist them. This produces the same effect in this heaven that the nature of the earth, combined with the heat of the element of fire descending halfway, produces in the lower heaven: namely, moisture. But because heat exerts greater power while ascending (that is, it heats more strongly), *Jupiter* heats slightly more than he moistens. 

*Mars*, however, is produced from the simple action and passion of *Jupiter* and the *Sun*. Hence, his nature consists of three parts of the *Sun's* nature—namely, one from *Jupiter* and two from the *Sun*. He also has a portion of the cold and dry sphere which he received immediately from the disposition of *Jupiter*. Therefore, because of the heat of *Jupiter* and the *Sun*, he has three "witnesses" of heat. Indeed, he is exceedingly hot and dry—that is, he has an entirely fiery nature—because the nature of the *Sun* heats more by ascending than by descending. This is proven by the nature of artificial fire placed on the grate of a wind-furnace; for downward (that is, below the grate) it heats but little, while above it heats vehemently. For a fiery nature is always carried upward with great violence, imitating the disposition of its origin from which it was first derived. 

But because the malignancy of the sphere of *Saturn* reflects the nature of *Mars* from the other side, it both renders him drier through its own dryness and inflames the nature of *Mars* from that side through *antiperistasis* <note>term: antiperistasis. An old philosophical concept where a quality is intensified by being surrounded by its opposite, like a cold winter day making the heat of a fire feel more intense.</note>. It drives and reflects his powers downward, granting him a malignancy fatal to human nature—not unlike a cloud enclosing lightning, which multiplies the rage of that lightning by its contrary disposition, renewing the exhalation trapped in its belly by the clash of natures, and bringing its destructive qualities into action. 

*Saturn* has a nature participating in that cold and dry ethereal mass pushed to those margins by the force of the *Sun's* rays, and in the constitution of *Jupiter*. Thus, he has three-quarters of contracting <note>original: "stipticæ," meaning styptic or astringent.</note> coldness and only one part of solar heat, which he received from the nature of *Jupiter*. From this, his constitution is very fixed, cold, and dry, closely resembling an earthy disposition and drawing his congealing coldness from the crystalline heaven. For this reason, this Planet is strongly opposed to *Jupiter*, *Mars*, the *Sun*, and *Venus*, and is a vehement adversary to the generation of lower things, [consuming] the radical moisture <note>term: radical moisture. The vital fluid or "oil" thought to be stored in living beings that sustains life; its exhaustion led to old age and death.</note>...

<summary>This page describes the elemental and astrological temperaments of the planets, detailing how their proximity to the Sun and the "crystalline sphere" determines their degrees of heat, moisture, and influence on life.</summary>

<page-num>148</page-num>
<header>TREATISE I. BOOK V.</header>

<note>original: "radicalis"</note> absorbing the radical moisture <term>radical moisture: the essential fluid believed to sustain life and internal heat in all bodies</term> with its dryness, and blunting the innate heat <term>innate heat: the vital, natural warmth that powers a living being's soul and functions</term> with its immense coldness. Here, however, it should be noted in passing that the fixed stars <note>original: "stella fixæ"</note> themselves also take on the natures of these Planets, or rather have possessed similar dispositions since their creation. These stars differ from one another according to the mass of their vaporous matter and the proportion of the light-form <note>In Aristotelian terms, "form" is the active, organizing principle—here, the light—while "matter" is the physical substance.</note> from which they are composed. Similarly, this occurs because of the proximity of one star to another of the same or opposite condition. Thus, it is found through experience that when a certain Planet passes under a fixed star or stars—which they call "signs"—that share its condition, it has a stronger effect on these lower things <note>A common term for the Earth and the sublunary world.</note>. In such a case, the Planet is said to be in its "house of exaltation" <term>exaltation: a specific sign in the zodiac where a planet’s influence is thought to be most powerful</term> or in its "joy." Conversely, if it moves under signs of an opposite condition, its strength and power over lower things are weakened; then the Astrologers say that the planet is in its "fall" or "detriment" <term>detriment: a sign where a planet’s nature is hindered or weakened</term>. Therefore, the fixed stars act upon the bodies of the Planets, either to hinder their virtues and natural influences, or to strengthen them and make them more effective.

---

## C H A P T E R XI.

### That it is necessary for two extremes to be produced before any middle can be assigned.

SINCE in the preceding chapters we have dealt with many "middles" <note>or intermediate things</note> which cannot exist without "extremes," it is necessary that the extremes in the order of creation be produced first, before a middle can be reached. For nothing can be called a "middle" unless it is placed between two extremes. For example, the "middle heaven" was not so called until the lowest heaven was created; just as **GOD** created the Empyrean heaven <term>Empyrean: the highest, most spiritual part of heaven, composed of pure light and fire</term> and the earth before He suspended His glowing and glorious fire in the middle space between both extremities. Nor did He produce the sphere of moisture in the middle of the lowest heaven until the fire and the earth had first been revealed, between which that sphere might move. So also Mercury hangs in the middle of the lowest part by the virtue of two extremes—namely, the sphere of ethereal cold and moisture, and the solar body. Similarly, it was necessary for the Sun and the sphere of cold and dryness to precede the birth of Jupiter, to whom a middle place between them is assigned. Finally, since the origin of the aforementioned middles arises from the mutual action and passion <note>In natural philosophy, "passion" refers to the capacity to be acted upon or affected by another force.</note> of the extremes, it is necessary by the order of nature that the extremes be produced before the middles which are composed from them.

## C H A P T E R XII.

### *On the influences of the Planets, and how they act upon these lower things through them?*

IT IS the opinion of the PHILOSOPHERS that stars act upon these lower things through motion, light, and influence. Among these operations, they consider "influence" <note>original: "influentia"</note> to be the most subtle and effective, which they believe penetrates without any hindrance even to the very bowels of the earth. Certainly, they do not wander far from the truth, since we are compelled by constant experience to recognize the wonderful operations of these influences. Therefore, we shall briefly discuss in this place what kind of influx <note>original: "influxus"</note> this is from the stars, and how many types there are. We call it the "influence" of some Planet when a spark of its own soul...

<meta>catchword: ...of light [lucidæ]</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how celestial light and planetary influences interact with earthly matter, specifically focusing on how the "subtle" nature of these influences allows them to penetrate physical bodies.</meta>

<page-num>149</page-num>

# ON THE CREATURES OF THE ETHEREAL HEAVEN.

...led in its most subtle and imperceptible spiritual vehicle <term>spiritual vehicle: a refined, non-material carrier that allows celestial forces to move through physical space</term>, a penetration by no means to be held back, it is accustomed to enter any thing whatever, and to produce new effects—whether manifest or hidden—within it, either for generation and preservation, or for corruption and ruin. From this, therefore, arises the difference of influxes <term>influx: the flowing of celestial power or "virtue" into earthly bodies</term>, which nonetheless do not differ in themselves, but in respect to the subject into which they descend. For if a Planet has an influx into matter naturally disposed toward it—specifically where the matter in the lower world is of the same composition as the planet in the higher world—then certainly this kind of influx brings fuel and nourishment to those bodies. But if it finds matter of a contrary condition, it undoubtedly acts upon it destructively. For either the stubbornness of the recipient subject receives it perversely, or its weakness cannot easily tolerate the influx. 

For example: the power of **Saturn** is indeed containing <note>original: "contentiva," referring to its cold, contracting nature</note>; while that of **Mars** is moving. The former often hinders by accident when it is received by colder matter, while the latter harms when received by hotter matter. Likewise, the former harms when it is received because it is taken by way of freezing; the latter harms when it acts through heat. This is also the cause of many fevers, namely when a great mass of raw humors <term>humors: the bodily fluids thought to govern health and personality</term> is gathered around the heart <note>original: "præcordia"</note>. The influence of the Martial planet entering such a body stirs up a battle of contraries, from which rot arises and the cause of fever is excited. 

Similarly, the influx of the **Sun**, and its light and heat, even if they seem to offend the weak, are nevertheless primarily necessary for life and generation. But to discuss the arrangement of congruent influxes that agree with the nature of the subject, we shall first approach the arrangements of the influences of the two luminaries <note>The Sun and the Moon.</note>. For it is certain that the **Sun** diffuses and sows its influences into well-disposed bodies, such as vegetables of every species, as if through fiery breathing-holes <note>original: "ignea spiracula"</note>; from this, they sprout, mature, thrive, and bloom. In this way, the Sun breathes life and heat, as it were, into such lower things by its light. 

Similarly, at the full moon <note>original: "plenilunio"</note>, because of the multitude of solar rays occupying the lower part of the **Moon's** body, a thicker portion of its sphere flows into lower things every month by the power of the light acting upon it (which is demonstrated by the *experiment of the altar* <note>This likely refers to a specific optical or physical demonstration involving reflected light, common in 17th-century natural philosophy.</note>). This influence is accustomed not only to penetrate the vast substance of the sea and make it swell, but also to enter secretly into plants themselves, increasing their sap; it is also accustomed to invisibly search the interiors of animals, multiplying their phlegmatic humor. This increase of the said humors could by no means come from the air, since it is established that the air is not subtle enough to perform such a task. For experience itself has taught us that air or wind enclosed in a bladder, a box, or some un-pierced vessel—or even in the stomach or head of an animal—is not accustomed to exit in any way unless the vessels or places mentioned are opened. 

From these things it is manifest that this augmentation of moisture flows from the sphere of the **Moon**, whose substance is much more subtle than that of the air. Thus, at the time of the full moon, it is accustomed to flow imperceptibly and without resistance into the aforementioned bodies, which are of a similar condition to its own sphere. However, when the splendor of the Moon fails, that infused moisture always decreases little by little, exhales imperceptibly from the bodies, and gradually returns into the concavity of its own sphere as the brightness recedes from the lower parts of the Moon. This is more clearly demonstrated by the effect of the *experiment of the lead globe placed against the Sun*: while the Sun's rays touch it, the thicker vapors are pushed downward around its circumference; but when the rays are removed, that thick air to its original place 

->returns.<-

<header>TREATISE I. BOOK V.</header>

# CHAPTER XIII.

## *That although the heavens and the stars may be governed by intelligences, as is the opinion of the ancients, it is nevertheless not likely that they are moved by them, but rather that a capacity was naturally infused in them from the beginning by the divine will.*

It is the opinion of all the Peripatetics <term>Peripatetics: followers of Aristotle, who believed the universe was moved by a chain of causes</term> that the stars do not move by their own power, but by the circuit of their sphere; and they teach that the sphere is moved by an intelligence, or its own daemon <term>daemon: in this context, a guiding celestial spirit or "angelic" administrator</term>, and that these daemons received their motion from GOD, the first mover. But, as it seems to me, in things that move naturally, the cause of their motion must always be called natural, and not supernatural. For GOD, from the beginning of heaven and earth, bestowed upon all creatures "first act" <term>first act: the initial realization of a thing’s potential or its "spark" of existence</term> as a most beautiful gift, so that they might be brought into their own being, and be led by themselves—either from one state to another, or from one place to another—according to the nature of the creature. 

Since light—which we called above the first and principal creature of GOD—is the swiftest "first act" in its motion, granting form, life, and greater or lesser motion to any creature by its presence, who will doubt that an airy and ethereal body, whose desire for formal light has been satisfied, has the power of moving by itself and from its own form? For we have experienced our own eyes seeing this same thing in lower natural bodies, which contain a smaller portion of that luminous form. We see animals move here and there by the action and agitation of their internal light. Indeed, since a double motion is perceived in animals (for sometimes they are led by the lust and appetite of their body, and sometimes by the will of the soul that moves them), why should it be impossible that the stars—in which the quantity of light is a thousand times greater than in animals, and from which that light of animals descends—should be led from west to east according to the appetite of their matter, and also be carried in the opposite way from east to west according to the disposition of their luminous form? 

Far be it from us, however, to attempt to prove—along with the Platonists and the Astrologers of the Arabs, Egyptians, and Chaldeans—that the heaven and stars are "animals" <note>original: "animalia"; here meaning living, breathing beings with souls</note>, since they are to be preferred over animals by infinite degrees of perfection. What should we think of even lowlier things, such as a plant or a tree, in whose growth motion is found from one point to another throughout the three dimensions of its body without any help from "intelligences"? 

But, to descend to even humbler things, what shall be said of artificial objects? For by the action of a certain light or a lit candle, images that are otherwise immobile are immediately moved and turned around by the effect of that light, as is demonstrated in the experiment below <note>The author is likely referring to a "magic lantern" or a primitive shadow-clock device</note>. Since, therefore, our artificial and elemental fire, which is derived from the effect of light, can perform such things, who would be so dull-witted as to deny that a stronger power lies hidden in the efficient cause of fire? For according to that old axiom of the philosophers: *That which makes a thing such, is itself even more so.* <note>original: "Quod facit tale, est magis tale"; a logical principle stating that if A gives a quality to B, A must possess that quality in a higher degree</note>. Since all these things can be performed in these lower regions—both by nature and by art—without the prompting of any "intelligence," then without a doubt they can be perfected and completed better and more exactly in the ethereal heaven. This is due to the multitude of never-resting light which predominates there; that is, it is possible for a long-lasting, untiring, and (according to natural probability) perpetual motion to be produced without the assistance of intelligences. And yet, I do not so much [dismiss] the intelligences or Daemons of the second order, whom, as in book 2...

<meta>catchword: chapter 4.</meta>

<summary>The author argues that the stars move through the heavens by an inherent, natural power given to them by God through the medium of light, rather than being pushed by external spiritual "intelligences" as the Aristotelians claimed.</summary>

<page-num>151</page-num>
<header>ON THE CREATURES OF THE EMPYREAN HEAVEN.</header>

<margin>Chapter 4.</margin>
The tradition of the *Magi* <note>The "Magi" here refers to the wise men and philosophers of ancient Persia and the Hermetic tradition, often cited by Renaissance scholars.</note> called these "celestial and mundane" spirits; yet we do not exclude them from the bodies of the stars as if they possessed no duty or lordship over them. We admit, following the opinion of the ancients, that beyond their role in divine worship, they are assigned to the spheres of the world and preside over each of the heavens and stars. They are distributed into as many orders as there are heavens in the world and stars in the heavens. For this reason, the wiser Magi made some spirits "Saturnine," others "Jovial" <note>Associated with Jupiter</note>, and so on. Indeed, the opinion of the Platonists <term>Platonists: followers of Plato who believed in a world of ideal forms and intermediary spirits</term> was not far from reason when it affirmed that infinite *daemons* <term>daemons: intermediary spirits or "guides," not to be confused with modern "demons" as evil entities</term> inhabit the spherical substances of the stars, just as animals inhabit the Earth. They divide these spirits into legions, over which they say kings, princes, or governors are placed. And this is the very thing that *Abbot Trithemius* <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a famous German abbot and occultist known for his work on cryptography and angelology.</note> seems to confirm, saying: *seven spirits have been set over the seven Planets from the beginning of heaven and earth*. Of these, the spirit-governor of the kingdom of *Saturn* is called *Orifiel*; of *Jupiter*, *Zachariel*; of *Mars*, *Samaël*; of the *Sun*, *Michaël*; of *Venus*, *Anaël*; of *Mercury*, *Raphaël*; and of the *Moon*, *Gabriel*. We conclude, therefore, that the motion of the planetary bodies no more consists in the power of the daemons inhabiting and occupying them than the spherical motion of the terrestrial body consists in a man—who exists as the noblest among all the Earth’s inhabitants and lord over all earthly things. Rather, we affirm with more confidence that the body derives all the power of its motion from its own soul and essential form. This is because we find in *Augustine* and in the *Resolution of Theology, treatise 2, part 3, portion 3, question 1*, that GOD administered the things He created in such a way that He allows them to be driven by their own motion. For GOD gave the stars and His other creatures those things which suffice for their own operation. Therefore, they are able to have their own operations without the special illumination or assistance of the "intelligences" <term>intelligences: pure spiritual beings (angels) thought by medieval philosophers to move the celestial spheres</term>. For the soul of any mobile body is the "first mover"—that is, it is mobile spontaneously and by itself—whereas its matter is by itself ineffective for motion and falls far short of the nature of the soul itself.

# CHAPTER XIV.

->*Why the heavens are moved circularly, and concerning the reason for the daily motion—that is, the revolution of the heaven in the space of twenty-four hours.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'I' featuring floral and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>
I believe those fall into a very great error who say that the substance of the Empyrean Heaven <note>The highest, most spiritual part of the universe, often envisioned as a realm of pure light or fire.</note> is immobile. Indeed, we said in *Chapter 2, Book 2* that the Spirit of the Lord moved in that place first of all; by whose motion, furthermore, it is certain that the most simple Spirit of that place is moved, just as we perceive the air to move at the motion of a fire below it. Sacred Scripture also reports that by the present and ineffable power of that uncreated moving Spirit, that first creature—the swiftest, most vital of all, and the opposite of rest—was produced as if by the radiance of that same Spirit. For, as that Royal *Psalmist* says:

> *By the word of the LORD the heavens were made firm, and all their power by the Spirit of his mouth.* <note>original: "Verbo Domini cœli firmati sunt, & Spiritu oris omnis virtus eorum." A reference to Psalm 33:6.</note>

Since, therefore, light is the first and principal power of the heavens and the universal world, it is necessary that it proceeds immediately from that divine and inexplicable Spirit. The Spirit of the Lord was carried in the spiritual heaven above the waters (or the corporal heaven), bounding them and attributing a spherical shape to them by its motion, as is evidently demonstrated by the effect to our rational eyes. From these things it is manifest that the motion of that same divine Spirit was not straight, but circular, so that by its motion it might make that mass of matter <note>original: "hyles massam"; "hyle" is the Greek term for primordial matter</note>—which it surrounded and separated from the other parts of matter excluded from the world—round and spherical. This was done also because a spherical figure of this kind, insofar as it has neither beginning nor end, is in some way likened to the Creator, since every effect necessarily possesses a certain...

...is accustomed to hold a likeness to its cause; and also because such a figure <note>the circle or sphere</note>, being the most noble, useful, perfect, and convenient of all others, is far more capacious than the rest. Immediately after the action of this Spirit, that first creature <note>Light</note> shone forth, proceeding as if from the motion and brightness of the **Uncreated Light** <note>original: "lucis increatæ"; referring to the divine light of God before creation</note>. It is probable that this light moved in the same way and by the same logic of motion, imitating the acts of its First Cause. Furthermore, the effect seems to reveal to us the path of this circular motion—that is, its starting point and its destination—namely, that it moved from the east toward the west, or rather from the right hand toward the left while facing the North Pole; (for the names *East and West* <note>original: "Oriens & Occidens"</note> took their designations from the creation of the Sun). This occurred for two principal reasons:

The *first reason* is that human nature, in which that divine light predominates above all other animals, usually moves the right hand by a divine appetite for its own light, exercising it in every manual operation as the more excellent and noble side; from this, it is manifest that the natural appetites of the primary light are moved from the right side of the heavens. 

The second reason, which indeed seems infallible, is because the rapid and swiftest motion of the heavens is perceived to tend from that side toward the west. But since the essential **created light** proceeding from the **uncreated light** is called the *most noble* by most philosophers—and even by those most skilled in Theology, among whom I name *Augustine in his Literal Commentary on Genesis*—because nothing is more mobile or subtle; and since it is also called by him in that same place the *foundation and principle of the highest natural motion*, it is necessary that the more grasping and swifter motion of the whole heaven be derived from its source. Consequently, the **diurnal motion** <term>diurnal motion: the apparent daily rotation of the heavens from East to West</term>, by which the entire material world is revolved in the space of twenty-four hours from the *East* toward the *West* (contrary to the opinion of *Copernicus*) by the power of that lucid spiritual fountain, flows from that light itself. 

This seems to be confirmed by *Trismegistus in his first sermon* <note>Referring to the "Poimandres," the first book of the Corpus Hermeticum attributed to Hermes Trismegistus</note> with these words: "The Artisan Mind, together with the Word, containing the circles and turning them with swift rapacity, turned its machine back upon itself, and commanded it to roll from the beginning to the end without end." By the "beginning," he seems to understand the rising (East), and by the "end," the setting (West). Do we not also see that Sacred Scripture, in almost every place, always places the name of the *East* before the *West*, as in *Genesis 13:14, Psalm 50*, and infinite other places? Furthermore, in every act of worship, the worshipers turn themselves toward the *East*, as in *Ezekiel 8:16, Matthew 11, Luke 13:29*, and many other places. Similarly, that *star* declaring the arrival of Christ appeared in the *East*. And so also the coming of the Lord will be from the east toward the west, according to *Matthew 24:27*. By these things, it is sufficiently demonstrated that the **Uncreated Light** moved from the East toward the West. For what could be clearer than this? Especially since the effect of that thing falls under our very senses. 

Are we not certain that a soul exists in a human or any other animal, although it is invisible, as long as we perceive its body moving here and there by itself? Do we not recognize that an invisible act is included in a plant when we see it flourish and sprout? Are we not also certain that an **intellectual soul** exists above the surface of the corporal world, while experience testifies that the world rounds itself together and revolves in a natural day? The matter of this world, since it is ineffective for motion by itself, could in no way accomplish this movement except through the motion of the fountain of light, which is the foundation, fountain, and origin of every natural motion. Consequently, it is certain that the motion of that first bright creature was circular, and from east to west; this motion was the primary one of all, and swifter, nobler, and more powerful than the others, since it arose from the fountain of universal action.

How that spherical matter, which is unfit for motion by itself, is moved by that fiery soul placed above the surface of its periphery, is declared by the following experiment; where **B. C. A.** is a wheel, on whose circumference that artificial fire, **A**, called a *Fuse* <note>original: "Fusum"; here likely referring to a rocket or pyrotechnic device used to create thrust</note>, is tied. By the power of this ignited fuse, the whole wheel is violently snatched away, performing its circular motion as quickly as possible.

We conclude, therefore, with the opinion of *Trismegistus* <note>Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary figure to whom many ancient mystical and scientific texts were attributed</note> put forward in his *third discourse*, that the wider circuit in the airy circle was carried along by the breathing together of the divine Spirit, and indeed from the beginning toward the end; and that by the action of its splendor and the resistance of the moist matter, a triple circular motion is produced in the heavens. The *first* of these is this motion from East to West, or from right to left, which we have already said is driven according to the disposition of the luminous source of the first heaven. The *second* has its cause in the matter of the stellar body and the heavens, as we shall explain below; that is, the motion which occurs from West to East, or from the left part of the heaven to the right. The *third* takes its origin from the mutual action and passion <note>the philosophical term for "being acted upon" or receiving an influence</note> of form and matter, which we call the motion of trepidation <note>an obsolete astronomical concept used to explain periodic shifts in the positions of stars and the precession of the equinoxes</note>. There are also circular motions proper to the creatures of heaven, which are stirred up by the action and passion between the matter of the star's substance (tending upward) and its form (seeking downward); such are their motions in their own <term>Epicycles</term> <note>small circular orbits used in ancient astronomy to explain the complex, sometimes backward-seeming movements of planets</note>, about which we shall also treat more extensively below.

# CHAPTER XV.

## Concerning the error of *Copernicus* and *Gilbert* who assert the daily revolution of the earth.

Concerning the daily revolution, various disputes arise among Philosophers, and a controversy not quickly decided; while some wish the entire machine to be revolved by the snatched motion of the <term>First Mover</term> <note>original: "primo mobili"; in traditional cosmology, the outermost sphere that moves all the inner spheres</note> in the space of twenty-four hours, and maintain that this motion occurs from East to West. Of this opinion are the *Peripatetics* <note>the followers of Aristotle</note>, and nearly all those of our age, with only a few of the contrary opinion excepted. Among these we find numbered and named from the ancients *Heraclides Ponticus* and *Ecphantus*, both *Pythagoreans*, *Nicetas of Syracuse*, and *Aristarchus of Samos*, and several others of the same sort; but among the more recent, *Copernicus*, that most skilled Astronomer, and *William Gilbert*, our colleague <note>William Gilbert (1544–1603) was a famous physician and physicist who studied magnetism; the author, likely Robert Fludd, considers him a contemporary and peer</note>, who for many years has been a studious researcher into magnetic virtue <note>original: "magneticæ virtutis"; referring to the invisible force of magnetism which Gilbert believed helped cause the Earth's rotation</note>. It is their opinion that the daily revolution is in no way produced by the First Mover or from East to West, but that the globe of the earth itself [rotates] from West to East follow-

...while they constantly affirm that the natural progression of the Planets naturally revolves in a space of 24 hours. And certainly their arguments, although they may seem probable and plausible at first glance, nevertheless, if they are inspected more exactly and considered diligently, we shall very easily discover them to be plainly trifling and frivolous. This we shall attempt to prove by reasons resting both upon philosophical speculation and common practice, as well as those taken from Holy Scripture. *First*, therefore, it would be miraculous if all the Planets of the heaven were moved according to the latitude of the world, while the terrestrial sphere—far viler and more ignoble than they—should constantly complete its natural course every natural day under the same elevations of the pole, and consequently be irregular in its motion compared to all other globes; for it is certain that any given place on earth is neither raised higher nor depressed lower, but always occupies the same seat of latitude. *Second*, if the circular motion of the earth were finished in the space of 24 hours, the earth would be moved many thousands of times faster than a horse hurrying at its most vehement gallop, or a staff violently struck, or a <term>cannonball</term><note>original: "globo tormentario"</note> emerging from the fiery mouth of a <term>bombard</term><note>An early type of large-caliber cannon used in the 14th to 17th centuries.</note>. Now, the violent course of a horse is seen to somewhat hinder the rider's breathing through the compression of the air and its resistance; a staff swung through the air by a strong arm is perceived to give off a sound and stir up no small noise; and a ball ejected from a bombard by the power of fire is proved, by the testimony of experience, to induce a still greater noise while flying through the medium of the air. Yet the motion of all these things could in no way be compared with that which the earth would stir up by its daily revolution (if it were ever moved by such a motion). Consequently, from that swifter motion of the earth by which it would complete its daily revolution, men standing upright on the earth's surface, great cliffs protruding very high from its surface, trees stretching upward, and proud palaces and towers reaching even to the clouds, would give off a far more vehement noise and clangor through the repercussion and resistance of the air. Since all these things can be perceived by no sense, we judge that the earth is in no way moved by such a swift motion. Nor indeed will it suffice to respond that the air itself is also moved with the motion of the earth and gives way to it in its motion, since we perceive winds sometimes blowing violently from the east to the west; these blasts, sent out against the motion of the earth, would likely induce horrendous whirlwinds and tempests on account of the opposing motion of the earth. Because none of these things happen, they evidently demonstrate that the earth is not moved by itself in a daily revolution. *Third*, we shall prove by these experiments drawn from nature itself that the natural motion of the earth itself is by no means to be compared in speed to the motion of the heavens.
