# ON THE CREATURES OF THE ELEMENTARY HEAVEN.

### CHAPTER VI.
->*On the origin of the sentient animated body.*<-

An animal is also fashioned from a simple nature—namely an ethereal one—and an elementary composition, in the same way as a vegetable body. However, a greater portion of celestial heat was required for the creation of sentient beings <note>original: "sensibilium," those possessing senses</note> than for vegetables, since the action of the senses is derived from a stronger power of heat, while their defect proceeds from an excessive coldness, numbness, and stupor. And this is why, in the days of creation, animals were not created before the rising of the Sun; for the heat dispersed everywhere through the regions of the world, and especially through the firmament, was weak before the fourth day. Consequently, it did not have the power to communicate sensation to creatures. The acts of sensation arise from an abundance of heat, which is why the earlier heat only granted vegetative life and imperceptible motion; through this, all vegetables were created on the third day—that is, before the production of the Sun. Nevertheless, within that same species, they perpetually receive multiplicative and generative power from the Sun, possessing no more heat than **GOD** assigned to them in the first creation. Hence, according to Artesius <note>This likely refers to Artephius, a legendary figure in alchemy and natural philosophy</note>, such creatures have three parts coldness and only one fourth part heat; for water predominates in them.

But indeed, all animals were created on the second and third days after the placement of the Sun <note>referring to the fifth and sixth days of the Genesis creation narrative</note>. Of these, the more imperfect were made immediately on the following day; for on the first day after the Sun’s appearance, the earthly coldness strongly resisted the warm actions of the Sun. Therefore, the colder irrational animals—namely the aquatic ones—were fashioned in the earlier part of that day, and the warmer ones—namely the terrestrial and winged creatures lacking reason—in the later part of the same day. These possess sensation over and above all the gifts of simple vegetables because, due to the application of the Sun’s rays during their creation, they are far warmer than vegetables. For this reason, according to all the Philosophers, animals are said to be more "airy" than vegetables, and accordingly much warmer.

However, if such irrational creatures are rightly compared to intellectual ones, we will find that the fire in them is greatly reduced and suppressed; but in the latter, we perceive Man to be exalted by a far more sublime degree. For this reason, the *Microcosm* <term>Microcosm: literally "little world," the philosophical concept that the human being mirrors the structure and forces of the entire universe</term>, or that intellectual animal, was created on the final day. He did not merely acquire the benefit of the solar heat now predominating more strongly over the earth, but he also claimed for his essence a portion of spiritual light and an enlightened intellect, as the divine will commanded. And this is why, while other animals naturally look toward the ground and are prone to it due to their lack of this super-celestial light, man reaches upward toward heaven and the stars. His head—like a little flower in which the most noble seeds of reason are enclosed—chooses for itself the highest seat of the entire body. For that divine light always lifts man up and attracts him to the contemplation of higher things. Hence the Poet <note>Ovid, in his Metamorphoses</note> says:

> ->*He gave to man a sublime face, and commanded him to gaze at heaven,*<-
> ->*and to lift his upright countenance toward the stars.*<-
> <note>original: "Os homini fublime dedit, cœlumq; tueri, Jussit, & erectos ad sydera tollere vultus."</note>

Because of this, he was made the most noble of all sentient creatures and Lord over the others. Therefore, once the animal was created, it is manifest that a twofold equality of nature concurs for its continuation and the preservation of the species: one is a manifest and extrinsic equality, the other a hidden and intrinsic equality. That extrinsic one consists in the equal composition of the body itself from four moisture-substances or humors: namely, *blood, choler, phlegm, and melancholy.* <note>These are the four humors of classical medicine: blood, yellow bile, phlegm, and black bile</note> This equality is the cause of the soul's union with the body. However, the *intrinsic* equality is the nature of the vital soul itself, made from an equal portion of celestial heat and innate or spiritual moisture. Therefore, whenever a strong and quantitatively large departure from that manifest equality occurs, the bond of the soul to the body is corrupted, and then it happens that the soul is separated from the body; this separation is called *death*. But if that departure from equality is only weak, and in quantity...

<summary>This page explains the hierarchical creation of animals and humans, attributing the capacity for sensation and intellect to the increasing presence of solar and spiritual heat. It defines the human "Microcosm" as a being naturally inclined toward the heavens and maintained by a balance of the four humors.</summary>

# TREATISE I. BOOK VI.

...if this part is small, perhaps sickness and bodily disturbance happen from it. So that the proportion of any of the aforementioned qualities may be maintained in weight or at least in justice, *two types of nourishment are required for the preservation of the whole compound*: namely, *spiritual or ethereal, and corporal or elemental*. 

That spiritual food enters the body either by means of a thin and invisible body—namely, air—or by means of a thick and visible body, such as common food, specifically animal and vegetable. This invisible nourishment contains within itself a certain portion of solar light and ethereal spirit, by which the <term>vital spirits</term> <note>In early modern medicine, "vital spirits" were refined substances in the blood believed to carry life and heat through the arteries.</note> hidden in the animal's arterial veins are wonderfully refreshed at every moment. Therefore, this kind of food—namely air—is drawn during breathing into the inner chamber of the heart, and its more essential parts are received for the refreshing of the vital spirits; the remaining parts, being as it were excrementitious and superfluous—namely the more watery substance of the air—are exhaled. 

In its visible and corporal food as well, which is usually either animal or vegetable, a double subject is found, suited to feeding both types of balance. One of these is of a celestial nature, which is called the essential or ethereal part of the nourishing thing—namely, the <term>radical moisture</term> <note>The "humidum radicale" or radical moisture was thought to be the fundamental fluid that sustained life, acting as the body's "fuel."</note> along with its innate heat. The other is of the nature of the elements—namely its more corporal part—from which a portion of the element of fire is drawn and secreted for the proper nutrition of the <term>choleric humor</term> <note>One of the four humors, associated with fire, heat, and bile.</note> in the thing being nourished. Similarly, the substance of the element of air is drawn to nourish the animal’s blood; and that of the element of water, by the addition of which the substance of natural <term>phlegm</term> <note>The humor associated with the element of water.</note> is preserved; finally, the grosser parts of the food strengthen the <term>melancholy</term> <note>The humor associated with the element of earth.</note>. 

And since the composition of the entire animal body is made from these elements, it is necessary that its more solid parts be extracted from the more subtle parts of the body's nourishment for its preservation in the state and vigor of a natural and excellently disposed body, and that the grosser and more dreg-like portion of it, being useless, be expelled either through the bowels or through the urine. It is for this reason that from a lack of this kind of elemental food, the animal body withers; indeed, it is rendered weak and, with its strength utterly cast down, falls into decay, just as happens to those who are urged by the sharpest hunger. 

From these things, therefore, it becomes manifest that the old axiom of the Philosophers is most true: *Namely, that like joys in like, and nature is not improved except in its own nature.* <note>original: "Similia nempe fimilibus gaudere & naturâ non emendari, nifi in naturâ sua propriâ." This principle suggests that things can only be healed or grown by substances that share their fundamental nature.</note> We conclude, finally, that if the matter of a creature is purer and more subtle than that of vegetables, and the essential light more powerful, a work far more beautiful and noble is produced than that vegetable thing—a work called an "animal," in which air and fire operate wonderfully. Regarding the virtues of specific animals, both hidden and manifest, we shall treat this in Volume 2, Treatise 4.

---

### CHAPTER VII.

->*Whence comes the difference between species of creatures, and why are some hotter, colder, wetter, or drier than others?*<-

<image-desc>Decorative initial V featuring an angel or putto at the top, surrounded by intricate floral scrollwork and foliage.</image-desc>

It is certain that the universal difference of all things flows, without a doubt, from their souls or essential forms—which we called above "substantial sparks of light" enclosed in the spirit—since matter by itself is only in potential, and is not brought into action unless it is informed by light. Now, some Philosophers call these forms *influences*, which, descending through the element of earth, penetrate even to its center in such a varied manner and for such diverse reasons that they produce diverse generations in the bowels of the earth through diverse impressions. Such a luminous soul, I say, does everything; without it, no action is found in the body. It draws fire from the earth of the creature, and from the water that light and airy spirit which we call air, so that if there is a large quantity of earth in the creature and a small amount of water, with a strong portion of essential heat making the mixtures, then the element of fire will demonstrate itself in that composition and have 
->dominion<-

...dominion. Conversely, if a sufficiently strong active heat is found over a small amount of earth and a larger quantity of water, the composition will be raw and watery; but if in the same composition an even stronger and more powerful heat is found, the compound will be drier, and from such a mixture a fatty body will arise. Therefore, when the <term>soul</term> <note>In this context, the "soul" (anima) refers to the vitalizing formal principle that gives life and structure to matter.</note> descends upon such matter, clothed in a spiritual garment most familiar to the ether (without whose dryness that soul and the composite body would never meet, be familiar, or be joined together), it moves that matter with its most tempered heat, and alters it in various and diverse ways, producing various habits and species of things, differing according to the greater or lesser proportion of that same light and the "obedience" <note>The author uses "obedience" to describe how easily matter can be shaped or "informed" by the spiritual light. "Rebellious" matter is thick, hard, or resistant to life.</note> of the material subject into which it enters. For a multitude of forms brings greater perfection, activity, subtlety, and purity to the body if the matter is well-prepared to receive it; and in this way, vegetables and animals are born. But if the body is rebellious, coarse, and disobedient to the quantity of its form, in that case the vigor of the soul is hindered in its actions, and it produces a mineral species. 

Where, however, in the threefold division of the aforementioned things, any individual species has its own distinction—as in the animal region **man** from the **lion**, and the **lion** from the **bear**, and again that from the **shrew**; in the vegetable species the **oak** from the **hazel**, and the **hazel** from the **bilberry**, and **vervain** from **betony**, and **sage** from **thyme** and the rest; and in the mineral, **gold** from **lead**, **lead** from **antimony**, and similarly with others—this happens because of the infinite proportions and degrees by which matter differs from its form. For in some, the form is exalted in such a degree in this creature above that one, because of the obedience of the material nature of the former and the disobedience of the latter. 

Now the disobedience of a species can happen in two ways: namely, where that essential light included within is very small, so that although the matter is sufficiently rare, thin, and manageable, its form is not powerful enough to govern it and promote it toward the goal of perfection. Thus <term>phlegmatic</term> and <term>melancholic</term> <note>These terms refer to the "humors" or bodily fluids (phlegm and black bile) believed to cause slow, heavy, or cold temperaments.</note> creatures move more slowly than those of a <term>sanguine</term> or <term>choleric</term> <note>Warm and active temperaments associated with blood and yellow bile.</note> nature; thus the **houseleek** and **hemlock** come to maturity more slowly than warmer vegetables. Or it happens where the matter is very disobedient, thick, hard, not at all porous, and binding its soul—though it be of no small quantity—as if with chains, and entirely hindering and prohibiting the actions of its power and vigor. We conclude, therefore, that the softer and more obedient the substance of the body is, the more the invisible light included within it makes its composition proportionally more noble. As the proportion of any material thing was to its form in the first creation, so also its generation from the origin of the world until its end continues in the same proportion of mixture and under the same species, by the divine will of the Creator; for sparks of light are continually rained down from above.

***

### CHAPTER VIII.

->*What is artificial or common fire? How does it participate in celestial fire?*<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'Q' with intricate floral and foliate scrollwork.</image-desc>That this lower fire of ours, which we call common and artificial, derived its origin from that celestial light, is sufficiently proved both by the reasons of the ancient Philosophers and by their own examples, whose opinions we also, while vigorously pursuing our intention, do not hesitate to easily confirm. For that immense, uncreated light and head of the world created the primary light, from whose super-celestial source rays are sent down into that solar body and the other stars; for with these bodies, the splendor of the <term>Empyrean heaven</term> <note>The highest part of heaven, thought in ancient cosmology to be the realm of pure fire and light.</note> undoubtedly communicates constantly, from which luminous rays are poured into this artificial fire of ours. From this it is manifest that this fire not only has a participation with super-celestial light, but also a certain likeness and presence of the uncreated light itself...

<sig>Z 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: since (cùm)</meta>

It also argues that common earthly fire is a direct descendant of the divine, uncreated light of the Empyrean heaven.</summary>

# TREATISE I. BOOK VI.

<margin>Bartholomaeus, Book 11.</margin>
...since the **evil demons** <note>original: *Cacodæmones*; literally "bad spirits" or "cacodemons."</note> themselves are weakened and put to flight by the approach of fire, just as good **spirits** <note>original: *Dæmones*; in the Neoplatonic sense, these are intermediary beings, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense.</note> are refreshed and made stronger by the sight of it. For this reason, the ancients placed lit candles and lamps beside the corpses of the dead, and did not allow them to be removed until the sacred purifications were finished and the bodies were placed in their tombs; this was done specifically to drive away the **light-shunners** <note>original: *Lucifugos*; spirits that flee from the light.</note> and the evil spirits that serve **dark magicians** <note>original: *Cacomagis*.</note>, or to block their access, as is found in Apuleius' *The Golden Ass*. Similarly, the **Vestal Priests**, according to the Roman rites, kept burning fires beside their altars. Finally, for this same reason, the true worshippers of GOD, as found in the sacred scriptures, are commanded according to the old Law of GOD to offer sacrifices with fire, and to ensure that the fire burns continually upon the altar. Indeed, even to this day, among the followers of the Roman Christian religion, that **anagram of Pythagoras** <note>Likely referring to the mystical significance Pythagoras attributed to the eternal fire or the *Tetractys*, which the author sees reflected in the sanctuary lamps of Christian churches.</note> is diligently observed, and a light is perceived to burn without ceasing in the temples. 

But what this burning light is—which they call common and artificial fire—or how it acts destructively by its own movement upon the subject it occupies, we shall briefly declare here. **Pythagoras** posited only a single fire penetrating all things, which **Pliny** called a "great portion of natural things"; we have called this the *essential light*. But this fire is found to be stronger or weaker in its action within any given subject, according to the greater or lesser resistance to its actions. Thus, in the **Empyrean heaven** <note>The highest, most spiritual part of the universe, often envisioned as being made of pure light and fire.</note>, because of the thinness of the place's substance and its meager resistance, it is immensely dilated, and it warms most mildly and sweetly; there it is exalted in the highest degree of spirituality and perfection, because it is more free there. In the **middle heaven**, because of the thicker consistency of the ethereal parts, it is more intense and hotter, because the composition of that body more strongly resists the actions of the supercelestial fire sent downward; yet it does not heat so much that it tends toward the ruin and destruction of a creature. Rather, because of the moderation of its temperament, it is continually occupied with the preservation and multiplication of the lower creatures themselves. 

However, as this fire sinks further down and hastens toward the center, its operative power is resisted all the more; its rays are narrowed and compressed because of the corpulence of the substance of the lowest heaven, which grows thicker step by step toward the center. Once compressed, the rays act more violently, since a united force is stronger. Thus we also see that in the narrower channel of a river or torrent, the waters move more swiftly and ravenously. 
<margin>Bartholomaeus, Book 11.</margin>
And this is why, among **Theologians**, that **hellish and cursed fire** is said to be destructive beyond all imagination, burning with immense pain and horror, and torturing with a never-ending agony. This is due to the excessive narrowing of the descending rays and the compression and condensation of the substance of the descending light; because of the confinement of the cursed matter containing it, it is said to be wondrously dark, horrific, and gloomy. 

The **intermediate fire**, however, participates both in that heavenly fire and in that of hell, according to the disposition of the thicker parts of the lower heaven which it occupies. From this it happens that the fire of this lower heaven both preserves and causes things to sprout, but in different ways. From these things it is certain that this single fire, extending itself through all things—though it is hidden and unknown in itself, as **Dionysius** <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a Christian Neoplatonist who wrote extensively on the "hidden" nature of God and divine light.</note> maintains—differs in its operation and reveals itself more or less according to the disposition of the intervening matter it occupies. It is by the resistance of this matter that the nature of fire is manifested as either conservative or destructive. Indeed, it breaks apart heterogeneous things and those that differ from the nature of its own **radical moisture** <note>The innate fluid believed by alchemists and physicians to sustain life and hold the vital heat.</note>, so that it may find its true fuel and, as it were, its nourishment; not unlike animals seeking the central substance of a nut, who strip away its husks and skins so that they may choose the marrow for their food, by which they are nourished. 

Therefore, just as that heavenly light—clothed in a "windy" body called air and spirit—is not only moved by its own heat and completes its operations, but also requires the pure and thin fatness of the ether for its fuel (for nothing can subsist without nourishment), so too the innumerable rays of light or heavenly fire, as if for the sake of hunting, descend into the lowest region of the world...

<summary>This page explores the dual nature of fire as both a spiritual protector and a physical force, explaining how its character changes from the gentle, life-sustaining light of the Empyrean to the violent, destructive heat of Hell based on the density and resistance of the matter it inhabits.</summary>
<keywords>fire, demons, Pythagoras, Empyrean heaven, hellfire, radical moisture, Pliny, Dionysius the Areopagite, light-shunners, metaphysics of light</keywords>

Having previously discussed the role of demons and sacred fires, the author now explains the physics of fire and decay, viewing them as the action of celestial light rays seeking nourishment within material bodies.</meta>

# ON THE CREATURES OF THE ELEMENTAL HEAVEN.

...they descend into the lower region, and being constrained, they long for thicker foods; for this reason, they invisibly seek out those subjects which are softer and easier to penetrate, and in which a greater portion of that **ethereal fatness** <note>original: *pinguedinis illius æthereæ*; a term used in alchemy to describe the oily, flammable essence of the vital spirit hidden in matter.</note> which they seek is likely to be hidden. They especially seek those things whose **forms** <note>The "form" refers to the organizing soul or life-force of an object.</note> have flown away and left their dwellings. It is from this, therefore, that dead animals and vegetables suddenly rot due to the invisible presence of these rays; from this comes the dissolution of a corpse into its primary elements; and from this comes the stench and foul, unpleasant odor of the air surrounding a corpse, which is an evident sign of its dissolution into **fire and air**. 

However, for this kind of action, it must be understood that fewer rays of light are gathered than for that of **artificial fire** <note>Visible, man-made fire as opposed to the subtle "celestial fire" of the stars or the "vital heat" within living things.</note>. In artificial fire, the rays are infinite; indeed, in its nature and operation, the rays and sparks are no less continuous with one another than the drops of water are when a body is submerged in water. Thus, it produces an effect that is prompter and swifter in its execution. For when that dense fire, imprisoned in thicker bodies, is drawn out by the collision of two hard objects <note>such as striking flint and steel</note>, it is visible because of its coarseness. It easily clings to a coarse body which, because of its softness and subtle fatness, is fit to be inflamed, to receive the **famished fire** <note>original: *ignemque famelicum*; Fludd depicts fire as a hungry entity seeking fuel.</note>, and to serve as its fodder. 

As soon as this famished fire begins to taste its food, it suddenly summons to itself the rays that are invisibly surrounding it. These fly toward it like wild birds or flies, whose numbers gradually increase around dead carcasses; for the presence of one summons another, then a third, and so on infinitely, until the entire carcass is covered with flies. Alternatively, it may be because a **magnetic virtue** <note>An attractive, "pulling" power.</note> exists in the substance of the coarser fire, by which rays are joined to rays and fire to fire. In any case, it is certain that the substance of such sparks is not made from nothing, but from the matter of **celestial fire** <note>original: *ignis cœleſtis*</note> lurking in the air because of its thinness. Because of the abundance of food upon which that dense fire acts, the celestial fire gathers from every surrounding part toward the "patient" <note>The object being acted upon by the fire.</note> subject. By the addition of many rays, it is condensed—just like the fire that was acting previously—and is rendered visible to us. It multiplies infinitely due to the constant union of arriving rays, provided that the quantity of food and fuel is sufficient to satisfy their appetite. 

But if the passive matter fails, the parts of that fire are gradually dissipated through the spirit of the vast air and vanish. Once the subject is destroyed and its **radical moisture** <note>original: *humiditas radicalis*; the essential fluid that alchemists believed maintained the life and heat of any creature or plant.</note> is consumed or invisibly snatched away by them, they tend toward their own natural place. 

And certainly, a great mystery seems to lie hidden here, and a massive doubt arises: whether these gathered rays that constitute common fire devour these lower bodies so greedily because of the great enmity between the opacity of matter and its coldness, or so that the famished rays might be refreshed by that ethereal nourishment contained within the "patient," or at least, so that they may redeem and free their imprisoned brothers along with their ethereal vehicles. Since this cannot be done without the destruction of the elemental body, they seem to perform that work primarily through the dissipation of the parts themselves. 

And indeed, as it seems to me, that invisible and thinned fire can be content with its natural food when it is dispersed through the widely open **ethereal sea** <note>The vast, subtle atmosphere of the heavens.</note>, the substance of which is very simple and acts as the radical moisture of the whole world. To this, the substance of the air comes closest, by which the fiery nature is also fed, though it is thinner and not as "fat" as that of the ether, and is polluted by the raw exhalations and vapors of the earth and water. But truly, when an infinite number of dispersed sparks are contracted together into one flame, then thicker food is required. This is very easily demonstrated in the life of an animal; for because of the moderate disposition of its own celestial fire, the inhaled air is not sufficient for its food, but more solid nourishment is also required for its preservation, without which it cannot long endure.

->End of the Sixth Book on the creatures of the elemental heaven.<-

# BOOK SEVEN.

->On Imperfectly Mixed Bodies.<- <note>original: *De Corporibus imperfectè mixtis*; in the natural philosophy of this period, "imperfectly mixed bodies" refers to phenomena like clouds, rain, or comets. Unlike "perfect" mixtures (like stones or animals), these were considered transient and lacked a stable, permanent soul or form.</note>

## Contents of this book.

<image-desc>A logical classification diagram using nested curly brackets to outline the contents of the book. The diagram organizes the "causes of meteors" into natural and supernatural categories, with natural causes further subdivided by the region of the atmosphere (Superior, Middle, and Lower) and specific phenomena (comets, clouds, lightning, thunder, wind, and springs).</image-desc>

In this seventh book, we treat the causes of **meteors** <note>original: *meteora*; in the 17th century, this term encompassed all atmospheric phenomena, including weather and comets, not just "falling stars."</note>:

- **Natural causes** of certain meteors, namely in chapter 6.
    - Of the **Upper region**, specifically: concerning the **Comet**, in chapter 1.
    - Of the **Middle region**, such as:
        - The **Cloud**, in chapter 2.
        - **Lightning & thunder**, in chapter 3.
        - The **Wind**, in chapter 4.
    - Of the **Lowest region**, namely:
        - **Springs** <note>original: *Fontium*; surprisingly to modern readers, springs and groundwater were often studied alongside weather because they were thought to be formed by vapors condensing within the Earth's "lower atmosphere" or caverns.</note>, in chapter 8.
- **Supernatural causes** of meteors in general, in chapter 7.

## CHAPTER I.

->That imperfect mixtures or Meteors are not according to the<-
->nature of the Macrocosm, but are preternatural, and are like<-
->passions and diseases, afflicting<-
->its lower parts.<-

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut drop cap 'V' containing figures of humans or cherubs amidst dense vines and foliage.</image-desc>

As we have shown above that perfectly mixed bodies exist naturally in the Macrocosm <note>The "Macrocosm" refers to the "Great World" or the universe as a whole, which was believed to mirror the "Microcosm" of the human body.</note>; so we shall now declare that its Meteors, which are called "imperfectly mixed" <note>In the physics of this era, "imperfectly mixed" bodies were those that were temporary or unstable, like rain, clouds, or comets, as opposed to "perfectly mixed" minerals or living things.</note>, happen to it not according to, but beyond the world’s nature; for they are, as it were, accidental obstructions and affections of that elemental mass, which we otherwise call diseases in animals. For what truly has the world or the nature of the world to do with *Comets*, flashes, lightning, thunder, "leaping goats" <note>original: *capreis saltantibus*; a term used in historical meteorology for flickering, darting lights in the sky, possibly meteors or aurorae.</note>, "burning spears" <note>original: *lanceis ardentibus*; another historical term for streaks of light in the sky.</note>, halos, mock suns <note>original: *parahelio*</note>, or the will-o'-the-wisp <note>original: *igne fatuo*</note>? What has it to do with clouds, dry or moist, hail, snow, mists, dew, frost, or the eruption and flooding of springs? What, likewise, has it to do with *winds* and *blasts*, moist or dry, hot or cold? What with the *earthquake* or its yawning chasms? Are all these things accustomed to happen by necessity to the Macrocosm, or can they not rather, in the manner of an accident, be present or absent without the destruction of the lower world and its parts? 

It is indeed certain and beyond doubt that the elemental region of the world and its creatures suffer and are afflicted by the presence of these things. For how many calamities *Comets* have brought upon the air and the earth is proven by infinite experiences; indeed, histories testify more than enough that by their appearance the temperament of the air has shifted into a pestilential constitution, and great changes have happened on the earth, so that even entire cities and regions have been destroyed as if by famine. Likewise, by *lightning*, temples and palaces have been burned, and crops, herbs, and trees have been scorched and withered. By *thunder*, cities, towers, and tall trees have been leveled to the ground; and the clear face of the air is veiled as if by darkness by the presence of *clouds*, looking upon these lower parts with a mournful countenance, as it were. An excessive abundance of *rain* and *snow* has brought on floods and inundations, which were followed not only by the ruin of houses and cities, but also by the slaughter of all kinds of animals and even the corruption of the earth itself. 

Furthermore, by *hail*, trees and fruits are ruined, and many other evils are brought in. *Winds* are accompanied by the subversion of towns, temples, and houses, and the stirring up of the sea and its swelling waves to the destruction and ruin of both ships and the men traveling in them. By a *whirlwind*, tall trees are cast down to the ground. By an *earthquake*, the terrestrial mass is shaken, and as if wounded by a *chasm*, cities are swallowed up either in part or in whole, trees are torn out by the roots, and innumerable other evils are introduced. Not in vain, therefore, nor inappropriately, do we say that the meteors of the greater world are beyond nature, and bring the greatest harm to its creatures, and for that reason are not undeservedly held to be its diseases; just as we shall clearly demonstrate in the second volume that all the meteors of the *Microcosm* <note>The human body.</note> are its diseased affections.

<summary>This chapter argues that atmospheric and geological phenomena (meteors) are not natural parts of the universe's design but are "diseases" or "afflictions" that cause destruction and suffering. The author draws a direct parallel between weather events in the Macrocosm (the world) and illnesses in the Microcosm (the human body).</summary>
<keywords>Macrocosm, Microcosm, meteors, comets, lightning, thunder, earthquakes, natural philosophy, disease, atmospheric phenomena</keywords>

# CHAPTER II.
## *On imperfectly mixed bodies in general.*

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'C' featuring floral patterns and a small figure or face.</image-desc>

IMPERFECTLY mixed bodies are those of the first composition, which are called **Meteors** <note>In historical science, "meteors" referred to all atmospheric phenomena, including rain, wind, and rainbows, not just shooting stars.</note>; those which the ancient Philosophers said were fashioned from vapor or exhalation; although we shall prove that some of them originate from elsewhere, as we shall say below in the description of the *cloud*. Moreover, Philosophers have distinguished this type of composition into fiery, watery, and intermediate; of each of these there are various subdivisions, distinguished from one another both by the *type of appearance* and the *place of generation*. For in the *highest region of the air* they place the "perpendicular fire," the "burning spear" or "beam," the "lit candle," "flying sparks," "leaping goats" <note>original: *capras saltantes*; a term for flickering or darting atmospheric lights</note>, the Comet, and so on. In the *middle region*, the lightning bolt, the flash, and thunder; In the *lowest region*, that "foolish fire" <note>original: *ignem fatuum*; the will-o'-the-wisp</note>, and so on. The *Meteors* which they call *intermediate* include the wind, the earthquake, and the whirlwind; the *Watery* ones truly include the cloud, the small cloud, the mist, rain, dew, hoarfrost, snow, and hail; to which are also connected springs, hot springs, rivers, and the sea with its saltiness and tide, because of their affinity. We do not intend to treat all of these in this place, because almost all Philosophers have dealt with them, but we shall bring to light only those whose generations and natures the ancient *Philosophers* explained not quite correctly (as it seems to me and as truth itself would require); such as the Comet, the cloud, wind, thunder, and so on. We shall also briefly discuss rivers and springs in passing.

---

# CHAPTER III.
## *On the composition of Comets, and of other Meteors located in the upper region of the air.*

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'M' showing a man in scholarly or clerical robes reading at a desk or pulpit.</image-desc>

THE opinion of the *Philosophers* regarding the generation and appearance of a Comet is manifold; while some say that it is raised little by little from the bowels of the earth into the highest region of the air by the power of the stars, made of a terrestrial, thick, fatty, and viscous vapor or exhalation and from densely compacted parts, and is there spread out, ignited, and set on fire; and they maintain that it lasts until its entire material is consumed by burning. *Others* describe it as being an exhalation—not thick, but subtle; not ignited, but shining from the light of the Sun and the stars incorporated within it; and they say that meteors of this kind have their origin from the action of the stars in the bowels of the earth, especially while the pores are constricted by cold. But in these opinions of the Philosophers, as it seems to me, many impossibilities occur; for since the nature of an exhalation is hot and dry (*for it is defined as hot and dry smoke* <note>original: *definitur enim fumus calidus & siccus*</note>), it is impossible for it to pass through the sieve and cold, moist diaphragm <note>The "diaphragm" of the air refers to a theorized boundary layer between different atmospheric regions that was thought to block certain substances.</note> of the middle region of the air; since there is an immense contrariety between the nature of the one and the other. Hence it is necessary that the immense quantity of cold substance in the middle region of the air should deny passage to a small portion of exhalation tending upward, and should violently and vehemently suppress it downward. From such exhalations, therefore, winds or rather lightnings or thunders and flashes arise,

<meta>catchword: arise, [from "oriuntur"]</meta>

<summary>This page defines "imperfectly mixed bodies" (meteors) and classifies them by their location in the atmosphere. The author begins to critique the traditional Aristotelian theory that comets are made of dry, earthy smoke rising through the cold middle air.</summary>
<keywords>Meteors, atmospheric layers, Comets, exhalations, vapors, four elements, Aristotelian physics, weather phenomena</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "imperfectly mixed bodies" (meteors) from the previous page. While the previous page categorized these phenomena based on their location (highest, middle, or lowest regions of the air), this page focuses on the physical mechanics of how comets and other fiery "meteors" remain suspended in the upper atmosphere.</meta>

# ON IMPERFECTLY MIXED BODIES

...[it is seen], as we shall explain below, than Comets and the rest of the meteors born in the highest region of the air. Therefore, another philosophical reason must be sought and given for the origin of the Comet and of other meteors of the same species and location. We say, therefore, that a certain denser spark of a **Martian star** <note>original: *stellæ Martialis*; this likely refers to a "martial" or fiery nature associated with the planet Mars, thought to influence heat and fire on Earth</note>, tending downward and enclosed in the thicker matter of the third region of the air as if in its own vehicle, is forced to hang in the middle of the highest region of the air. This occurs because of the lightness of its body and the fiery heat of the star, partly due to the attractive power of fire and partly due to the resistance of the middle region of the air, which completely prohibits its descent and passage through those regions. There, it is held aloft always in the same state and at the same distance from the earth, sustained by the power of the soul or stellar spark, by the attractive power of the element of fire supporting it proportionally, and by the reflection produced through **Antiperistasis** <note>original: *Antiperistafin*; an ancient scientific theory where a quality, such as heat, is intensified when surrounded by its opposite, such as cold. Here, the author suggests the cold middle-air compresses and stabilizes the hot comet.</note> caused by the coldness of the middle region of the air. Moreover, the two following experiments demonstrate what power air thickened by cold has for lifting solid things upward, and the power of fire’s attraction for raising and retaining them.

->Experiment I.<-

<image-desc>An engraving of a scientific experiment. A rectangular container labeled A, B, C, and D has a vertical pipe rising from its top center. At the top of the pipe is a flared nozzle labeled E, from which jets of water spray upward. These jets support a small sphere labeled F, which is shown floating or "dancing" in the air above the spray.</image-desc>

Water forcefully emitted upward from the small vessel **A. B. C. D.** through the pipe **E.** will lift that light wooden sphere **F.** into the air, so that it seems to **dance** <note>original: *tripudiare*; to dance, skip, or leap in a rhythmic fashion</note>; and in this way, as long as the water exits through the pipe, so long will that little sphere dance and leap.

<sig>Aa</sig>
<meta>catchword: Expe-</meta>

<summary>This page explores the physical causes behind the suspension of comets in the upper atmosphere, attributing their position to the balance of fiery attraction and the resistance of cold air (antiperistasis). An experiment with a water jet supporting a wooden ball is used as an analogy for how fluid pressure can keep a solid object aloft.</summary>
<keywords>comet, meteor, Martian star, antiperistasis, atmospheric regions, physics experiment, fluid dynamics, stellar sparks</keywords>

# TREATISE I. BOOK VII.

## Experiment II.

<image-desc>An engraving of a scientific or alchemical experiment involving solar heat. In the upper left corner, the Sun is depicted with a human face, emitting numerous straight rays that fill the sky. One specific ray is aligned with an eggshell, labeled "A," which is fixed to a tall, vertical spear or staff. The staff is planted in a grassy foreground. The background depicts a detailed landscape with clouds, a body of water featuring a small sailboat, and a shoreline with a house from which smoke rises into the air.</image-desc>

An eggshell <note>original: *oviconcha*</note> **A.** filled with **May dew** <note>original: *rore Majali*; dew collected in the early morning during the month of May was highly prized by early scientists and alchemists, who believed it was impregnated with the "spirit of the world" or solar vitality</note>, and afterwards very securely sealed with wax, if it is placed next to a spear raised perpendicularly before the shining Sun, the Sun will gradually lift the shell upward over the top of the spear by its rays; just as common experience has proven. This occurs by the **attractive power** <term>attractive power: *attractiva virtus*; a pre-Newtonian concept of "pulling" force, often attributed to heat or magnetism, used here to explain how the Sun draws lighter substances toward it</term> of the solar rays, which lifts that pure and subtle matter enclosed within the eggshell.

**Fiery...** <meta>original catchword: *Ignea*</meta>

# OF IMPERFECTLY MIXED BODIES

The fiery power, therefore, of the Planets—and most frequently that of **Mars** <note>original: *Martialis*; the author attributes the "fiery" quality of comets to the astrological and physical influence of the planet Mars</note>—enclosed within that thin and somewhat viscous vehicle, attracts the closer parts of the surrounding denser air of that same region by a certain magnetic virtue. It thickens them and reduces them into a body in the shape of a star. Against this body, the rays of the Sun and other stars are reflected, as if against a mirror, illuminating it and making it visible. They either create the appearance of a star or infuse their own "ideas" <note>original: *ideas*; in this context, the term refers to the formal images or "likenesses" of celestial bodies being projected onto the comet's mass</note> into it, by which it represents the likeness of a true star to us. Therefore, we cannot sufficiently condemn those who wish for the body of this **Comet**, as well as others of the same nature, to be set on fire and consumed in the manner of a flame. If this were true, it would follow that that thin mass ignited in the lowest region of the air, which they call **will-o'-the-wisp** <note>original: *ignis fatuus*; literally "foolish fire," a phosphorescent light seen over marshy ground, often attributed to "thicker" vapors than those of comets</note>, would more sensibly burn the things it touches, since it is made of thicker material than a **Comet**; yet experience itself has denied this. 

We say, then, that smoke of this kind has an occult nature for gathering together rays that are invisibly dispersed, as has been said above regarding decaying wood, fish scales, the firefly <note>original: *cicendula*; likely referring to a glow-worm or firefly</note>, and other such things that shine at night. We also assert that this body is indeed ignited and burned, but in a way that is imperceptible to us. The cause of the dissolution and vanishing of the Comet’s body is the constant reflection of rays generating heat, increasing it gradually every day by the continuous bouncing back of those rays. When this power of heat has been increased and strengthened after many days, the body is opened, the enclosed spark is freed, and the entire composition of the **Meteor** <term>Meteor: *Meteorum*; in the 17th century, this term referred to any atmospheric phenomenon, including clouds, rain, and comets, not just falling rocks</term> is gradually destroyed by the imperceptible power of light, dissolving into subtle air and vanishing. This usually happens especially when that Martian planet—which generated the Comet and nourished its spark—is rendered weak, either by the approach or aspect of some Planet of contrary quality that diminishes its vigor, or when it falls into its "detriment" or "fall" <note>astrological terms referring to specific positions in the zodiac where a planet's influence is significantly weakened</note>, or when the hidden matter is gradually diminished by the fiery and destructive power of Mars.

## CHAPTER IV.

### *On the generation of clouds.*

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'P' showing a floral or foliate design.</image-desc>

THE **PERIPATETICS** <term>Peripatetics: *Peripatetici*; followers of the philosophy of Aristotle, who often taught while walking about the Lyceum in Athens</term> define a **cloud** as *vapor raised from the earth into the middle region of the air, and there constricted and as if frozen by the power of cold, which is then driven and tossed here and there by the various motions of the winds.* But I shall explain—first by experiment and then by sufficiently manifest philosophical reasons—that the matter behaves quite differently in the generation of this **Meteor**. 

While traveling from Venice toward Augsburg in Germany through the Alpine mountains, I perceived a very thin mist appearing on the summit of a certain crag of immense height, which the solar rays were striking vigorously. By thickening more and more, it was finally reduced into a very dense cloud, clinging only to the front peak of the mountain; however, I saw the other parts of the crag free from the cloud and shining with solar rays. I noticed, therefore, that the cause of this kind of Meteor came from a wind blowing strongly against that side of the crag or mountain peak. By its blast, the wind drove the thinner air before it and compressed it against the crag (which, due to its hardness, held back the fleeing air) in such a way that it always placed the following parts of air against the preceding ones. Because of the mountain’s resistance, it gradually condensed the air into the consistency of a cloud; immediately, the solar rays striking the peak of the crag finally dissolved it into drops that brought on rain. But someone will perhaps object that clouds arise where mountains...

<sig>Aa 2</sig>
<meta>Catchword: ...mountains (original 'tes' from 'montes')</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the nature of comets as reflective bodies influenced by the planet Mars and provides a personal observation of cloud formation in the Alps, challenging the traditional Aristotelian view of vapors.</summary>
<keywords>comets, Mars, will-o'-the-wisp, atmospheric reflection, Peripatetics, cloud formation, Alpine observation, meteorology</keywords>

# TRACTATE I. BOOK VII.

[moun]tains or rocks are least found <note>The text begins with the syllable "tes," completing a word from the previous page, likely *montes* (mountains).</note>: To which it must be answered, that the condensation of air is produced by a similar compression, although by different means or instruments; for two opposing winds blowing at the same time—even if one is stronger than the other due to its predominance in the air—are very often accustomed to compress and condense the thinner air and turn it into rain, according to the nature of the wind or the exhalation. If both of these winds act and resist more strongly, they produce a sound in the air similar to that of two rivers violently falling with contrary motion into the same bay or abyss.

I remember also when I was on a ship plowing the sea with sails spread, making my way toward **England** with a stronger wind blowing; as it blew more weakly by turns, I observed a wind opposite to it and adverse to our journey pushing the sails backward, until that wind favorable to us, by overcoming the vigor of the other opposing one, finally carried us happily into **England**: meanwhile, however, various clouds were gathered and resolved into rain. 

Finally, if we bring forth more evident philosophical reasons, we will perceive that those arguments of the **Peripatetics** <note>original: *Peripateticorum*; followers of Aristotle's philosophy, who taught that clouds were formed purely by solar heat drawing up vapors.</note> demonstrating the generation of clouds are to be considered worthless; for if the origin of a cloud were from the attraction of vapors into the upper region of the air, it would follow that clouds would be more frequent in the summer time than in the winter. This would be true both because in summer the Sun, with its more direct rays, attracts vapors upward more easily, and also because in the warmer time, the terrestrial pores—vomiting out such vapors—allow the fumes to exit without any hindrance of cold blocking the way. 

Indeed, we see that the air in summer time is very thick because of the constant vapors rising from the earth and water; in fact, ponds, springs, and rivers are either entirely or in large part converted into the substance of air through **refinement** <note>original: *subtiliatione*; the process of a substance becoming thinner or more "subtle," like liquid turning to gas.</note>; just as in winter, through **thickening** <note>original: *inspissatione*</note>, when the grosser part of the air is condensed by the power of the cold, the air is converted first into clouds, and then into rain. Whence ponds, springs, and rivers are refilled in the winter time by the mutation of air into water, and they rise up as a torrent: and from this, the entire surface of the earth, dried in summer, is rendered moist and slippery in winter. 

From these things it is evident that it is not vapor immediately elevated into the middle region of the air, but the condensed air itself that is the cause and origin of the substance of a cloud. It is also evident that clouds are produced more in winter than in summer, both because of the contrary blowing of opposing winds, and because the blasts of the **Northern winds** <note>original: *ventorum borealium*; Boreas was the personification of the cold North Wind.</note> are more frequent, whose nature it is to freeze—even if they do not always bear that name, perhaps because of more evident blasts of an opposing wind—and finally, because the air is prepared and fit for impressions of this kind. The nature of the middle region of the air, due to the absence or more remote distance of the Sun, is well-disposed to freeze the parts of the thick air and sustain them in the manner of ice, until they are dissolved into drops by the power of the stellar rays. 

If, therefore, vapors are elevated during the day, experience constantly teaches us that they produce mists after sunset, and not clouds. Finally, various effects are produced in the generation of clouds by the blowing of the winds; for where the **South Wind** <note>original: *Auster*; the warm, moist wind from the south.</note> predominates, while the **North Wind** <note>original: *Borea*</note> nevertheless blows secretly, clouds are resolved into rain because of the freezing disposition of the one (with coldness and dryness working on the substance of the cloud) and the dissolving quality of the other (which by its warm nature destroys the freezing works of its opposite). 

But where the North Wind holds dominion, the cloud cannot be entirely dissolved by the South Wind blowing imperceptibly, but is turned into snow or hail. But if the South Wind is still stronger, it generates partly snow and partly rain. The **West Wind**, however, for the most part produces warmer and drier clouds, which are accustomed to sustain the rays of the sun and other stars without the dissolution of their parts; whence they appear bright and serene. But where a single and simple wind blows, or at least finds a transverse wind opposing it very weakly, there for the most part the air is found without clouds. If this does not suffice for the wise man, what is to be thought of a cloud carrying lightning in its vitals, which, appearing on the farthest edge of the Horizon, is perceived to move against the motion of the wind? Without a doubt, meteors of this kind...

<summary>This page critiques the Aristotelian theory of cloud formation, arguing instead that clouds are created by the compression and condensation of air caused by opposing winds and cold, rather than just solar attraction of vapors. The author supports this with personal maritime observations and a comparison of seasonal weather patterns.</summary>

# ON IMPERFECTLY MIXED BODIES

<note>original: "ron" — likely the conclusion of the word "non" (not) from the previous page</note> ...not at the point of each horizon; it arises from the breath of two winds blowing from opposite directions, one of which was stronger in one horizon, while the other held dominance in the other. That wind, blowing more strongly and upward from the hidden horizon <note>The "hidden horizon" refers to the part of the sky or earth below the observer's line of sight.</note> and in the opposite direction, is perceived to move the cloud that generates thunder against the breath of the weaker wind in the visible horizon. We conclude, therefore, that a cloud is formed by the compression of the thinner parts of the air, caused by the breath of winds moving toward opposite regions of the world. Furthermore, contrary to the opinions of the **Peripatetics** <note>Followers of Aristotle who believed the "middle region" of the air was perpetually cold and still.</note>, we say that wind is found in the middle region of the air as well as in the lowest, since the bodies of clouds are moved by their breath, just as the **Peripatetics** themselves have written.

## CHAPTER V.
### Concerning the winds.

The ancients also wrote variously concerning the origin of winds, from which their uncertain knowledge of the cause of this **meteor** <note>The term "meteor" here refers to any atmospheric phenomenon (wind, rain, snow), not specifically a falling star.</note> is very easily recognized. For the **Peripatetics** said the matter of wind was a hot and dry exhalation moving laterally around the earth; **Bede** <note>Bede the Venerable (c. 673–735 AD), an Anglo-Saxon monk and scholar who wrote on nature.</note> called it vaporous air, agitated by smoky matter emerging from the earth; **Constantius** defines wind as a cold and dry resolved vapor, separated from earth or water, which moves the air vigorously by the power of heat. *Others* posited that the origin of winds comes from the violent motion of clouds pushing the air before them; *Others* maintained that the wind had its origin from the reverberation of the "arms" or branches of the sea coming from any region of the earth; they say that the southern arm turning toward the north brings about the **Subsolan** <note>original: *Subsolanum*; the classical name for the East wind, literally "under the sun."</note> or Eastern wind, and so on for the rest. *Several others* write that winds come from caves and caverns. 

We, however, are taught by a certain practice that the origin of winds is primarily derived from two things: namely, either from enclosed air breaking out simply and violently from the belly of the earth, or from water itself, dissipated into hot vapor by the force of the **central fire** <note>The "central fire" (ignis centralis) was a common theory in early modern science, suggesting a source of heat at the center of the Earth that drove geological and atmospheric processes.</note>. Air enclosed in the earth is expelled either by its **subtilization** <note>Subtilization: the process of making a substance thinner, lighter, or more refined through heat.</note> caused by the central heat—as is demonstrated in the following experiment—and then, because of the scarcity of matter, it excites only a light wind, of the kind we see blowing in the summer.

*Alternatively, wind may be caused by a great quantity of water rushing into the bowels of the earth, pushing out the entire substance of the air from the earth's belly, as is demonstrated in the following experiment; from this, not only winds but also earthquakes <note>original: "terraemotus"</note> most frequently occur.*

# TREATISE I. BOOK VII.

For water in the center <note>of the Earth</note>, thinned <note>original: "subtiliata"; referring to the process of expansion where heat turns liquid into vapor, increasing its volume</note> by the power of heat, seeks a larger place to contain its body, since it cannot remain in its previous seat due to the extension of its mass. Therefore, both because of the expulsive nature of fire and its own thinned substance, it very easily bursts forth into the region of the air. By its presence and motion, it agitates the air here and there, and by means of the sulfurous spirits retained within its vapor, it generates thunder, lightning, flashes, and other such meteors <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomenon, including rain, wind, and lightning, not just falling stars.</note>. From this type of hot vapor, that wind which they call a <term>Typhon</term> <note>original: "Typhonem"; a violent, swirling wind often associated with fire or heat</note> is derived because of its fiery nature. We can prove that these causes of the winds are certain and infallible not only by the mechanical examples described above, but also by natural experience.

For in Dalmatia <note>modern-day Croatia</note>, as **Pliny** and **Pomponius Mela** <note>Classical Roman geographers</note> relate, there is a lake in a very deep cave with a vast, steep opening. They write that if even the lightest stone is thrown into it, even on a calm day, winds are suddenly stirred up and storms like whirlwinds arise. Here, without a doubt, an immense quantity of air is trapped at the base of the cavity, whose exit was blocked by the mass of water plugging the mouth of the cave. When the stone is thrown down, it penetrates the surface, and a large portion of the air escapes, pushed out as portions of the water enter. 

Furthermore, in the province of **Cyrenaica** <note>eastern Libya</note>, a certain rock is said to be sacred to the **South Wind**; and in the **Aeolian** region <note>the Lipari Islands near Sicily</note>, winds are observed to blow constantly because of the multitude of infinite caverns and caves contained within it. For this reason, the Poets invented their first king, named **Aeolus**, as the GOD of the winds; for through such caves, the earth more easily emits the smoky material of the winds. 

Some inhabitants of the **Promontory of Puteoli** <note>modern Pozzuoli, near Naples</note>, who are still living, have said they remember a horrific earthquake happening on their promontory in this final age. They testified that winds and sulfur were agitated with stupendous thunder for the space of 48 hours; that the earth was moved from one place to another, and a mountain rose up in one spot which they called **Cineritius** <note>original: "Cineritium"; literally "The Cinder-Heap," referring to Monte Nuovo, which formed during a volcanic eruption in 1538</note>. They also testified that in the nearby area, no small portion of land—indeed, the greater part of the city of **Cumae**—was submerged by sea waters (an admirable spectacle of this time). I myself have seen all these things. 

I propose the reason for this event is an immense quantity of sulfur burning under the earth. By its destructive faculty, the greater portion of that sulfurous earth was turned to ashes and gave way to the weight of the earth hanging above it. This earth, plunged into the bowels of the globe down to the burnt ashes, forced the trapped hot air to burst out. By the violence of this air, the aforementioned earthquake, flaming winds, lightning, thunder, and other mournful accidents were undoubtedly brought about. Therefore, such sulfurous vapors are most often the authors of fire-vomiting winds. Hence, in those regions where the bowels of the earth are either visibly inflamed or where the air is usually polluted by such vapors through invisible exhaling, flashes and thunder happen very frequently.

We conclude, therefore, that winds are usually excited either:
1. By the pushing out of air trapped in the bowels of the earth;
2. Or by its thinning <note>rarefaction</note> (and then it creates a weak wind called the **Zephyr**);
3. Or when its entire substance bursts from the womb of the earth, due to both the violent entry of waters occupying its seat and the plunging of an immense portion of earth down toward the center. By this fall and presence, the air held there is suddenly thrust out and induces a vehement agitation of both the earth and the air.
4. Or by the thinning of waters in the womb of the earth arising from sulfurous heat. 

This last type is perceived to blow more often from the **Southern** part because of the **Sun's** constant familiarity with it. Its fiery faculties are removed in the winter time because of the vehement resistance of the northern blast; thus, thunder and lightning are not produced as frequently in winter as in summer. Winds coming from thinned air shaken out of the belly of the earth are also more frequent in summer. However, those coming from air thrust out of the earth in its integral nature <note>air displaced as a gas rather than expanded from liquid</note> are more common in the spring time...

# ON IMPERFECTLY MIXED BODIES.
<note>original: "De Corporib. Imperfecte Mixtis"; in Aristotelian physics, "imperfectly mixed bodies" refer to atmospheric phenomena like rain, wind, and lightning, which are temporary transitions between elements.</note>

work more effectively, and especially around the month of March. For as the solar rays approach, the waters are penetrated, and the trapped air is permitted to escape little by little; this air, due to the infinite compression of the waters, is usually forced to yield its place to them. But when the Sun shines perpendicularly upon them and thins them during the summer, they are converted into the substance of air. In this way, it is manifest that a yearly rotation of things occurs. Furthermore, the causes of the lateral blowing of the winds from one region of the world to another are the planets, through both their exhalations and their motions that agitate vapors, as well as their mutual aspects <note>The angular relationships between planets in astrology, believed to influence earthly events.</note>. These drive that sublunary spirit here and there, according to the disposition of their own natures. For some planets are Eastern, others Western, others are in Northern latitudes, others in Southern; some are in hot signs, others in cold, some in moist, and several in dry signs, stirring up winds of the same kind. Some, when joined with others, bring about mixed winds; others are more ready, according to their position, to agitate a sulfurous nature, others an aquatic nature, and others finally a terrestrial one. And in this manner, according to the disposition of the planets, various winds are brought forth at various times of the year, as we shall teach below.

## CHAPTER VI.
### On lightning and thunder.

The Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy.</note> write that thunder and lightning occur when a hot and dry exhalation is raised, along with a moist and watery vapor, by the heat of the Sun into the upper part of the middle region of the air. There, they claim that the exhalation is surrounded and enclosed on all sides by the vapor, and condensed into a cold and thick cloud. Resenting the injuries of that intense cold, the enclosed exhalation is driven here and there as if in indignation, seeking an exit. Finally, through agitation and <term>antiperistasis</term> <note>The intensification of a quality when surrounded by its opposite, such as heat becoming more intense when trapped by cold.</note>, it ignites like tinder and bursts through the sides of the cloud by its own fiery force. As the cloud is broken and disrupted, it gives out a massive crash, which they call *thunder* <note>original: "tonitru"</note>, while simultaneously calling the light of the ignited exhalation *lightning* <note>original: "fulmen"</note>, *a flash* <note>original: "fulgur"</note>, and *a spark* <note>original: "coruscationem"</note>. We cannot entirely disprove their opinions in all things; for we say that the "patient" matter <note>The substance that is acted upon.</note> which produces the crackling is the air reduced into the substance of a cloud. However, the "agent" form that induces the crash is that sulfurous and windy matter ignited by the force of both cold and moisture. This matter, illuminated according to the nature of the enclosed fire, seeks the open air, and to satisfy its appetite, it bursts its prisons and flies out into a more spacious and thinner place where it can live more freely, and there it vanishes. Thus we also see that the substance of **Saltpeter** <note>Potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in gunpowder.</note> is nothing other than air congealed by cold; if a certain portion of sulfur is added to it, even if a very small amount, it gives out a massive noise and emits artificial lightning, as is demonstrated by the following experiment.

# TREATISE I. BOOK VII.

*Over salt of nitre <note>Saltpeter</note> melted in a pot A. B. C. by the fire of coals, and sprinkled with sulfur, I cast a live coal E.; and suddenly I heard a great noise, and perceived a massive conflict between the nature of the salt of nitre and that of the fiery coal with sulfur. From this, artificial thunders arose, with the appearance of lightning. Having observed these more closely, I judged the nature of the arrangement of thunders.*

Indeed, this very thing is sufficiently demonstrated in the operation of gunpowder <note>original: "pulveris bombardici"</note>. From these examples, it is manifest that thick air, containing within itself a nitrous disposition, is the material cause of lightning and thunder, and a sulfurous exhalation is their formal cause <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, the "material cause" is the substance something is made of, while the "formal cause" is the essence or pattern that gives it its specific shape or behavior.</note>. How this condensation of air occurs, however, we have clearly enough demonstrated in our discussion on the generation of clouds, where we were concerned with opposing the opinion of the Peripatetics <note>The followers of Aristotle, who dominated medieval and early modern science.</note>. For when the South wind blows—whose vaporous matter we have said is for the most part drier and more sulfurous due to the proximity of the Sun to that part of the world, especially in the summer time—sulfurous vapors are frequently and more intensely (though invisibly) lifted upward by the perpendicular attractive power of the Sun. These vapors, moved toward the Northern part, are whirled and gathered together into a cloud formed by the opposition of the aforementioned winds (the North wind also blowing at that time). All its parts, which were once scattered, are reduced into one dense mass. Thus, force is united with force, and sulfurous atoms with sulfurous atoms; finally, they are ignited through the resistance of the freezing nitrous nature. This happens both by the repercussion of the sulfurous mass being agitated, and by the active power of the fiery mass itself, which is strengthened through <term>antiperistasis</term> <note>The intensification of a quality (like heat) when surrounded by its opposite (like cold); a common explanation for why lightning forms in cold clouds.</note>. The parts of the sulfur, which previously existed only in potential, are now brought into action and refined by the illumination. Consequently, because of the thinness of their parts, they vigorously seek a larger and more spacious place, penetrating downward even to the outer parts of the cloud. They violently burst through its sides, and are perceived to emit a massive crash, which we also call thunder. Moreover, that a certain sulfurous mixture can be ignited and illuminated through <term>antiperistasis</term>—that is, because of the resisting coldness—is confirmed by the following experiment.

### Experiment II of this chapter.

<image-desc>A landscape engraving depicting a scientific experiment. In a body of water labeled with 'B' on both sides, a large oval object (an egg) labeled 'A' is partially submerged in the center. A dramatic plume of fire and smoke is shown erupting from a small opening at the top of the egg.</image-desc>

*Take an egg and extract its insides through a very small hole made in it. Let its interior surface be dried, and let there be placed inside a sufficient quantity each of salt of nitre, sulfur, and quicklime <note>original: "calcis vivæ"; calcium oxide, which reacts violently with water.</note>, and let the hole be plugged again with wax. Let this egg A., prepared artificially, be cast into a stream B., and suddenly a flame will emerge from the water itself.*

### *Experiment II of this chapter.*

<image-desc>An engraving of a man in 17th-century attire standing in a landscape. He holds a small, round object labeled 'A' up to his face; the object is emitting a plume of fire. In the background are rolling hills and a small castle or fortification.</image-desc>

### *Experiment IV of this chapter.*

<image-desc>An engraving of a rounded metal vessel resting on a bed of flaming logs. The vessel has a decorative embossed band around its center and is sealed with a stopper at the top. A narrow, slanted pipe or neck extends from the shoulder of the vessel.</image-desc>

*Let the opening of this copper vessel be thoroughly blocked with wood so that nothing escapes, and let its stopper be A. From the side of the vessel, however, let a narrow pipe of the same copper material ascend, and let it be B. C. Therefore, let a third part of the vessel be filled with water before it is blocked, and let it boil over a strong fire: In this way, thinned together with the air, it will cause constant blasts to exit through the pipe; and if the pipe is closed, it will force out its cover with a crash not unlike thunder or a cannon <note>original: "bombarda"</note>.*

<header>TREATISE I. BOOK VII.</header>

Finally, we do not say that the blast and nature of the South wind <note>original: "venti Australis"</note> alone produce sulfurous and drier vapors of this kind, but also the other winds blowing from any quarter of the world, provided that they partake of sulfurous spirits. These things will be perceived more clearly if we diligently observe the motion of a thundering cloud that vomits forth lightning; for a cloud emitting lightning is noticed to be agitated against the motion of clouds that lack sulfur. A crash is usually produced at the moment when both clouds have come together into one mass and have firmly and powerfully enclosed the sulfurous vapor accompanying the opposing wind. Here it must also be observed that in the opposing clash of clouds, the cloud burdened with sulfurous spirit moves even the contrary cloud against its own motion because of the multitude of **living fire** <note>original: "ignis vivi," referring to the active, burning essence within the vapor</note>, in the manner of a most powerful soul moving its body. By its vigor, the power of the other contrary cloud is repelled, not unlike when a stronger man carries a weaker one somewhere against his will and despite his struggles. 

Hence arises the origin of the contention in which—since the disposition of the sulfurous cloud is fiery and sulfurous, while the constitution of the cloud moving against it is extremely cold—the sulfurous nature is ignited according to the reasoning shown in the previous demonstration regarding artificial fire in water. We conclude, therefore, that common experience has sufficiently and evidently demonstrated that the formal origin of thunder and lightning consists of certain sulfurous vapors invisibly dispersed in the air, which, when gathered together, are brought into action—that is, they are ignited. For while lightning and flashes are scattered through the air, strongly sulfurous odors are perceived; indeed, the true nature of sulfur is recognized by common eyes both in the color of its flame and in the condition of its execution. Furthermore, we have seen in foreign regions that thunder is more frequent near those places where sulfur mines or sulfurous mountains are found ignited by some accident, such as in Sicily and around Naples; similarly in rocky regions where the solar rays are struck forcefully against the peaks of the mountains. Finally, what could more clearly demonstrate the origin of thunder than that horrendous production of Mount Cinereus <note>The "Ash Mountain," or Monte Nuovo, which formed near Pozzuoli (Puteoli) during a volcanic eruption in 1538</note> at Puteoli, of which we spoke in the preceding chapter.

## CHAPTER VII.

->How the higher bodies act toward the production<-
->of such meteors?<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'I' featuring intertwining foliage and scrollwork.</image-desc>IN the preceding sections, we made mention only of the more immediate <term>proximate causes</term> <note>The physical, earthly materials directly responsible for a phenomenon</note> of meteors. Here, however, we shall briefly explain by what means the stars and planets produce these **imperfectly mixed bodies**. Since it is determined among all philosophers that the higher things <note>the celestial spheres</note> act constantly upon these lower things to produce new objects, it is certain that such celestial bodies are the primary causes of the production of any meteor and the positioning of its substance in its proper place. But we will briefly review here what the astrologers feel and strongly assert on this matter; then we shall conclude with our own opinion. Some astrologers teach that comets for the most part imitate the nature of Mars and Venus, whence they wish them to portend wars, heat, and tumults. Others say they have their origin from some unlucky position of the stars, such as the conjunction of the higher planets and an eclipse—especially when Saturn, Mars, and Mercury look upon the Sun or Moon with unlucky <term>aspects</term> <note>The geometric angles between planets that astrologers believe influence earthly events</note> during an eclipse, and most especially if some unlucky...
